# A21 Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The Balsas Basin is one of the two regions in Mexico with the highest level of climate vulnerability. This is exacerbated by negative feedback loops between land use and climate change dynamics which are ending vulnerable rural livelihoods and productive systems, which have yet to adapt to climate variability and change. The basin has experienced a significant reduction in rainfall during the wet season and an increase in dry seasons as a result from Climate Change and El Niño Southern Oscillation (ENSO) continue to aggravate social vulnerability and environmental degradation of the Balsas Basin. This is leading to increased soil erosion, reduced water availability, and adversely affecting already dwindling ecosystem services. With the length of the dry season anticipated to increase, the basin will experience more frequent and severe droughts in the future. The combination of increasing temperatures with flooding, crop damage and soil erosion will be compounded in the rainy season while extended drought may be experienced during the dry season. This will lead to a reduction in agricultural productivity and food security. If the rainy season will only increase in the productivity of the vegetation.* Finally, increasingly dry soils will take longer to recover from flooding events. The basin is currently experiencing severe water scarcity with limited access to clean water restored and their capabilities to buffer these extremes improved, the positive changes in the wet season do not carry over into the dry season. This project aims to address this situation by investing in a climate-resilient development model that will increase the adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities and ecosystems currently local capacities are insufficient and institutional modalities do not consider climate change adaptation criteria in priority setting.

The cross-cutting project (80% allocation, 20% mitigation) aims to increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and reduce emissions from production systems in the Balsas Project Area. This will be achieved by investing in a community-based approach that will provide rural development incentives through a community-centric bottom-up approach; i.) investing in the climate-sensitive agricultural sector (e.g., agroforestry) to increase adaptive capacity of vulnerable communities, and generate social benefits; ii.) supporting a climate information and risk management system, and the systematic management of knowledge and information.

The objective of the project is to enable community-level investment in adaptation and mitigation initiatives through the climate-focused transformation of rural development incentives for the conservation of forest resources and the strengthening of local governance. The project will be implemented over 5 years (2017-2022) with a total budget of $324,578 ha of land, contributing to Target 5 on the Updated Strategic Plan of the GCF for 2024-2027 This objective will also contribute towards the achievement of SDG targets 1.5, 2.4, and 6.3. The project will be implemented in a way that promotes social inclusion, benefiting and empowering women, youth, and indigenous peoples. The proposed project will shift current development incentives systems used by rural development institutions to a new integrated territorial development approach which:

* Uses climate change-related criteria for selecting and prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries.
* Interventions are designed with local communities.
* Strengthens local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the sub-basin level.
* Empowers communities to manage their natural resources in a sustainable way through the conservation, sustainable use, management, and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted in the Balsas Basin. The project will also contribute towards the strengthening of local governance by supporting Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in value chains of timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and agroforestry systems.
* Empowers communities to manage their natural resources in a sustainable way through the conservation, sustainable use, management, and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted in the Balsas Basin. The project will also contribute towards the strengthening of local governance by supporting Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in value chains of timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and agroforestry systems.
* Empowers communities to manage their natural resources in a sustainable way through the conservation, sustainable use, management, and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted in the Balsas Basin. The project will also contribute towards the strengthening of local governance by supporting Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in value chains of timber and non-timber forest products (NTFPs) and agroforestry systems.

The National Foresty Commission of Mexico (Comisión Nacional Forestal CONAFOR), Nacional Financiera S.N.C. (Nafin), and Hacienda y Crédito Agrícola Nacional (HACIENDA) will be the implementing partners for this project. CONAFOR will undertake all operational responsibility for the implementation of the Project and NAFIN and HACIENDA will only contribute to the financing of the project. The National Foresty Commission of Mexico (Comisión Nacional Forestal CONAFOR), Nacional Financiera S.N.C. (Nafin), and Hacienda y Crédito Agrícola Nacional (HACIENDA) will be the implementing partners for this Project, it will strengthen its capacities and experience to prepare and execute GCF co-financed climate change adaptation projects in Mexico. The project is aligned with the priorities of the National Forest Commission of Mexico region and its multi-year international experience with several similar projects with the GCF, wants to capitalize on its previous experiences in the design of rural development incentives and provide innovative solutions to the project and strengthen the capacities of the partner organizations involved.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1 Climate context (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### General information on the project area
1. The Balsas Basin (Hydrological-Administrative Region RHA IV) and part of the South Pacific Hydrological Administrative Region RHA V are located in the state of Guerrero, Mexico.
2. The basin is shared by six municipalities: Acapulco de Juárez, Chilpancingo, Huixtla, La Unión, Tlaltepec, and Zihuatanejo.
3. The Balsas Basin covers an area of 105,472 km² and has a population of approximately 6 million people.

#### Climate
- The basin is located in the tropical zone with a mean annual temperature of 22 °C and a regional average annual rainfall of 1,027 mm.
- The rainy season occurs from June to September, while the Inter-monsoon depressions suffer from dry to hot climate.
- The Balsas Basin has a hydraulic infrastructure of great relevance for Mexico with the highest level of climate vulnerability.

#### Socio-economic context
- The population of the PA is vulnerable to the impacts of climate change due to its high levels of poverty and inequality, which are reflected in low access to basic services such as health, food, education, social security, housing, drinking water and basic sanitation.
- The most vulnerable households face extreme poverty with limited access to resources for their livelihoods. These practices threaten the sustainability of natural resources and the most vulnerable households, putting high pressure on the environment.

#### Poverty
- Extreme poverty is concentrated in rural areas, where it mainly locates in the southern and southeastern regions of Mexico.
- The Balsas Basin has a large population living in extreme poverty conditions (representing 21% of the total population), with Guerrero having the highest percentage of people living in poverty: 43.05%. There are also 9 out of 10 municipalities that have the highest poverty rate or one of the highest poverty rates.

#### Social and economic context
- Mexico has been suffering from violence and insecurity, mainly due to the fight against organized crime.
- The Balsas Basin is experiencing severe situations of social fragility and violence, with high levels of unemployment and underemployment.
- In response to these challenges, the Government has transformed a more important role for the armed forces for the maintenance of public security.

#### Objectives
- Strengthening the capacity of local communities in the Balsas Basin to address climate change through improved access to information on climate change and adaptation strategies.
- Improving the quality of life of vulnerable populations, particularly women's participation and gender transformation.
- Strengthening of the social fabric and the dynamization of sustainable development models that are important contributions of this project to government efforts to confront conflict violence and migration.

Figure 1: Percentage of population living in poverty (dark blue > 51.1%): Guerrero 64.4%, Oaxaca 61.7%.

4. This usual inequality faced by women to access different services and assets have been aggravated by the effects of climate change. On the one hand, the activities traditionally assigned for backyard orchard subsistence production (e.g., fruit trees, vegetables, and other crops) are affected by the increase in temperatures, water scarcity or water excess, in addition to the absence of public drinking water service. On the other hand, women’s traditional roles as collectors of firewood, water, and other resources (e.g., etc.), which are also often conducted by women, are affected by the loss of vegetation due to droughts, fires, or floods. These effects have a direct impact on food security and nutrition for families in the region. In addition,
these effects cause houses to lose fruits production, farm animals and domestic goods, situations that must be faced. The lack of access to water services is also an important factor that affects women’s health as it was mentioned before usually contributes to illnesses and accidents, which also led to additional burden for the women in the region. In addition, women are more vulnerable than men because they have a greater responsibility
prone to gender-based violence in emergency and disaster contexts.

5. and 904,700 ha between years 2002 and 2018, respectively.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 7

## Overview of climate trends affecting the Project Area

### Ecological climate analysis of the Project Area

1. The Project Area is located in the Andes mountain ranges and includes two different lowlands.
2. It can be divided in at least four ecoregions (Fig. 4) with different climates:
   - a.) The Inter-montane Depressions (Di), inner lowlands characterized by high temperatures, arid conditions, and low precipitation.
   - b.) The Southern Pacific Coastal Plains and hill landscape (PCP) with an area of 2.5 million hectares.
   - c.) The Temperate Mountain Ranges (TMR) with an area of nearly 300 thousand hectares.
   - d.) The Transverse Neovolcanic System (TNS) temperate mountain ranges of with nearly 3 million hectares.

### Figure 4: Four main ecoregions in the Project Area, and location of selected weather stations used for the analysis

Source: Authors (see Annex 2b)

1. To gain a more differentiated insight on the impact of climate change and climate variability on rural livelihoods and vulnerable ecosystems, daily climatological data from weather stations located in the Project Area were obtained through the National Meteorological Service of Chile.
2. As year 2018 was the last year with sufficient data for the stations that had information since 1970, 2005-2018 was selected as the period to be analyzed and the number of weather stations used in this analysis is shown below.

### Table 1: Number of weather stations from which data could be analyzed for the project rationale

| Terrestrial ecoregion | Number of weather stations |
|-----------------------|------------------------------|
| Intermontane depressions Di | 106                          |
| Southern Pacific coastal plains / hills PCP | 27                           |
| Temperate Mountain Ranges TMR | 39                           |
| Transverse Neovolcanic System TNS | 92                           |

## Observed climatic trends between the reference period (1975-1999) and study period (2000-2018)

| Variable | Trends in Eastern Plains and Llanura Depressions (EPD) | Trends in South Plain and Llanura Plain (SPLP) | Trends in Sierra Madre Oriental (SMO) | Trends in Sierra Madre Occidental (SMO) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Precipitation - annually accumulated | 5.00% | No trend | +17.4% | +9.80% |
| % of days accumulated | +3.2 points | +6.5 points | +5.0 points | +5.0 points |
| During the dry season | No trend | -2 days | -6 days | -6 days |
| At least 10 mm | No trend | No trend | No trend | No trend |
| % of anomaly wet months | -0.3% points | +1.3% points | +7.5% points | +5% points |
| % of anomaly dry day | -13.3% points | -2.7% points | -1.7% points | -3.4% points |
| Temperature - annual mean | +0.1°C | +0.43°C | +0.29°C | +0.46°C |
| Maximum temperature maximum | +0.5°C | +0.82°C | +0.78°C | +0.87°C |
| Minimum temperature minimum | -0.1°C | +0.18°C | +0.30°C | +0.34°C |
| SPEI - 6 of wet months | +8% points | +9% points | +5.2% points | +3.4% points |
| SPEI - 6 of dry months | +20% points | +10% points | +8% points | +8% points |
| SPEI - 6 of extremely wet months | +7% points | +9% points | +5.2% points | +3.4% points |
| SPEI - 6 of dry months | +11.2% points | -3% points | -22.0% points | -12.8% points |

### Observed increase in precipitation - Precipitation dynamics within the PA are predominantly influenced by the Nino-Southern Oscillation (NSO) phenomenon, which is currently in its "La Nina" phase of the NSO, leading to heightened rainfall levels across the area. Consequently, a marked increase was observed in the annual precipitation in the EPD (+5% points), SPLP (+17.4%), and SMO (+9.80%). Over the same timeframe, there was a reduction in the frequency of dry days with decreasing trends noted for the SPLP (-2 days) and SMO (-6 days). Regarding extreme rainfall occurrences, the percentage of abnormally wet months (defined as months with a precipitation anomaly > +15% points) increased by 7.5% points in SPLP (+3.4% points), from +1.3% points in EPD to +7.5% points in SMO. Conversely, the percentage of abnormally dry months (months with a precipitation anomaly < -20%) decreased significantly for all four ecoregions (-8.6% points). The only exception was observed in the ID, where the percentage of abnormally wet months remained stable (+ 0.35% points), while the percentage of abnormally dry months increased by +3.3% points.

### 17. Projected increase in temperatures
A significant increase in temperatures is expected between the study period (1970-1999) and the projection period (2041-2060), across all scenarios. Under the mitigation scenario, maximum temperature increases are expected to range from +3°C to +5°C with a median of 4 ± 1°C for both minimum and maximum temperatures.

### 18. Comparison with regional context
To provide regional context, we compared our results on climate projections (SIPs) with those derived from the IPCC AR5 interactive atlas, for land in Southern Central America, and the projection period of 2041-2060, compared to the reference period of 1995-2014. Regional findings show an increase of +1°C under SIPS-S.5 and +3°C under SIPS-S.4 for maximum temperatures, with a median of 1 ± 1°C under SIPS-S.5 and +1.5°C under SIPS-S.4. For minimum temperatures, regional findings show an increase of +1°C under SIPS-S.5 and +2°C under SIPS-S.4, with a median of 0.8 ± 0.3°C under SIPS-S.5 and -0.6 ± 0.9°C under SIPS-S.4.

### Conclusion
The results confirm the direction of the trends we found in the PA.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 11

Local sensitivity to climate change exposure - Given that any long-term variation in temperature has a significant impact on crop yields, these results are of great relevance to the project area, where most of the rural populations depend directly or indirectly on agriculture and crops, leading to reduced yields. In the case of temperatures above average, the plants have an increased respiration rate, which suppresses flower bud development and accelerates crop maturity, causing a decrease in yield. Conversely, when temperatures are below average, the plants have a lower respiration rate, resulting in more developed flowers and fruits.

Impact of drought - According to the National Centre for the Prevention of Disasters CENAPRED*, during the period 2000-2018, 1,337 emergency and disaster declarations at municipal level due to drought were registered in the PA. The municipality with the highest number of events was Tlaxcala (456), followed by Hidalgo (395) and Puebla (385). Ninety-five per cent of these events were recorded between 2000 and 2009. The years with the highest number of declarations in the PA were 2017, 2016, and 2004. In 2017, a total of 358 emergency and disaster declarations due to drought were recorded, most of which were issued at the expected beginning or during the rainy (planting) season and ended at the end of the dry season (harvest). The second highest number was in 2016 with 349 events. A phase E to NINO was observed, which is associated with wetter events during the rainy season in the PA. In 2004, 158 emergency and disaster declarations due to drought were recorded, most of them issued at the beginning or end of the dry season (planting and harvest). The second highest number was in October (the second planting season). During this period, ENSO dynamics were in a neutral phase. In 2005, 269 drought events were declared, most of which occurred during the rainy season (planting) and ended at the end of the dry season corresponding to the dry season. However, in most cases, their duration extended into September and even October. The second highest number was in August with 130 events.

The effects of both phases can be associated with dry events during the rainy season (planting) and extend into the dry season (harvest). In this case, the ENSO dynamics for that period were a transition from La Nina to El Nino, so the dry events are attributed to this phenomenon.

## Economic impacts

27 In addition to water resources, climate change will exacerbate shortages that are already being experienced by rural ACS in the PA, both at the household level (drinking water) and on farm-level (water for irrigation). This impact is likely to be felt most acutely during droughts, when demand for water increases due to increased exposure to certain ecosystems and many human activities (domestic and agricultural) given climate variability. For example, in 2015, the Basin experienced a severe drought that lasted from April until September. The dry subregional impacts, increasing competition among users and reducing supplies to ACS. In addition, the effects of climate change on water resources will be felt more acutely by rural areas than urban areas because of their reliance on surface water sources. This is particularly true in rural areas due to the reduced availability of water and changes in temperature, causing displacement of agricultural activities, especially from the lowlands and loss access to grazing.

## Combined economic impacts

28 The hydrometeorological events described above have resulted in both the loss of human life and environmental damage. For example, the 1994 flood caused by Hurricane Luis killed 30 people in the region and caused 561 deaths. These events also damaged 147,000 homes, 1.4 million ha of cropland and 1.05 million ha of pastureland. The floods also destroyed 28,000 livestock units (LSU) and 39,000 head of cattle. These events increase the vulnerability of the Basin's populations and ecosystems. In fact, the Basins is one of the two most arid regions in Mexico. The region has an average annual rainfall of only 584 mm, which represents a very low water availability (27.4% (6.9 million ha) high vulnerability and only 13.4% (5.7 million ha) low vulnerability). In addition to the Basin's aridity, the population is highly concentrated in areas that experience average temperatures of 27°C (80.6°F), which are very high for this region.

## Mitigation rationale: GHG emissions profile

29 According to Mexico’s Third National Report to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) and the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC 1995-2010), the total estimated net emissions in all sectors at the national level were 534,688.6 MCGCO2e. The “Agriculture, Forestry and Other Land Uses” sector contributed to this total with an emission of 72% (389,000 MCGCO2e). This sector is composed mainly of cropland, farmland or grasslands which absorb CO2 from the atmosphere to release oxygen and generate biomass to store C. The agricultural land use in Mexico accounted for 54.1% (54.3 MCGCO2e), 26.3% corresponded from forest conversion to agricultural land (20.7 MCGCO2e) and 19.6% was due to other types of land uses (18.9 MCGCO2e). The emissions linked to the expansion of agricultural frontiers in the Basin are occurring in a current context where deforestation is still taking place.

## Situation de definition and FREL in the Basins Basin

30 In line with the definitions used in the Second FREL, for the period 2007-2016 submitted by Mexico in 2009, in the Basin, the area of forest land that has been deforested is underlain through 42,000 km² over the grid of the National Forest Inventory (NFInventaria as its acronym in Spanish). While the gross annual deforestation rate for the period 1985-2007 was 3.6% per year, it increased to 10.2% between 2007 and 2014. Deforestation increased by 15% of the period 2013/2014 to reach 1,856 ha / year. The total forest area lost between 2007-2016 was 9,000 km² (of which 7,000 km² were located in the Basin). More than 70% of these forests were lost to pasturesland and the remainder mainly for other uses. The total forest area gained between 2007-2016 was 354 km². These figures are presented in Annex A. It is to be noted that the Second FREL, for the period 2007-2016 submitted by Mexico in 2009, includes only two activities: reduction of emissions from deforestation, and partial reduction of emissions from forest degradation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 15

The project will support the implementation of Mexico's National Strategy for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation (ENEDIF) and its corresponding lines of action of the ENADEH2020+2030 strategy. This aims to achieve the following milestones by 2030:

- Achieve an 87% deforestation rate by 2030.
- Significantly increase the area of primary forest compared to the baseline.
- Achieve an increase in sustainably managed forest area, natural and induced regeneration and forest cover.
- Maintain or improve the provision of environmental services of the territory by conserving biodiversity.
- The project will contribute to the conservation of genetic resources and species.
- Increase the use of best practices of sustainable management on the rural landscape scale.

The Balsas Project will support the achievement of all the above mentioned six milestones of the ENADEH2020+2030 strategy. It will also contribute to the implementation of the corresponding lines of action of the ENADEH2020+2030 in the Project Area, as shown in the alignment matrix included in Annex 1.

The National Forest Program (PNF) 2020-2024 is a Special Programme derived from the National Development Plan (PND) 2020-2024. The PNF is an integral part of the National Strategy for Sustainable Forest Management and Illegal Trade in rare or endangered species, including the National Strategy for Community Forests and Indigenous Peoples' Rights, which are key elements to achieve the goals of the National Forest Program.

The PNF covers a total of 189 strategies and 168 specific actions, including the National Strategy for Community Forests and Indigenous Peoples' Rights. The PNF is based on the principles of sustainable forest management and participatory planning that contributes to guaranteeing a healthy environment for the development and well-being of the population living in forest areas and of society as a whole.

The PNF has five strategic objectives:

1. Strengthening the institutional framework for the conservation, protection and sustainable use of forests.
2. Promoting the integration of forest management into rural development strategies.
3. Strengthening the capacity of communities to manage their natural resources in an integrated manner.
4. Diversification and diversification of forest resources as an integral part of the development of local networks or competitive value that triggers socio-economic impacts to improve quality of life for people living in forest areas, particularly indigenous peoples and other vulnerable groups.
5. Strengthening the institutional framework for the conservation, protection and sustainable use of forests.

The Balsas Project will implement the mandates of the General Law for the Sustainable Forest Development (LGFSD) and the National Strategy for Sustainable Forest Management and Illegal Trade in rare or endangered species, including the National Strategy for Community Forests and Indigenous Peoples' Rights. The project also strengthens value chains which in turn contribute to climate change mitigation and adaptation.

The Balsas Project will implement the General Law for the Conservation of Natural Forests and Forest Protection (LFCF) and the National Strategy for Sustainable Forest Management and Agroforestry Systems (PNCA). It will also support the implementation of the National Strategy for Community Forests and Indigenous Peoples' Rights. All outcomes, in a cross-cutting way, will target women, youth and indigenous peoples, and propose specific activities for their empowerment.

#### B

**Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change**

This outcome will be achieved through:

- Conserving, restoring and sustaining use of forest landscapes by agrarian communities through the implementation of community-managed forests (CMFs) in accordance with the principles of good governance and governance achieved via Outcome 1. I will provide a platform to support the achievement of Output 2.1.
- Strengthening the climate resilience of agrarian communities' productive systems (Output 2.0), through the implementation of climate-resilient agricultural practices, including agroforestry in degraded forest areas (Activity 2.2.1) and climate adapted commercial forest plantations and agrosyndesis systems (Activity 2.2.2).
- Empowering agrarian communities and producer organizations, especially women and the youth, in forestry and agroforestry value chains and building the climate resilience of their livelihoods through the implementation of community-managed forests (CMFs) that promote sustainable forest management and ensuring inclusive access to timber and non-timber forest product value chains (Activity 2.3.1), establishing a market for carbon credits from CMFs (Activity 2.3.2), and establishing community forest protection brigades to monitor and ensure the sustainable climate-responsive use of forest resources (Activity 2.3.3).

#### Outcome 3: The producers, stakeholders and authorities of the Baia Pașa have timely and relevant climate information for decision-making and disaster prevention.

This outcome will be achieved through:

- Supporting accessible climate hazard-related early warning and monitoring systems in the Baia Pașa (Output 3.1) by developing a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information and early warnings, including those from the National Climate Change Observatory (NCCO), the Forest Fire Prevention and Protection System (SPPF), the IUCN Forest Phytophagist Surveillance (VOCIFOR) system, the Meteorological Agency's Mesoscale Radar (MRR) system (Activity 3.1.3), and the Social and Environmental Safeguards Management System (Activity 3.1.4).
- Developing policy-relevant knowledge products through systematic documentation of good practices and lessons learned from climate change adaptation interventions in the Baia Pașa region, and systematization materials to support ongoing capacity development (Activity 3.2.1), exchange of experiences, and South-South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC) at the local, regional, national and international levels (Activity 3.2.2). I will also develop and implement a rigorous monitoring and evaluation (M&E) system for the project (Activity 3.2.3).

#### Barriers:

The proposed project has been designed to respond to specific barriers to the achievement of its proposed outcomes that are described in detail below, as well as those that have been identified by them and presented below.

[...]

[...]

**Technical Note:** A key factor shaping climate-related challenges is the weak level of existing climate governance for the design and implementation of climate change adaptation and mitigation initiatives, as well as the absence of a coordinated approach to address these challenges. This situation has only recently begun. Furthermore, institutional coordination among the involved actors, specialized technical assistance, and capacity building have been insufficiently developed in the Baia Pașa region.

Specifically, the lack of technical assistance and coordination among actors, has delayed the roll out of climate change adaptation interventions in the Baia Pașa region. This is also reflected by the limited number of development initiatives. Climate change adaptation interventions have therefore only been considered as an afterthought or a secondary priority within existing development projects and programs rather than as action for climate change mitigation and adaptation. ## B

### Barriers and Opportunities for Mitigation of Climate Change Impacts in the Basin

#### Evaluation of CONAFOR's ongoing work (2) in the Balsas Basin which indicates a sectorized vision of the territory generating a contradiction between initiatives, as well as scattered and disorganized efforts; deficient coordination between different actors involved in forest management and climate change adaptation.

Regarding specialized technical assistance, the findings indicate little relationship in the development of support activities for the implementation of projects. The lack of information on the participation of women and youth regarding community participation and the participation of women and youth, the reports indicate a negative and low-perception of the role of women and youth in forest management and climate change adaptation.

A coordinated approach will ensure that climate change adaptation is front and center of development in the Balsas Basin. This will be achieved by addressing the following barriers to the sustainable management and resilience of local communities:

* Facilitate the creation of Forest Management Units (FMUs) and the corresponding Regional Committees for the Development of Forests (CRDFs), as well as the strengthening of forest management unit and bodies in each basin, Sub-basin and sub-territory prioritized by the Project (Activities 1.2.1 to 1.2.3).
* Strengthening multi-sectorial construction structures, operation rules and mechanisms for co-investment in the FMUs and CRDFs.
* Raise the awareness among agrarian and indigenous communities on how to benefit from incentive programs that promote climate resilience (Activity 1.2.1).
* Developing participatory forest management plans (PFP) and integrated (mitigation and adaptation) multiannual investment plans at the sub-basin level with active involvement of women and youth (Activity 1.2.2).
* Developing a participatory forest protection system (PFPS), including fire prevention facilities, and forest protection brigades (Activities 1.3.1 and 1.3.2) of CONAFOR's Promotoras Forestales (Activity 1.3.3); to plan and implement the construction of firebreaks in priority areas for the establishment of firebreaks; and the establishment and operation of forest protection brigades and climate change resilience facilitators in the project area.

#### Barrier 2: The main institutions working in the Balsas PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURAL PRODUCTION INSTITUTE) are not adequately prepared to address the challenges posed by the medium-term evolution of climate patterns and climate risk prevention. However, information climate is hosted on a variety of platforms that are difficult for many communities to access. This includes local communities, municipalities and even extension services. The local population cannot access this information as they are unable to use computers or mobile phones, which are not available in most rural areas. In addition, the cost of computers, which many community members do not have access to due to research and development (R&D) projects (INFARP), but its accessibility by ACs, municipalities and local technicians remain difficult. Consequently, climate risk assessment is not carried out at the sub-basin level in a participatory manner with communities. The lack of local priorities, context and knowledge. Evidence of this barrier is presented in several recent CONAFOR assessments that indicate that there are no clear mechanisms for the collection and dissemination of information on climate change and the sustainable management of forest resources; vi) limited dissemination and use of information by ACs and municipalities; vii) lack of a platform for the exchange of experiences and best practices, and viii) lack of an institutional framework for the development of a common platform for registering PADD/SB support requests on which it is still in its infancy; and viii) little relation between the areas of intervention of ACs and municipalities. The lack of coordination among actors involved in forest management and climate change adaptation, as well as the lack of information on climate change and the sustainable management of forest resources, vii) limited dissemination and use of information by ACs and municipalities; viii) lack of a platform for the exchange of experiences and best practices, and ix) lack of an institutional framework for the development of a common platform for registering PADD/SB support requests on which it is still in its infancy. These barriers will be addressed through the activities described below:

* Communication and awareness-raising campaign to disseminate calls for proposals (Activity 1.2.1).

## Assumptions and Co-Benefits: The assumptions underlying the achievement of the project's outcomes are described briefly hereafter:

Active and consistent support from all levels of institutional and local stakeholders, including communities, will be required to ensure that the desired results will be achieved in a coordinated and sustainable manner. This will be anchored on the willingness of vulnerable agricultural producers and forest-dependent communities to adopt new and alternative approaches to the management of productive systems and forest ecosystems that increase productivity, enhance resilience, improve livelihoods, and reduce the use of unsustainable environmentally degradative practices. The uptake of such approaches will be facilitated by a conducive policy environment and improved access to information for decision-making.

The main co-benefits, which are described further below in more detail in Section D.3 are the following:

### B.3. Project/programmes description (max. 1500 words, approximately 5 pages)

**54. Project Area Focalization:** To specifically target the highest levels of social and environmental vulnerability in the Balsas Basin, the following targeting criteria were applied:

- **Poverty and Social Marginalization:** Areas with high vulnerability to climate change according to seven vulnerability dimensions established by the SEMARNAT® and the National Commission for Indigenous Peoples (CONAIE).
- **Prioritized Municipalities:** A list of 401 municipalities was selected based on the Evaluation of Social Policies CONAVI.

**55. Major Watersheds:** The following watersheds were prioritized: Rio Balsas, Rio Grande de Santiago, Rio San Juan, Rio Tepetongo, Rio Moxorito, Rio La Arena and Rio Mixteco. Additionally, 17 municipalities located in two indigenous territories, the Xico Mixteca (Valles del Río Balsas y del Río Grande de Santiago) and the Sierra Norte Mixteca (Valles del Río Tepetongo y del Río Moxorito), were prioritized. These watersheds have a high vulnerability to climate change, according to the seven dimensions of vulnerability established by SEMARNAT® and CONAIE, as well as the highest level of prioritization (orange color in Figure 11 below). Once plotted and successfully implemented in these municipalities, the project will be extended to other municipalities with similar characteristics (yellow color, below) for a total of 401 municipalities in the Balsas Basin. The detailed analysis and the focalization strategy, as also the detailed list of the municipalities can be found in Annex 2a.

Source: CONAFOR(2022)

The project aims to increase capacity for resilience, adaptation and climate risk management for agropastoral families, women, children and men from vulnerable rural households, assuring social inclusion of women, youth and working indigenous communities. They represent 9-25% of the population of the 10 prioritized municipalities and subject to high vulnerability due to their economic situation. The project will directly benefit 40% women; 58% indigenous peoples (Nahua, Mixteco, Tlapaneco, Amuzgo among others); and 32% youth. This represents a total of approximately 17,690 people or 10-25% of the population. Detailed project beneficiary calculations are presented in Annex 2). The project is structured into three interrelated components: (i) capacity building; (ii) climate risk management and adaptation; and (iii) community development.

Outcome 1: Government programs and community development plans integrate climate change criteria for allocation of support (USD 10,991,728)

### Recalling the national context in which women with agro/communal ownership rights represent about 23%, and that only 4% of the land is under the direct control of those who own it. Women have access to land and natural resources. Through a new special sub-program for self-led women (see rules of operation), CONAFOR seeks to increase their participation in decision-making processes related to climate change adaptation. Figures from CONAFOR in the communities supported by the PROFOOT project (World Bank) showed that the percentage of women participating in these processes was 10%. This is a very low figure, and it is clear that the participation of women is much lower than 40%. This has also been confirmed during consultation activities being performed for this project. The objective is to achieve the ambitious gender target of 40% in the project, which thrives through its intense gender participation approach.

# Output: Mechanisms for intersectoral coordination are strengthened to include climate change criteria in institutional structures and procedures.

## B

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) proposal mandates that CONAFOR delinks Forest Management Units (FMUs), based on physical, environmental, social, and economic aspects, with the purpose of managing forest resources more efficiently. This will allow for better management of water, soil, and other natural resources. As FMUs in the Basin have yet to be created the Project will support the establishment of FMUs in the basin. The project will also facilitate the development of Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCMTs) at the sub-basin level. These committees will include representatives from indigenous communities, indigenous communities, producer organizations, women, and youth, as well as to CONAFOR local employees.

At the level of each state, CONAFOR has created consultation bodies ("Consejos Forestales Estatales" (CFES)), which are responsible for coordinating with the municipalities, agrarian communities, producers of timber and non-timber forest products. Nevertheless, at present there are no similar coordination and governance structures in place to manage water resources, soil conservation, biodiversity, climate change adaptation, landscape, the provision of environmental services, river basin management and hydrometeorological risks and deals with the intersectoral coordination among all relevant institutions and actors. Therefore, a new approach for development within a watershed and climate change adaptation approach is needed. To advance towards this objective, CONAFOR will develop a territorial planning approach at each sub-basin level that includes all relevant institutions and actors contribute and take decisions in a coordinated and synergistic way. Inter-sectoral coordination among the different stakeholders will be strengthened through the development of an integrated management model for water resources, soil conservation, biodiversity, climate change adaptation, landscape restoration and need and as well as "risk management" shall be further developed within each RCMT. The project will support the establishment of a territorial planning approach at the sub-basin level to improve prioritization criteria to be implemented during project implementation. Nevertheless, the basic elements for this approach are already in place: the existence of the RCMTs and the commitment from all relevant institutions and actors contribute and take decisions in a coordinated and synergistic way.

This approach will be one of the key innovations introduced by the Basin Project; a paradigm change innovative to be implemented at the sub-basin level. The territorial planning approach will include indigenous territories FMUs and the corresponding committees (RCMTs) through intensive technical and facilitation assistance, as well as the participation of an expert in landscape restoration, a specialist in forest management specialization, a senior watershed planner and a junior social participation specialist, that will provide technical, communication and facilitation support to the RCMTs.

### 5.1.2 Priority on intervention areas at the sub- and micro-basin level

Within each Regional Committee for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCMT) and with project technical assistance and the cultural and physical-nature areas that facilitate the social management of micro-watersheds, the RCMTs will define criteria to prioritize areas for landscape restoration and risk management within each basin, sub-basin and indigenous territory. The criteria for prioritization should be based on the following elements: water supply and demand zones, among others. Detailed description of prioritization criteria for each of the intervention areas at the sub- and micro-basin level will be provided in Section 5.2.1. These criteria will be defined by the RCMTs as well as in the Operation Rules of the PRS included in Section B.

### 6.1 Sub-basin territorial planning approach

The project will support the development of a sub-basin territorial planning approach (TAP) methodology and taking on board relevant elements and lessons learnt from the "Integral Basin Management Action Plan" (IPAN) methodology, which is currently being developed by CONAFOR. The TAP will be developed through a participatory process led by CONAFOR; a participatory territorial planning process will be facilitated. This participatory prioritization and planning process will include indigenous territories FMUs and the corresponding committees (RCMTs). It will focus on landscape restoration at the basin and sub-basin level, as well as on the conservation and restoration of forests, its natural resources and associated environmental services at the micro-watershed level. The TAP will be developed in close collaboration with the municipalities, agrarian communities, other state and federal government entities.

The development land use zoning plans based on socio-economic and climate change vulnerabilities including higher risk areas for landslides, floods, droughts, among others.

### Main elements of a stepwise participatory territorial management plan are as follows:

- Identification of prioritized micro-basins, in terms of hydrological functionalities and vulnerability
- Assessment of ecosystems, water supply and demand zones (ground and surface water supply and demand), and the most vulnerable rural communities that have no supply from irrigation systems or local drinking water networks.
- Participatory validation of techniques/models for sustainable management and conservation of ecosystem services, with a focus on the most promising value chains in terms of the existing market: the inclusion of smallholders, women and youth (leading to Outcome 1.3) and the Agri-Governmental Alliance (leading to Outcome 2).
- Capacity building activities will include communication and awareness raising activities about the Project, its objectives, and its approach.
- Administrative support for CONAFOR and CONAGUA Forestal via RCICMT for facilitation support acting as technical secretariat
- Dissemination of information on forest management techniques capacities and organization for forest firefighting and prevention, dealing with extreme hydroclimatic events, as well as the adoption of best practices.
- Disseminate information about the incentive programs offered by CONAFOR, CONAGUA, Agri-Governmental Alliance, and other actors.
- Sharing of information about institutional programs and budgets to strengthen coordination between public institutions and civil society organizations for the promotion and oversight of local actors and the Agri-Governmental Alliance (leading to Activities 1.1.3 and 1.3).
- Explore opportunities for involvement and additional support from other actors and the private sector.

### Activity 1.1.3: Strengthen multi-sectorial coordination structures

Rules of Operation and mechanisms for co-investment within the basins, CONAFOR + PROFINFOR Forestales will support and facilitate the planning and execution of joint actions in the basin, sub-basin and ethnic territories. CONAFOR, CONAGUA, local governments, research institutions, civil society organizations, and other actors will be involved in the preparation of basin management plans at each basin, sub-basin and ethnic territories. CONAFOR, CONAGUA, local governments, research institutions, civil society organizations, and other actors will be involved in the preparation of basin management plans at each basin, sub-basin and ethnic territories. The identification of prioritized actions and work plans and promote the participation of grassroots organizations, women, youth and other vulnerable groups in the planning process. Once a plan has been prepared, it is expected that the public sector will be able to identify the most vulnerable rural communities in each basin and sub-basin and submit their investment proposals to CONAFOR and CONAGUA for possible more detailed investment plans to be financed by the public sector. The preparation of proposals by groups with broad representation from women, youth and other vulnerable groups in each basin and sub-basin will be an essential step towards involving these entities will be invited to participate in innovative financing mechanisms for forest landscape conservation, like the Local Climate Fund (LCF).

### Activity 1.4.2: Rules and operational criteria for adaptation to climate change in institutional program procedures

The Rules of Operation (Régimen de Operación) that from the PRIFORFUND regulate the implementation of the project will be reviewed and adapted as necessary, taking into account the improvement. Its prioritization and eligibility criteria are based on socio-economic and ecological aspects. Nevertheless, it is important to note that the inclusion of women and youth in the planning process for women and youth and fail to include clear criteria for climate change adaptation that are oriented to improve community resilience and increase the livelihood resilience of the most vulnerable rural communities. Through this activity and with project technical assistance, CONAFOR and CONAGUA will adaptively improve, simplify, and adjust the operational procedures.

## Outcome 1.2: Participatory climate responsive multilateral investment plans within prioritized basins, sub-basins and regions

### Activity 1.2.1 Communication and awareness raising campaign to disseminate calls for proposals
While CONAFOR is responsible for the implementation of the project, it will be necessary to develop a communication strategy that includes a communication and awareness raising campaign that has the objective to inform Agrarian and Indigenous Communities (ACs) about the importance of climate change adaptation and how they can participate in the process. The campaign will be done through public websites and social media, but also in traditional ways such as radio, television, posters, leaflets, etc., with a special focus on women and youth. Climate change adaptation principles and criteria for landscape restoration practices will be communicated to ACs. In addition, information about the project’s objectives, activities, and benefits will be disseminated. Economic, financial, and social benefits of climate adapted practices will be explained; discussion forums will be organized with ACs in order to generate interest among them. To increase interest of the most vulnerable and marginalized ACs and for members to submit Project Profiles to be included in the project’s portfolio, a simplified form will be provided (see Annex 2). The most vulnerable population) will receive technical support and will have simplified forms for presenting their proposals.

### Activity 1.2.2 Formation of climate smart multilateral investment plans based on micro-basin zoning plans and criteria for landscape restoration practices
The formation of the project’s portfolio will be done in accordance with CONAFOR’s Promotoras Forestales. Those that are aligned with the Regional Forest Management Programs (RFMPs) and/or the National Forest Program (NFP), as well as those that meet the climate change adaptation and livelihood resilience criteria will be prioritized. Special attention and support will be given to ACs in vulnerable areas, such as indigenous communities, women, youth, and people with disabilities. In accordance with the Rules of Operation of the PADFSSB, will transfer financial resources to the beneficiaries for the implementation of the project’s activities. The Project Profiles will be developed by CONAFOR, while technical services by themselves; double-dipping of technical support services is not possible. Project Profiles will have a maximum length of 10 pages and will include: (a) an executive summary, (b) description of the proposed activity, (c) detailed information on the project’s portfolio, (d) detailed description of the project’s portfolio items in schemes also in Outlines 2 and Annex 2), including local zoning plans as well as criteria for landscape restoration practices. The Project Profiles will be submitted with a cover letter that includes the name and contact details of the AC representative and other stakeholders.

## Outcome 1.3: Strengthened capacities of local actors for climate change adaptation, resilience, and livelihoods

### Activity 1.3.1 Capacity development programs for extension workers, local facilitators, and forest protection units
The project will provide capacity development programs to extension workers, local facilitators, and forest protection units in order to increase their knowledge about the impacts of climate change on forests and the impacts of climate change and variability. Through this activity specific curricula will be developed in which one or more of the following topics will be covered: (a) climate change adaptation practices for protecting and restore forest ecosystems; (b) gender equality, social inclusion and equity; and (c) criteria for landscape restoration practices. The training programs will include practical exercises and will be specific to the socio-economic and youth groups in each region. At least 50% of the participants should be women and at least 20% from indigenous communities, including Mixteca, Nahualt and Pamehuica. Positive action will be taken to make sure that at least around 30% of the participants are youth and 50% indigenous or also descendant. Subject of the training curriculum will include, but not be limited to:

- Ecosystem services and community-based adaptation practices for forest landscape restoration (see also examples of best practices described in Outcome 2).

## Output 2:1. Agrarian communities improve land-based services through multiannual payment of environmental services (PES)

### 72. **Agrarian Communities** and the protection of large areas of forests and forested landscapes that provide essential ecosystem services for the Basin, Ramsar wetland regulation, storage and erosion control is also critical because the Eucaliptus dam, which is the second most important hydrometric production in Mexico after the Benito Juárez Dam, depends on these ecosystems. The protection of these areas is also necessary to protect their organic soil components need to be protected as they have the capacity to store rainfall water and release it slowly into rivers and streams.

### 73. **Activity 2.1.1. Payment of Environmental Services (PES)**: For the strategic areas for the provision of environmental services, the communities will be paid for the ecosystem services provided by the forested landscapes that they manage through a payment process promoted through the activities in Outcome 1. Agrarian Communities will be put in the capacities to generate and receive payments from the public sector (e.g., Federal Government) as well as private sector entities such as its Hubs for Operation. In contrast to past practices where only forested ecosystem protection was supported by CONAFOR, this payment process will also support other types of ecosystems managed by communities that represent 50% or more of the total area under their management (e.g., wetlands and grasslands). CONAFOR will now pay PES not only for forest protection, but also consider conservation of water resources, soil erosion control, and carbon sequestration. The payment process will be based on a minimum of 50% of PES represented by the communities in the strategic areas (e.g., wetlands and grasslands). Through this mechanism vital ecosystem services of forests and natural ecosystems on forested landscapes that are managed by communities will be protected, restored, and/or improved. Additionally, these investments will leverage conservation finance from the private, state, and NGO sectors to support the transition of public land management systems (e.g., CONAFOR) towards a more sustainable transition from catalytic public (PAGS) to sustained long-term regional and private finance (LM-TF). To be eligible for payments, communities must have signed the Community Forest Management Agreement included in CONAFOR’s Rules for Operation* and include in their proposals for areas of reasonably well-conserved forests that will be managed under PES. Payments shall be provided by the RCMT to secure adoption benefits and alignment with the RMCT.

## Output 2:2. The productive systems of Agrarian Communities are strengthened and better adapted to climate change and variability

### 74. **Agrarian communities** that sustain the livelihoods of rural households of Agrarian Communities in the project region are threatened by changes and variability of climatic regimes as described in detail in Section B.1 above. To address this challenge, a set of adaptation measures will be identified and prioritized to be introduced and implemented by these communities to help them adapt better to significant higher temperatures, increased frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (e.g., droughts, floods), and changes in the vital ecosystem services. The expected main adaptation impact of the activities described below are the following: (1) reduction of the risk of crop failure due to prolonged dry spells; (2) reduction of the risk of flooding; (3) increase of the time that water remains within the landscape and the watershed, decreasing the force and sediment load of runoff; (4) improvement in the quality of soil for agricultural production.

### 75. To this end, an Agrarian Community Federation will be created to finance the investment plans developed by Agrarian Communities through a payment process promoted through the activities in Outcome 1. These payments are invested by its members. These incentive payments ("proyecto") will be done through the following components of the project: a) Forest Plantations and Agroforestry Systems: "Plantaciones Forestales Comerciales y Sistemas Agroforestales" and Strategic Areas "Restauración Forestal de Montesecos y Regiones Estratégicas (RFRM); b) Commercial

**(IPFC) and Community Forest Management and Value Chains "Manejo Forestal Comunitario y Cadenas de Valor" (MFCCV). Through project activity 1.14 these incentives schemes of the PAFDES, which pursue the objective of promoting forest restoration and reforestation in degraded lands, will be complemented with climate adaptation criteria for enhanced ecosystem and livelihood resilience of vulnerable rural population.

76 Activity 2.2.1 Climate Smart Forest Landscape Restoration of Micro-ecoregions and Strategic Areas (ECM). This activity supports implementation of Agroforestry Systems (AFS) in degraded lands, which will reduce soil erosion and ecosystem productivity, generate productive land use for food security, and provide a range of other ecosystem services. These systems exemplify three possible soil and water conservation options out of a multiplicity of variants and their combinations that can be used by farmers who may want to include them in Their Proposals and Investment Plans to be submitted to CONAFOR for financial support.

77 Sub-activity 2.2.1.1 Establish climate resilient agroforestry systems. Cacao and coffee are important cash crops in the region, but they are also very sensitive to changes in temperature and rainfall patterns. Moreover, high value Arabica coffee, especially the type that meets the qualifications of the Fair Trade label, is particularly vulnerable to climate change because it requires a specific microclimate for optimal growth, including a long dry season followed by a short rainy season, which is conducive to the ripening of coffee berries, leading to poorer cup quality. Rainfall in the Bajos PA will increase and become erratic, whereas temperatures are expected to rise. This will have an adverse effect on the production of Arabica coffee, rainfall patterns. Coffee flowering is triggered by the first rainfalls at the onset of the rainy season, but it prolongs flowering if there is a second rain event during the dry season. The result would be that the quality and yield of fruit growth would result in fewer smaller berries of lower quality, which would in turn fetch lower prices! Additionally, coffee trees are more susceptible to diseases such as coffee rust, caused by the spreading of phytophthora and disease development, such as coffee rust, especially at higher altitudes!

78 Through this activity the agrarian communities will be put in the capacity to use degraded forests landscapes and pastures into bio-diverse coffee and/or cocoa agroforestry systems (AFS). They will be provided with the necessary inputs for the establishment of these AFS. The objective is to increase the productivity of the coffee and cacao crops, while at the same time improving soil fertility and water retention in degraded lands. This will be done by buying inputs and materials to establish AFS and restore degraded pasture, rangeland, or renew old degraded croplands. These systems will include a combination of trees and shrubs that are adapted to the local climate and rainfall patterns, as well as annual crops such as beans, maize, sorghum, millet, and other legumes. The AFS will be established in crop-rotation within an AFS as presented in Annex 2a, Chapter 3, would include corn intercropped with 3 or more species of trees and shrubs, including fruit trees, shade trees, and nitrogen-fixing plants. These systems will also include other fruit trees in an area of one hectares. Of especial relevance is the introduction of improved crop varieties, more productive and resistant to climate change, as well as the use of organic fertilizers that will improve soil productivity and high-organic quality. The combination of banana, fruit and native timber tree species and annual crops such as beans, maize, sorghum, millet, and other legumes will increase the resilience of the agroecosystem to climate change, and lower ambient temperature as a result of increased shade. I will protect the soil and water sources; enhance biodiversity and ecosystem services; improve food security and income generation opportunities. An integrative management of AFS will improve social, economic, and environmental sustainability. In addition to these benefits, the establishment of agroforestry systems will also contribute to the conservation of natural resources.

79 Sub-activity 2.2.1.2 Establish Agro-forest water filter and soil retention fences. Increasing and more intense rainfall in heavily degraded areas located on moderate to steep slopes are subject to increased soil erosion and the formation of gullies, which can lead to landslides and other forms of environmental degradation. The premature removal of vegetation, including wild agaves, exacerbates the degradation process caused by extreme rainfall events. This sub-activity will establish Agro-forest water filter and soil retention fences in areas where restoration practices to slow down runoff, enhance rainwater infiltration, retention and storage in the soil, as well as improve the quality of surface water.

80 Approximately 50 m wide fringes of densely planted agaves (Agave americana, A. cupreata, A. augustifolia and others), will be established along the contour lines, followed up with 100 m fringes of biocenosis naturally regenerated forests (SCM) in areas where there is no natural regeneration or where it has been destroyed by human activities.

These fringes will be protected through fences during the first 4 years to prevent goats and cattle to destroy the young agaves. These fences will also protect the agave fringes from being trampled on by livestock, if they are strong enough, will form thorny fences that will hinder goats and cattle to enter the naturally regenerating forest fringes.

As mentioned above, these natural agave barriers will provide a number of ecosystem services such as soil retention, erosion control, water quality improvement, habitat for wildlife, and maintenance of biodiversity. These natural agaves barriers will also improve the performance of these natural agave barriers. Over time the agave fringes will retain and accumulate sediments and organic matter which will form natural terraces contour lines against the slope. These natural terraces

slow down runoff during extreme rainfall events, diminishing soil erosion and improving water infiltration storage and allow for slow water release.

B.1.2. Forest restoration: The ample offer of products from the wild agave species (synops, fibres, arsénical mezcal; others) have a rising demand and are important as income sources for households of Agarian communities in the region. In addition, forest restoration is an opportunity to increase carbon sequestration and sustainably managed forest fragments are important for the provision of timber and non-timber forest products (NTPs). Forest restoration will also provide ecosystem services such as water regulation, soil conservation and to supply the demand for artisanal mezcal production. The adaptation benefits of landscape restoration through agave plantations include: 1) increasing carbon sequestration; 2) improving water regulation; and 3) reducing erosion estimated at 28.2 t CO2e/ha/year.


### Sub-activity 2.1.1. Establish forest watershed retention terraces

This sub-activity forms a part of activity 2.1.1. Establish forest watershed retention terraces. ### Sub-activity 2.2.2.1. Establish climate adapted plant forests (FCPA). Due to climate change and variability, the species composition of forested areas will be different from what it is now in these which forests were established. The principles and strategies* for adaptation are: 1) to maintain ecosystem services; 2) to ensure that the forest continues to provide productive ecosystems that will not be able to be maintained under future climatic conditions. Adaptive silviculture uses advantage of the different coping strategies with which ecosystems deal with changes*, among the main ones are:

- Maintaining species diversity: This is a strategy that can be used in all types of forests, this capacity is useful when ecosystems face small changes. Forest managers can help forest's resistance by improving their defenses maintaining the original ecosystem conditions.


* The principles and strategies for adaptation are described in Annex 2a.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND GRANT PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 18

- **Resilience:** The ability of forests to return to their previous condition after a major disturbance such as wildfires, deforestation, forest pests, or not extreme climate changes.
- **Response options:** If disturbances or disturbances are strong and constant, ecosystems can change in response to the new conditions. This may require changing ecosystem to adapt to the new conditions.

87. Adaptation of forests to climate change through applied genetics that seeks to foster a system to address the functionality of forest ecosystems under conditions of environmental change to meet future needs.
- The model is based on:
  - Tree diversity management: having an optimal density reduces risks such as wildfire, water stress, and pest outbreaks. It also allows for better control over the density of shade, so this can interfere with natural regeneration and can be controlled by silvicultural practices.
  - Stand composition management: maintaining tree diversity in terms of species, age, and size. This increases resilience to climate change because it provides a greater variety of species and genotypes allowing expanding the genetic pool and therefore, improving the adaptive capacity of the forest.
  - Assisted migration of species that do not inhabit the area, but are very likely to do so, due to the projected changes in climate.

88. Symptoms of forest decline have been detected in the forests of the Trans-Mexican Volcanic Belt of the Baja California Peninsula (Mexico). These symptoms include: a) dieback and mortality of trees; b) precipitation causing a significant loss of habitat for Mexico's conifer and pine-oak boresomes, as a collateral effect of climate change; c) decline in the number of species of birds, butterflies, and mistletoes, and the populations of Abies religiosa, the Monarch Butterfly Reserve and threatened species such as the Mexican wolf.
- Together with national academic research partners, including the Forest Faculty of the University of Guadalajara, CONAFOR (National Forestry Commission), and the management practices of Adaptable Silviculture mentioned above, support the adaptive adjustment of the forest to climate change. This will be achieved through the development of a training program for training and capacity building of CONAFOR's local technicians of the Promotoras Forestales, technical supervision, and the implementation of pilot projects in the Baja California Peninsula as models and facilitators.
- The mitigation impact of climate adapted forest plantations in the Baja Peninsula has been estimated to be 0.11 CO2e / ha / year.

Sub-activity 2.2.2.2 Establish climatic-spatial-ecological systems (PFCA2). In contrast with the passive forest buffer zone described for Activity 2.2.1.3, which is designed for extensive degraded areas of common use land of Agroforestry Communities, this sub-activity consists in supporting individual farmers or groups of ACs to implement a system that allows them to adapt their agricultural activities and livestock production located on moderate to low slopes, in relation mainly to housing the service settlement, or town. These systems can be characterized by: a) The presence of trees with high water retention capacity, which are planted at the base of further degradation through soil compaction, erosion and consequently, lower runoff infiltration leading to the formation of gullies and landslides; b) The presence of trees that provide shade for livestock grazing in summer. On the other hand, low feed quality contributes to higher methane emissions from enteric fermentation while also increasing the risk of heat stress in cattle due to high temperatures and more erratic precipitation patterns.

## Sub-objective 2.3.1: Participation of Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations in forest and agroforestry value chains is strengthened.

### 95. Agrarian Forest Management adapted to climate change, inclusion, and access to markets (MFL CCOV). This Component of the PAFSBP aims to support Agrarian Communities that own natural forests and/or forested lands in the province of Chiriquí, Panama, to develop their technical and cultural capacities to manage, utilize and market their forest resources in a sustainable way. Activities will focus on strengthening governance structures, improving forest management for climate change and promote greater inclusion and empowerment of the most vulnerable women and youth in production processes.

### 96. Sub-activity: 2.3.1.1 Manage Forests sustainably adapted to climate change (MFL CCOV). During the project approval mission several Agrarian Communities that were visited and local CONAFOR technicians reported that their forests have been affected by climate change, which has led to a reduction in yield or decline had reduced natural yield variability. Resin yield of Pinus occidentalis and piceafoetida trees being forested on the slopes of the Chiriquí mountains has decreased significantly over recent years due to increased susceptibility to pests and diseases, particularly to bark beetles (Desmotsipora sp.), which have already led to significant mortality in some communities. The same is true for other species such as Pinus caribaea and Honduras pine on the natural forests of Burica province traditionally used by Agrarian Communities for timber production.

### 97. Through this sub-activity, the project will support Agrarian Communities to plan forest management; strengthen governance structures and to implement cultural and harvesting practices applying principles, methods and techniques that are adapted to climate change. The project will also support the development of sustainable forest management that improves the resilience of the forest ecosystems which are an important source of livelihoods for these communities. This will be achieved by strengthening the capacity of local communities in terms of water regulation and soil protection. Furthermore, the project will support community forest monitoring and implementation of best practices to improve the quality of timber production. The project is implemented with regular inputs.

### 98. Agrarian Communities will be supported with incentive payments included in PAFSBP CCOV.1 to obtain technical support from CONAFOR's accredited technical assistance service providers, to formulate management plans and to develop forest management plans that are adapted to climate change. The project will also provide training and technical capacities to undertake climate adapted, sustainable management and harvesting of timber and non-timber forest products.

### 99. Apart from evident adaptation benefits, resin (m) production will be maintained, and climate adaptive diversification strategies will be developed for the communities in order to increase their resilience. The baseline for Agrarian Communities is that at base line the case is that they have access to natural forests, the forest area is only 0.851 CCovha (1 ha).

### 100. Sub-activities: 2.3.1.2 and 2.3.1.3 Sustainable use and added value to timber and to non-timber forest products. The project will support the development of a market for resin, wood and other forest products through commercialization of forest products to sustain their livelihoods. For product sold in raw or poorly transformed raw prices are low and often do not provide income at the end of the year. This situation often causes overexploitation of forest degradation putting vulnerable rural households and forest ecosystems even greater peril and perpetuating the poverty-resources-degradation vicious cycle. ## Subactivity 2.3.1.4 Support for women and youth in households with forest value chains (BFVCY)

Financial and technical support will be provided specifically to trigger entrepreneurship and projects designed, implemented and managed by women and young people in the context of BFVCYs. This will include support to women-managed productive projects and enterprises with the objective to support the empowerment of women and youth in households with forest value chains (BFVCY). These projects may involve any step within the forest production chain, as well as any product and service that can be generated from this chain. Examples are: the use of wood for construction; the use of pine trees, pine resin, oilseed essence (Bursera simaruba), oak charcoal, palm to produce handicrafts (Brachychiton populneus); the production of honey (Apis mellifera); the production of firewood and charcoal; the use of pine wood for construction; the use of pine resin, oilseed essence (Bursera simaruba), oak charcoal, palm to produce handicrafts (Brachychiton populneus); the production of honey (Apis mellifera); the production of firewood and charcoal. Some groups in the Project Area have developed nurseries to produce seeds for reforestation and agroforestry systems; others are involved in the production of medicinal plants, agave and native tree saplings. Projects will be implemented under PMFSC initiative line MFC0213 for productive activities and MFC0214 for social services.

## Subactivity 2.3.1.5 Capacity building for local producers in sustainable forest management adapted to climate change

The training will include courses within the Agrarian Communities, exchanges of experiences between women-managed projects and enterprises, as well as scholarships for women to share best practices and experiences, as well as scholarships for community members.

## Subactivity 2.3.2.2 Support households to improve food security and nutrition

Higher temperatures and more erratic rainfall patterns are expected in the Project Area, which will have a significant impact on vulnerable communities that can be mitigated through targeted strategies to improve the adoption of the traditional maize (maize) and beans and traspalo orchards systems, which are the main sources of food for these vulnerable households.

## Subactivity 2.3.2.2.1 Traditional maize (maize) and traspalo orchards are transformed into climate-resilient agroforestry systems

Vulnerable households will be supported in improving the maize and traspalo house-near orchards through land management practices that increase soil fertility, improve water retention, conserve and multiply native varieties, introduce new crops more resistant to higher temperatures, provide shade for livestock, reduce firewood consumption, reduce the risk of crop loss due to pests and diseases, dependence and spread risks, and introduce more fruit and firewood-producing trees. Other measures to increase the resilience of these systems include: the use of improved seeds; the use of intercropping techniques; the use of solutions and improved cooking stoves that consume less firewood.

## Subactivity 2.3.2.2.2 Traditional maize (maize) and beans are transformed into climate-resilient agroforestry systems

The technological innovation to be introduced is that fruit trees will be established along the contour lines to reduce soil erosion, improve water retention in the soil, provide shade for livestock, and increase biodiversity (e.g., in summer zones: peaches; in the mountains): and other native trees (firewood) will be planted every 50 meters. Other measures include: the use of improved seeds; the use of intercropping techniques; nutrient cycling, and traspalito irrigation. Additional specific technical assistance will be provided to support the compulsory design and implementation of water harvesting and storage solutions, including collector roofs (collectores), tanks ("cubiertones") and reservoirs, among others. Due to the relevance of women in traspalo

### Outcome 2: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Baías have timely and relevant climate information for climate risk management, disaster prevention and knowledge management (USD 4.64 M)

#### 107. Output 3.1.1: Climate hazards related early warning and monitoring systems supported.

The main institutions working in the Baías PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURA) have been developing advanced climate information systems for the region. These systems provide a wide range of climate data and patterns and climate risk prevention. However, climate information is hosted in different systems and platforms that are not easily accessible to local actors. The project will support the development of an online platform and applications that will facilitate access to climate information and early warning systems.

As a result, an online digital platform and its applications will be designed in coordination between CENAFOR, CONAGUA, AGRICULTURA, and the Baías PA authorities. This will allow for the strengthening of the capacities of the main institutions involved in the project region (Baías PA). The project will also strengthen the capacity of local actors to access information on weather forecasts and early warning about hazards from extreme precipitation events.

The online platform will be designed with a user-friendly interface that allows users to easily access climate diagnostic of the regional and local reach and the barriers of the existing climate information systems and risk management.
