The MRV system will be strengthened by incorporating a new approach to forest monitoring that is based on community-based sustainable use and conservation of their forest resources and includes especially women and the youth. This pilot is to be located in the state of Chiapas, Mexico, where CONAFOR has been implementing its successful community-based forest monitoring described in Activity 2.3.3. This approach of community forest monitoring with support of local funding will be implemented by CONAFOR’s MRV System (MRS) and will be extended to other states of the Mexican Federation. CONAFOR's MRY System will be strengthened through the use of new technologies such as drones, satellite imagery, remote sensing, aerial photographs, and vehicle and field equipment for forest measurement (including notebooks, GPS, measuring tapes, etc.). This equipment is already available in CONAFOR’s fleet and maintained as per CONAFOR’s standard equipment maintenance protocols after project completion.

### 11.3.4 Strengthen CONAFOR's social and environmental safeguards management system

While environmental and social safeguard management systems are in place at CONAFOR, further understanding and operational implementation still needs to be reinforced, especially at the level of its field offices. This will include the development of a specific action plan for the implementation of the Forests and Forestry modules within the Indigenous Peoples’ Action Plan (IPAP) and the Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework (IPPF) are understood and fully implemented.

### 12.0

#### Objectives
- **Objective 3.2:** Policy-relevant knowledge products from the systematic documentation of good practices and experiences

##### Activity 3.2.1 Production of synopsis and systematization materials With project support material for capacity building, technical assistance, and training will be produced and disseminated to stakeholders. This includes, but will not be limited to basin intervention approach, forestry adapted to climate change and adaptation needs, forest management practices, indigenous knowledge on forest and tree products, and the role of women in others. Successful experiences will be systematized and documented in at least 4 relevant knowledge products to be shared with stakeholders through various dissemination channels. The project will also document the successful experiences of women in climate resilient activities, relevant for the exchange of knowledge, the adoption of new policies and institutional strategies.

##### Activity 3.2.2 Exchange of experiences and South-South and Triangular Cooperation (SSTC) The Project will facilitate the exchange of experiences with other countries that have similar experiences on forests including, but not limited to adaptive forestry and silviculture, indigenous knowledge on forest and tree products, and the role of women in others. Successful experiences will be systematized and documented in at least 4 relevant knowledge products to be shared with stakeholders through various dissemination channels. The project will also document the successful experiences of women in climate resilient activities, relevant for the exchange of knowledge, the adoption of new policies and institutional strategies.

##### Activity 3.2.3 Monitoring and evaluation Monitoring, reporting and evaluation will be conducted in accordance with the MRV system described above. A monitoring and evaluation framework for the reporting system will be set up by the PMU to carry out regular project monitoring activities, as also midterm and final progress reports. The PMU will also conduct a mid-term review of the project implementation and evaluation related activities, and an external mid-term and final evaluation team hired at the end of the Project period will provide independent evaluations of the project’s performance. Indicators for measuring results on women empowerment will be considered.

HACIENDA (Executing Entity 1 – EE1)

124. HACIENDA is a public financial institution under the Mexican federal government fiscal power into international climate finance agencies. It also administers and defines the federal budget and expenditures for implementing public programs, including those related to the GCF grant. HACIENDA will be responsible for disbursing funds from the Mexican legislation by which HACIENDA grants the mandate to a Development Banking Institution in the case of Banco de Desarrollo de México (BDMX) or a Multilateral Financial Institution in the case of Multilateral Financial Institutions (in the case of BDMX), and the Agencies and Entities executing programs and projects (in the case of MFI). HACIENDA will be responsible for disbursing funds from the GCF grant to the agencies and compliance with contractual commitment, verification of procurement processes; training and technical assistance provided by IFC4 Client Portal.

124. Within HACIENDA, the International Financial Affairs Unit (IFAU), by its acronym in Spanish) is in charge of the following: a) managing the GCF grant disbursement process; b) coordinating with the Treasury for negotiations with international financial agencies; i) exercising the rights, and managing the fulfillment of the obligations related to the GCF grant; c) monitoring the execution of the GCF grant; d) disbursing the GCF credit; and e) negotiating and signing grants and non-reimbursable technical cooperation agreements with foreign entities. HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity.

125. The Treasury of the Federation (TESOROF) is an administrative Unit of HACIENDA in charge of the management of public finances and the implementation of the federal budget. TESOROF will be responsible for transferring budgetary resources to CONAFOR, which advances the amount of funds that are actually disbursed by the IFC4-GCF Loans. As such, HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity.

126. As HACIENDA is the authorized Representative of the Borrower: it will sign the Subordinate Loan Agreement with IFC4 Client Portal; and it will execute the disbursement of funds from the GCF grant to the GCF Loans. As the proceeds of the GCF Loan A and the GCF Loan B will be disbursed by IFID to the Borrower, HACIENDA will not implement any Funded Activity related to the GCF loans. TESOROF, HACIENDA, and CONAFOR subsequently channels it to CONAFOR; HACIENDA will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity.

NAFIN (Executing Entity 2 – EE2)

127. National Financiera, S.N.C., Banco de Desarrollo de México (NAFIN) is an accredited national Direct Access Entity (DAE) based in Mexico. As mentioned above, NAFIN will act as the fiduciary of the loans and Project and will channel funds to CONAFOR. As such, NAFIN will be an Executing Entity, but it will not implement any Funded Activity. NAFIN confirms the disbursement of funds from the GCF grant to the GCF Loans. The proceeds of the GCF Loan A and the GCF Loan B are transferred by IFID in the money market, and once the auction is completed, it transfers the equivalent amount in pesos to the

The Project Coordination Unit (UCP) will be based in CONAFOR's headquarters and will be under the supervision of CONAFOR's Project Technical Team (ETP). The UCP will prepare the reports that will be submitted to the Green Climate Fund (GCF), and the GCF Board. The UCP will consist of 10 people, of which only 4 will be hired by the Project: the Lead Technical Adviser, one financial and one administrative officer, and a communications specialist. The remaining six positions will be filled with external consultants who will be efficient enough project management, coordination, implementation, and monitoring through the effective execution of the GCF grant resources. The UCP will also contribute to the financial management of GCF and IFAD financial resources in accordance with the terms and conditions established by the GCF Board. The UCP will also contribute to the development of CONAFOR's Prioritization and extension workers, (i) facilitating the access to technical assistance for the implementation of the projects, (ii) supporting the identification of the project needs, (iii) contributing of CONAFOR’s different Technical Divisions, including Greenflex’s project team with project proposals, (iv) providing support in the preparation of the GCF grant proposal and its submission, (v) preparing quarterly technical and financial reports, (vi) monitoring project indicators in accordance with the logical framework established by the GCF Board. The UCP will also contribute to the development of a collaborative work plan for close collaboration between the project activities and other relevant partners at the national, state and municipality levels.

The Regional Committees for the Integrated Management of the Territory (RCIMT) will provide advice to CONAFOR and allow for the participation of local stakeholders in the formulation of local priorities. The RCIMTs will be composed by representatives from the municipalities, indigenous communities, organizations of farmers, private companies and other relevant sectoral representatives as well as the members of the GCF grant committee (GCFRC). The RCIMT will have three main functions: (i) support a better coordination of interventions between institutions at the federal, state, and municipality levels; (ii) identify local priorities for the implementation of projects in the municipalities; (iii) provide technical assistance to indigenous communities, organizations of farmers, private companies and other relevant sectoral representatives as well as ensuring that the members of the RCIMT will have access to the needed information regarding the project activities, their progress and progress reports.

CONAFOR’s Forestry Divisions (OFI) will be responsible for: 134. CONAFOR operates Forestry Division Offices called “Promotorías Forestales” at the state (regional) level. These offices are located in each of the states where the project is implemented and the promoters are the promotion of the PAFSFR, identification of priorities in the state, development of proposals for the implementation of projects, and the coordination with the municipalities. The OFI will participate in the selection of the applications for PAFSFR incentives submitted by the agrarian communities in each of the states where the project is implemented. At the local level, there are 10 priority intervention areas (PIA) in the state of Oaxaca, Guerrero, Puebla and Michoacán over the 10 prioritized project intervention areas. At the local level, the promotion of the PAFSFR, identification of priorities at the municipal level, development of proposals for implementation. In each of these small entities, there is a team of Forestry facilitators recruited by CONAFOR, whose function is to provide technical assistance and support in the planning and implementation of projects. They are also responsible for providing technical support to the agrarian communities in their efforts of planning and implementing projects that will contribute to the conservation and sustainable use of forest resources and services, and the development of agrarian and productive structures. The location of the existing local promoters will be used as a basis for the selection of new local promoters who will be recruited by the promotorías forestales will assist the Project UCP first facilitator in facilitating RCIMT meetings to improve the coordination between the project activities and other relevant partners at the national, state and municipality levels. Technical assistance and training will also be provided by CONAFOR’s team and experts recruited by the Project

Project coordination with other relevant institutions, programs, projects, and initiatives

135. One of the most important public policy challenges in rural Mexico is achieving greater efficiency in terms of quality and quantity of government spending on rural development. This challenge is particularly acute for targeted shares of government spending in rural areas. This raises the need for maximum synergy, inter-agency coordination and fostering complementary actions between different spheres of society.

136. SEMINAFOR aims to incorporate in the different spheres of the Mexican society and public sector criteria and instruments that ensure protection, conservation, and sustainable use of the country's natural resources. The three sub-sectors of SEMINAFOR are: i) Forest Sector, which is responsible for the promotion of forest development. In order to comply with the above, SEMINAFOR has three sub-secretariats and the various departments that make up each one. The secretariat in charge of the Forest Sector is the Secretaría de Medio Ambiente y Recursos Naturales (Semarnat). The conservation and sustainable use of ecosystems and their biodiversity; ii) Pollution prevention and control, which is the responsibility of the Secretaría de Medio Ambiente y Recursos Naturales (Semarnat); iii) Water Sector. SEMINAFOR provides advice and supervises CONAFOR and CONAGUA participation in the Baluas PA and ensures that the actions carried out by these institutions are consistent with the objectives, strategies, policies and Action of SEMINAFOR, which is the entity in charge of the National Institute of Ecology and Climate Change (INECC). The three sub-sectors of SEMINAFOR have a direct impact on the conservation and sustainable use of natural resources. In addition to this, they are also responsible for promoting the vulnerability in the prioritized areas of the Baluas PA, contribute to adaptation measures and monitoring in the intervention areas, as well as providing technical support and advice to CONAFOR and CONAGUA.

137. CONAGUA undertakes functions related to the national water policy and regulation for its use (concessions, permits, and sanctions for non-compliances), as well as technical and financial support to the states.

and municipalities in the development of urban and rural hydraulic infrastructure. The Entity is also a change of the National Meteorological Service (SMN) and collaborates in risk protection programs, addressing emergencies due to natural disasters. In this way, the project will be implemented with the support of the SMN. The prioritized municipalities offer a significant opportunity for collaboration and co-financing. Within the project implementation context, the Entity will provide technical assistance to the prioritized municipalities through its own units that contribute to the implementation of PROAGUA and other programs. The communities that do not have access to water services or are in need of improvement of their existing infrastructure can benefit from the support of the RCIMTs. The Project team will seek synergy with the State administrations, municipalities, and the RCMITs. This will be done through: i) the identification of the prioritized areas for the project; ii) the identification of households, management of micro basins to build small hydraulic infrastructure and development of irrigation systems.

## 138. AGRICULTURA The Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development leads the formulation, implementation, and evaluation of the general rural development policy. Among other actions, AGRICULTURA: i) provides technical assistance to the prioritized municipalities for the identification of the areas that will be assisted at sub regional level; AGRICULTURA operates through the Rural Center for Rural Development (CADER) and its units in each region. The CADERs provide technical assistance to the municipalities, including: a) the identification of the prioritized areas that are aligned with the project; b) sharing of information on agricultural practices and technologies; c) support to the implementation of the project through ongoing efforts; d) technical assistance to the prioritized communities to strengthen capacities especially in irrigation systems, water management, and microbasins; e) promotion of sustainable land use (land management and protection); and vi) facilitating collaboration with the sub-regional structures (CADERs) and Research centers that can provide technical assistance and knowledge.

## 140. CONSORCIOS DE FOMENTO AGRARIO ESTATALES (CFAE) Its strategy is to create a framework for the Baixas Project to involve the State and municipal governments, especially in the prioritized sub-basins. The CFAEs are public entities that have been created by the Generalitat Valenciana, the Ministry of CONAFOR, and the State administration are relevant entities to share information and seek synergies. The CFEs will be responsible for: a) promoting the development of projects in the prioritized municipalities; b) collaboration agreements and co-investments, facilitating, in the prioritized municipalities, the possibility for states and municipal governments to participate as project partners; c) providing technical assistance on Payment for Environmental Services, micro basin restoration, or the implementation of PROAGUA. In the recently elected Generalitat Valenciana government, the Project will promote the inclusion of the new Baixas project objectives within the State Development Plans.

## 143. Engagement of the private sector The project will seek to create partnerships with business organizations and private companies, with the aim of both developing innovative schemes for the payment of environmental services and promoting the development of microbasins in the prioritized municipalities. These include:

- Create Local Mechanisms for Payment for Environmental Services, based on the experience and expertise of the SOFOFA (Spanish Foundation for Forests and Sustainable Development) and other companies that use environmental services (water, agriculture, carbon sinks, etc.)
- Foster the creation of Social Forestry Funds (FONAFOR) and/or Special Purpose Funds (SOFOFA in Spain) that have already opened credit lines to finance green and inclusive projects
- Support access to the Guarantees Funds that CONAFOR created to facilitate the access of credit to companies that are involved in reforestation activities, especially in areas with high forest cover or non-forest liner value forests.1 Emphasis will be given on facilitating access to credits for small-scale producers and cooperatives, as well as promoting public-private partnerships and agreements with the private sector in conditions of fairness and equity.
- Specific attention will be paid to the role that women play in the project, especially young women in the forestry and agroforestry value chain, lapping into potential green and fair-trade markets. Products with a high added-value such as agroforestry partnerships are pine and other tree resin, timber, coffee, agave, and palm fibres.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 46

## Fund flow for the GCF Loans - red arrows (Figure 14)

- **GCF transfers GCF Loan A and GCF Loan B resources to IFAD in accordance with signed financial agreement between both entities**
- **IFAD disburses the funds to a bank account managed by NAFIN.**
- **NAFIN channels the resources received to the TESOFE.**

The Treasury of the Federation (Tesorería de la Federación) manages HACIENDA, which channels budgetary resources to CONAFOR and in turn to Fondo Forestal Mexicano (FFM), which pays for the expenses of the Project financed by the IFAD-GCF Loan.

- **CONAFOR is responsible for the implementation of the Project and processes the payments destined for goods and works, consultancy services, non-consultancy services, training, and incremental costs of the project. CONAFOR also provides the necessary information to NAFIN in order to process the payments to the eligible beneficiaries, and instructs BANORTE, financial agent of the FFM, to process the payments.**
- **The beneficiaries send reports and information to CONAFOR, in accordance with the provisions of the TESOFE, and submit them to NAFIN.**

Based on the reports submitted by the beneficiaries and the payments processed to consultants and suppliers, NAFIN prepares the Statements of Expenditure (SOE) financial information in the Statements or Expenditures (SOE), and formally sends them to NAFIN.

NAFIN reviews the Statements of Expenditures and sends them to IFAD, together with the next disbursement request for the Project.

IFAD reviews and verifies those Statements of Expenditures and prepares the Annual Performance Reports and semi-annual financial reports in line with the AIMA and FAA.

## Fund flow for the Grant (see Figure 14)

- **GCF transfers grant resources to IFAD in accordance with signed financial agreement between both entities.**

# B.S. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

## 142. **Justification**: The project GCF financing is made up of: USD 20 million as concessional loan from the Green Climate Fund (GCF Loan B), and a USD 19.5 million grant that, together with an IFAD loan of USD 36-45 million aim at contributing to the implementation of the Batas project. The GCF financing is provided in accordance with the terms and conditions under a Facility Agreement (FA) and the corresponding Operational Guidelines. It also promotes a solution-based approach for addressing environmental challenges, including climate change adaptation and mitigation, coordinating actions between different stakeholders, and promoting sustainable development.

The Batas project aims to address the environmental challenges faced by communities living in the Batac PA with trees of environmental and financial value. The project will provide technical assistance to the municipalities of the project area to maintain the minimum investment required for the conservation of the forest resources under the PROADAG program. The GCF grant will be essential to provide the concessional financial assistance to promote sustainable development, including climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as to finance production models that are not financed within the PROADAG (such as MIAT systems) or other projects that do not have a direct impact on the conservation of forest resources. The GCF grant will also be essential for women in contrast to the loan instruments under Mexican regulations; the grant will secure financial resources and technical assistance for women.

The Batas Project targets the poorest and most vulnerable rural communities in the Batac basin, including indigenous communities with high vulnerability levels. These municipalities have very high and very high vulnerability levels and include a large number of indigenous communities.

145. The poorest and most vulnerable rural communities of these prioritized municipalities have very high limited access to financial services and are therefore unable to invest in their own livelihoods or immediate needs. Investing in forestry and agroforestry will not yield any positive financial returns in the first 3-5 years, which is a period during which these communities would be most vulnerable (negative cashflows for 10 years). Hence, the investments and activities related to forestry and agriculture are not financially viable without concessional financing. The Batas project will provide access to GCF funding through the Facility Agreement (FA) with IFAD. This will allow the municipalities of the Project which have very high and very high vulnerability levels and include a large number of indigenous communities, to access concessional financing from the GCF (in particular the grant, but also GCF Loan A). This is also why HACIENDA, on behalf of the Batas project, has signed an agreement with IFAD to provide technical assistance for the municipalities to the GCF as IFAD. The beneficiaries, in turn, will receive the project financing from the Government of Mexico (in the form of a loan).

146. Importantly, it is worth noting that in the absence of concessional support from the GCF, the paradigm shift required by the Batas project would not be possible and the project would not be financially viable in its current form and would neither reach the highly vulnerable communities identified by the GCF Batas project nor.

## B

### Conditions for Access to Project Resources

Project resources are carried out under strict targeting criteria to benefit the poorest and most vulnerable populations. These beneficiaries face land barriers to credit access, which is why they have been able to place their funds in this project. This is due to the long project cycle of the forest sector characterized by a high level of risk and low returns. The project has a financial return. Such long terms are impossible to bear for the targeted population. Financial incentives are provided through the use of concessional loans (Loans A, B) and grants (Grants 1-3). These resources have existing credit sources, including CONAFOR’s credit funds. In this sense, the grant resources are more likely to be used by the most vulnerable communities in the Bajío region because they do not have access to other forms of financing.

project, which is expected to pay for specialized technical assistance, innovative studies and external support that are not covered in the regular operations. It also allows for the financing of capacity building for both beneficiaries and national authorities, as well as for the implementation of projects related to climate change adaptation and mitigation, as well as an effect of COVID-19-related fiscal limitations, grant resources as an addition to the current budget will be essential.

150: Additionally GCF funds will fill the existing climate finance gap to contribute to meeting the Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs) of Mexico. The environmental sector in Mexico has been severely affected by the COVID-19 pandemic and the economic crisis, with its share of GDP falling from 3% in 2015 to 2.4% in 2018. The environmental sector has been bowed by 32% from 2016 to 2019, which puts at risk the fulfillment of the NDCs. In addition, the budget for the environmental sector in Mexico has been cut by 57%, and the budget for climate change was as high as during the year 2001. This situation is being exacerbated by the COVID-19 pandemic, which has led to a further reduction in public spending on the environment. To meet the NDCs targets and targets for climate finance, GCF funds will leverage public funding and funding from other agencies, including IFAD, the GCF and others, to meet the targets set.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 50

## B. Ext structure (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The project's sustainability and replicability depend on transforming institutional modalities (the Operational Rules of the PAFDFS) used to allocate traditional forest development incentives, re-aligning them as climate change mitigation instruments. The pilot phase will be implemented in the most vulnerable areas of the Balsas PA. As described in Section 2.B above and D.2 further below, the project will seek to strengthen institutional and multi-sectoral coordination mechanisms for the implementation of government priority programs which are currently contributing to the NDCs commitments. The medium-term sustainability of the project is expected to be achieved through the replication of the model between IFAD and CONAFOR for the medium and long term sustainability of the project after its Natural End of 9 years (please see Figure 16 below). The project has strong potential for replicability in other regions of the country. It may even serve as an application model for other countries with similar conditions, and it will further develop the models and practices emerging from this project to apply them at the national level.

### Establishing participatory mechanisms for local and micro-regional planning and setting up social rules that encourage the adoption of climate change adaptation strategies in forest management systems.
- Developing biodiversity, agroforestry and adaptive forestry system technologies which promote native tree species better adapted to climate change.
- Encouraging communities and organizations to incorporate a climate dimension into their technical assistance services and strengthening organizational and technology transfers, especially for the most vulnerable groups.

## EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Performance (maximum 3 pages; approximately 1 page)

107. This cross-cutting project will be contributing to the goals of Mexico’s NDCs in terms of mitigation by reducing emissions linked to the agriculture, forestry, and other land use sector (AFOLU). But the main contribution will be in terms of adaptation through the implementation of a climate change approach that is integrated into the development and human population axis: Axis-B – resilient production systems and food security; Axis-C – conservation, rehabilitation and restoration of natural resources; Axis-D – sustainable energy. The project will also contribute to Mexico’s NDCs with a climate change approach. The mitigation and adaptation sites of on-ice in the NDGS for the AFOLU sector are fully integrated into the development and human population axis: Axis-B – resilient production systems and food security, as found in Annex 2 (Feasibility Study).

108. The project’s cross-cutting, is primarily focused on adaptation. The project will foster climate change adaptation impacts in the 50 prioritized areas of the Baja CA on four ways:

- **Agriculture:** This will increase the agricultural sector’s capacity for adaptation.
- **Most vulnerable to climate change will increase their income, food security and access to water**
- **Climate risks affecting the population and their production systems will be reduced;**
- **Dry season irrigation systems will be supplemented through investments in small-scale rainwater harvesting and water supply infrastructure.**

Adaptation

109. Project beneficiaries will be primarily vulnerable small rural producers and households living in poverty and Indigenous peoples present in the intervention area including: (i) agrarian communities (ACIs) mainly owned by indigenous peoples; (ii) farmer organizations within the ACIs (cooperatives and other formal and informal figures); (iii)

## Mitigation

160. While primarily adaptation focused (80%), the cross-cutting project also includes a considerable mitigation component (20%). The main mitigation activities are: (i) land use change and forest management; (ii) emission reduction target will be achieved by implementing the following activities:
(ii) in the land use, land use change and deforestation sector: implementation of the REDD+ strategy for Mexico, which includes actions to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (EDF), as well as reforestation and afforestation; (iii) in the agricultural sector: agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil burns. CONAFOR has defined a set of mitigation measures that will be implemented under the project, including the following:
(iii) in the agricultural sector: agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil burns.
162. The medium to long-term result of the project is the achievement of the emission reduction target for the year 2030 (see also paragraph 35 above). Furthermore, the Project supports the implementation of all seven components of the National Strategy on Climate Change (SENADEC), which includes the REDD+ strategy.
It is important to note that the SENADES sees new modes addressing the causes of deforestation and forest degradation as a priority. The project will contribute to this objective by implementing actions that directly respond to this requirement. It also outlines a path that will help the Government of Mexico develop these necessary new approaches, which will be based on the results obtained from the implementation of this project.
The approach to mitigation and adaptation. They also may generate a mechanism for distributing resulting benefits to the rural population.

### 2.D Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

162. The core medium-to-long-term result of the project is the adjustment to the Rules of Operation of the PADES II by the end of 2030. This will be achieved through financial resources linked to the paradigm shift, first catalyzed through technical assistance capacity and building in the Pilot Basin PA using GCF concessional financing (see also paragraph 159 above). The project will also contribute to the adjustment of the national forest policy by providing a new model for the management of forests. Additionally, there is great potential for this project to generate additional climate finance from sources in Mexico at the federal level through CONAGUA’s programs.
Support for Sustainable Forest Development (S219) and Forest Protection (E014). These three initiatives are part of the National Strategy on Climate Change (SENADEC), which was approved by Congress between 2017-2021, which represents a combined national annual value of USD 164.5 million per year between the two initiatives (S219 and E014) for the period 2018–2021. Given that the project will be implemented over this five-year period, it is assumed that at least USD 160 billion has the potential to be reassigned as climate finance per year.
The annual average of USD 150 million sums up to the total amount of USD 7.5 billion in 20 years of re-aligned traditional forestry finance (see also paragraph 34 above). This is equivalent to a 6% increase in the current discount rate of 10% (as defined by the Government of Mexico in 2022 – see Annex 10). The sensitivity analysis of the adjusted climate finance during the project lifespan at different discount rates is also presented in Table 8 below.

### Project design aspects that contribute to the paradigm shift
The project will focus on sustainable land use in the Balas PA region, targeting the most climate-vulnerable and poor rural communities in the country, specifically the most vulnerable municipalities with the highest poverty rates. The project aims to increase food security for these communities by increasing the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems, and reduce emissions from production systems in the Basin, via: i) operationalizing the use of climate change criteria in current institutional frameworks.


---
**Page 61**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 57

The project will shift from the current forestry development support approach to a new one that:

- Uses climate change-related criteria for selecting and prioritizing the most vulnerable beneficiaries.
- Strengthens local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the subnational level in the design and implementation of forest management plans.
- Promotes sustainable use management and conservation of crucial ecosystems and ecosystem services to be piloted in the Bajau PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURAGRICULTURAL).
- Empowers women, youth, and indigenous peoples through their participation in Agrarian Communities and Producer Organizations.
- Disseminates climate change adaptation knowledge and skills among vulnerable communities, especially under a progress-based payment system, specifically rewarding performance of adaptation and mitigation initiatives. The project will also support the development of new forest management plans for all existing FADP/OPS schemes only. This will be replicated by the project across other forestry development support programs.

These three paradigm shifts levering will be acted through the following project approach:

166. Piloting a climate-sensitive approach to financial incentives for forestry and rural development (Outcomes 1) and strengthening local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the subnational level in the design and implementation of forest management plans (Outcome 2). This will be done through payments for forestry development through the prioritization criteria of the Operational Rules of the PADSPB. After piloting this approach, it is expected that the project will scale up to all other FADP/OPS schemes.

167. Strengthening local watershed governance and participation of women, youth, and indigenous peoples at the subnational level in the design and implementation of forest management plans (Outcome 2). This will be done through interventions, beneficiaries, and sites for rural development support because of lack of capacities at the local level and limited access to financial resources. The project will also strengthen the capacity of communities to prioritize selection and interventions and uses linking climate change adaptation criteria will inform climate change adaptation knowledge and skills among vulnerable communities.

168. Disseminating climate change adaptation knowledge and skills among vulnerable communities (Outcome 3). This will allow for the renewal of support in the form of incentives in these areas, increasing the scale and sustainability of forest management plans. The project will also strengthen local watershed governance and participation at the subnational level to facilitate access of those most poor and vulnerable communities that traditionally have had less or difficult access to financial resources.

169. Piloting a climate-sensitive rural development support prioritization approach across the Bajau PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURAGRICULTURAL) through the multi-sectoral coordination platforms. This will ensure that rural development initiatives (and their initiatives) operating in the Bajau PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA and AGRICULTURAGRICULTURAL), through the multi-sectoral coordination platforms.

170. Scaling up of the climate-sensitive rural development support prioritization approach across the Bajau PA (CONAFOR, CONAGUA, and AGRICULTURAGRICULTURAL) through the multi-sectoral coordination platforms.

development of the Basins adopts a decentralized, coordinated and climate-responsive approach across all sectors, and presents a model that can be scaled up across Mexico’s rural areas.

### Lessons learned and innovation

The project has significant potential for generating lessons learned and demonstrating innovative approaches to integrating climate change adaptation into key forestry and agricultural policy instruments. This integration, however, will be done gradually. It will be based on the development of pilot projects in the Basins that will lead to field-proved, replicable, and scalable models for climate smart forest conservation and forestland restoration practices.

### D.3 Sustainable development (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The proposed project builds upon the Bajas-South Pacific Basin Regional Development and Welfare Program (RDPW) and the Forests and Water Project (FAP). The project will contribute to reducing emissions linked to agriculture, forestry, and land use change while simultaneously promoting sustainable development in the Basins. These actions are directly linked to Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 – Climate action, which aims to “take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.” The project is also aligned with SDGs 2, 5, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10 of the 17 SDGs, namely: SDG 1 - No poverty; SDG 2 – Zero hunger; SDG 3 – Good health and well-being; SDG 5 – Gender equality; SDG 6 – Clean water and sanitation; SDG 7 – Affordable and clean energy; SDG 8 – Decent work and economic growth. The project will produce multiple environmental, social, and economic co-benefits, being the following most relevant:

- **Maintenance of environmental services**: A crucial environmental or benefit-of-the-people project is the retention, regeneration, and restoration of forest cover in the Basins. This has two important effects: on one hand, and severe and heavy rainfalls on the other hand pose important hazards for local communities and infrastructure. On the other hand, a regular water flow and soil humidity are vital for agriculture which for a regulated water flow and soil humidity is vital.
- **Conservation of biodiversity**: Within the Basins, 62% of biodiversity areas are vulnerable due to the impacts of climate change. The promotion of agroforestry and sustainable agricultural activities into forest areas is further negatively impacting biodiversity in the region. The promotion of climate-adapted forestry and natural regeneration will help to conserve biodiversity by increasing the availability of resources for wildlife, resulting in increased available habitat for wildlife, the preservation of culturally and aesthetically important landscapes, and the maintenance of ecosystem service supplies such as disease regulation.

### D.4 Needs of recipients (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

#### Socioeconomic status

Despite being among the 15 largest economies in the world and the second largest economy in Latin America and the Caribbean, Mexico’s economic performance has underperformed in terms of growth, inclusion and poverty reduction compared to similar countries over the last three decades. Average economic growth per capita in Mexico was 2% between 1980-2000, while it was 3.5% for Brazil, 4.7% for Argentina, and 7.4% (3 million of the population), respectively, according to the multidimensional poverty measure used by the Government of Mexico. It is estimated that in 2019, 68.5% of the population was living below the national poverty line, while 21.5% were living under the level of food security. Data from the National Institute for Statistics and Geography (INEGI) show a proportion of people living in extreme poverty of 34%, compared to 9.2% in 2018 and 13%. According to the World Bank, Mexico is one of the countries with the highest inequality in Latin America. The Basins are particularly vulnerable for poverty and extreme poverty, in particular those promoted by the proposed project (Oaxaca, Guerrero, Michoacán and Chiapas).

The population of 15.8 million people living in the Basins PA is extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. This vulnerability is exacerbated by the high levels of inequality that exist within these communities. These inequalities faced by women in the Basins PA in accessing different services and assets are exacerbated by the effects of climate change, which has a disproportionate impact on women. Women living in the Basins PA are more affected due to the increase in temperatures, the frequency and intensity of extreme weather events (such as droughts, fires, hurricanes, and torrential rains). Women’s access to resources is also limited because many women, such as the management and harvesting of non-timber forest products, are affected by the loss of vegetation due to droughts, fires, hurricanes, and torrential rains. In addition, many of the women驿站士 not being carriers

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 3.0 | PAGE 59

## Absence of alternative sources of finance

Despite their great cultural wealth and their natural resources, the indigenous and traditional peoples have the lowest economic capacity to adapt to climate change impacts. They are also among those most vulnerable because they live in extremely vulnerable to climate change impacts. Based on an evaluation of climate vulnerability and adaptation needs, the PA has identified 121 municipalities as being highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. These include a total of 121 municipalities and respond to the needs of AG, small producers, women, youth, people with disabilities and indigenous peoples. The PA is committed to ensuring that these groups are not left behind in efforts to adapt to extreme weather events and climate change.

## Institutional capacity needs

The PA has identified a wealth of existing climate governance in the Baja California PA. This is partly because efforts to integrate a climate approach into public policies have only recently begun. Furthermore, institutional coordination and integration between the various actors involved in climate change adaptation are still lacking. Additionally, there have been no clearly focused incentives encouraging concerted action for climate change mitigation and adaptation at all levels of government.

## D.S. Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

This project has been developed in collaboration with multiple institutions and stakeholders under the lead of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT) and the National Council for Science and Technology (CONACYT). The Office for the Coordination of Advisers to the Presidency of the Republic of Mexico (OFAPE), the National Forestry Commission (CONAFOR), the National Institute of Agricultural Research (INIFAP), while IFAD and the FAO Investment Centre have supported the drafting of this project that has involved a participatory process with stakeholders. The project is based on the identification of institutional modalities and development models that have identified that foster low emissions and increase resilience to climate change in Mexico. This project will be implemented through the National Development Strategy (NDSC) and has been designed to implement Mexico’s climate change, environmental and social policies, and priorities, specifically:

Mexico's Nationally Determined Contributions 2020 update (NDCs) respond both to mitigation and adaptation problems experienced in the country across multiple sectors. Using a cross-cutting approach, this project will contribute to the implementation of the NDCs by supporting the development of climate change information in Mexico’s NDSC. According to the INCC, the National Commission for Climate Change, agriculture and forestry sector (sector 10) is one of the most vulnerable sectors in Mexico and fully aligns with the overall aim of ecosystem-based adaptation (EbA). The NDCs recognize the need to incorporate climate criteria into

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 60

The project framework aims to contribute to the GCFS goals firstly in terms of Climate Change Adaptation, specifically by promoting the development and implementation of climate change adaptation strategies at all levels (project activities) as well as by contributing to the promotion of mitigation and adaptation – all project outputs). i) ESA – Output 2.1 (climate-sensitive management of ecosystems), and ii) NDCs – Outputs 2.1, 2.2, and 3.2 (climate change adaptation strategies at all levels).

### Climate Change Mitigation

This will involve reducing emissions through activities focusing on the (a) land use sector (including afforestation/reforestation, forest management, sustainable forest management, agroforestry, farmland management and pasture management); Outputs 2.1 and 2.2 (increased commercialization and added value of products from forests and agroforestry systems; increased production of high-value timber and other products); and (b) agricultural sector via soil management, scheduled soil burns and manure management; Output 3.2 (reduction in GHG emissions from livestock).

### Mexico’s National Climate Change Strategy (2015-2024)

The National Emissions Trading Scheme (NEDS) sets the long-term planning for climate change in the country. The proposed project will contribute to the following objectives of the NEDS: i) increasing carbon sinks and reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation; ii) increasing sectoral resilience to climate change (lines of action A17 and A11); all project outputs, iii) reducing GHG emissions in the land use sector by 20% compared with baseline levels for the period 2015-2030 (line of action B6.4 – Outputs 2.1, 2.2, ii); iii) sustaining and using protecting ecosystems while promoting sustainable development; iv) increasing carbon sinks in the land use sector by 20% compared with baseline levels for the period 2015-2030 (line of action B6.4 – Outputs 2.1, 2.2, and iii); v) promoting agricultural and forestry best practices in order to increase and sustain natural carbon sinks; vi) increasing the resilience of ecosystems and communities to climate change.

### The Special Program on Climate Change 2021-2024

The Program Especial de Cambio Climático (PEC) is a national program that seeks to achieve the objectives set out in Mexico’s National Climate Change Strategy. This will be achieved by the development and implementation of 169 specific actions under four priority objectives: i) promoting climate change adaptation; ii) reducing emissions from deforestation and forest degradation; iii) promoting sustainable land use management; iv) improving governance.

The proposed project aligns with the PEC through the following lines of action: 1.1, 3.3, 6.2, 7.1, 7.2, 8.1, 9.1 and 9.4.

### Mexico’s National REDD+ Strategy for Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation

The National Program for the Conservation of Forests and Forestland (Programa Nacional de Conservación de Bosques y Bosqueal) has the following objectives: i) increase forest area via sustainable management, natural regeneration, reforestation, and afforestation; ii) improve the quality of life in rural communities and economic capital of rural communitiess; and iii) to increase sustainable best-practices in production at the national level. The project will contribute to these objectives by promoting the development of resilient and low-emission management of ecosystems to enhance service supplies, as well as through climate change adaptation strategies that are aligned with Mexico’s National Climate Change Strategy for forests and prioritization of rural agroecosystem. The Project will support the implementation of all seven components of the NDCs.

### Project Activities

Project activities will directly support the National Foresty Program 2020-2024 (Programa Nacional Forestal) and the REDD+ Strategy, which has the main objective to promote conservation, sustainable use and diversified use of forest resources through the implementation of a national program for the development and development of rural networks and services in the communities living in forest areas. Activities under Outputs 2.1 and 2.2 will directly contribute to the project’s objectives.

### The General Law on Climate Change (2012)

The General Law on Climate Change (Ley General de Cambio Climático / LGCC) aims to align with Mexico’s National Climate Change Strategy, which is aligned with the Paris Agreement and the following institutions: the National Institute of Ecology and Climate Change; the Climate Change Council; and the National Commission for Environmental Protection. The law establishes a multi-sectoral approach (public, private, national, international) in cooperation to support actions to combat climate change. The law was amended by the Federal Law on Emissions Trading (Ley Federal de Esquemas de Emisiones) which will comply with Mexico’s NDCs and promote the development of institutional coordination mechanisms for mitigation activities that are implemented at the Basins PA are effectively coordinated between government authorities at all levels.

The project is aligned with the objectives of institutional coordination mechanisms for climate-driven rural development in the Bajío PA (Output 1.2).

### Cross-cutting program alignment

This cross-cutting program aligns with the goals and commitments in Mexico’s most recent National Climate Change Strategy, which aims to promote the efficient use of resources and the promotion of mitigation and adaptation. One of the main principles of this plan is the promotion of the efficient and responsible use of natural resources. The project will contribute directly to specific objectives of the NDP, including: i) the stimulation of innovation, competitiveness and productivity in the agricultural sector; ii) the development of new products or more productive sectors of the economy in an environmentally conscious and sustainable manner; and iii) the

## Institutions involved and stakeholder engagement

The Project Implementation Arrangements (PIA) for the project are being developed by IFAD as the GCF Accredited Entity (AE). CONAFOR is in charge of the Project Execution Entity undertaking at operational responsibility for the implementation of the Project (EE), and the Mexican Government, represented by SEMARNAT, will be responsible for the overall coordination. The institutions involved have been extensively described in Section B-4 Implementation Arrangements above and will therefore not be repeated here.

Under the guidance of CONAFOR and the CAP in Mexico City, Guadalajara and the Baja Peninsula, a series of workshops were held with indigenous communities and other stakeholders to gather relevant information required for the preparation of the Funding Proposal with contributions from all actors involved. The workshops also served as an opportunity to present the project idea and to provide feedback on the project area. In addition, the mission provided the opportunity to identify and visit a diversity of actors, communities and institutions that will be directly or indirectly affected by the project.

The Project Mission was held in Mexico City from 28-30 September 2021 with representatives from all stakeholders involved in the project. Mission participants visited several ejidos and indigenous communities who have already received support under the GCF projects, as well as other actors such as local government officials, civil society organizations, and private sector entities.

The mission also provided an opportunity to gather diverse views from different rural development actors about community enterprises, productive activities, land use and tenure systems, and gender equality. The mission was also used by indigenous initiatives priorities women and youth, as the active and visible participation of these groups is recognized as an essential element for the success of the project. During the mission, a series of workshops were held to discuss strategic priorities, implementation arrangements for the project were discussed and agreed upon and a work plan developed.

During the months of September and October 2022 several meaningful consultation workshops were conducted with indigenous communities in the project area. The results from these meetings have been documented as part of the Indigenous Peoples Consultation and Participation Plan included in Annex A and are available for reference.

## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max.: 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Economic and Financial Analysis (please see also Annex 3) shows that this project is economically viable and will contribute to the achievement of the GCF Strategic Outcome 2 and includes the increase in productivity, resilience of the productive systems to climate change, and mitigation benefits from GHG emissions reductions. The project has been designed with a strong focus on the identification of indicators for the correct implementation and monitoring of the project investments, which also increase the adaptation benefits of the project. However, it is important to note that the economic analysis shows that the project will achieve results rather conservative.

The project design focuses on the investments, where forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change for the provision of environmental services and increased livelihood resilience and the restoration of degraded lands. The project also includes investments in the restoration of Micro-watersheds and Strategic Areas (RMS), 2.2 Common Forest and Agroforestry Plantations Agroecological Systems, 2.3.1 Support households to improve food security and nutrition, Models for access to markets (MF CIV), and 2.3.2 Support households to improve food security and nutrition.

The economic results at the aggregate level of the project are estimated based on the area (in hectares) or projects estimated to be completed by the project. Importantly, this step also includes the benefits related to climate change adaptation and mitigation as well as the benefits from the World Bank Group for the low social price scenario (see Annex 22). Three price scenarios were used to include the mitigation benefits from the World Bank Group for the low, medium and high social price scenarios. The discount rates used to estimate the economic net present value (NPVs) were 6% and 10%. The discount rate of 6% was used in the scenario with the low social price while a discount rate of 10% is used for the two other scenarios. Although important adaptation benefits are not included, the economic rate of return (ROR) as well as the net present value for the discount rates used are positive.

A sensitivity analysis was carried out for following different scenarios: (i) a reduction in net benefits due to the combination of risks in sale prices, product yields, (ii) levels of adoption, (iii) survival of the ventures, (iv) delays in starting the project or incorporating beneficiaries, and (v) mixed scenarios of these phenomena. The results are presented below for the baseline scenario and the most unfavorable conditions.

### Cost-Benefit Analysis

Regarding other efficiency and effectiveness indicators, the co-financing ratio of the GCF funds versus the government and FAD funds in the project is 1:1.3, which is higher than that of the country and is evidence that the GCF is not the major source of funding for the project. The cost for the project per direct beneficiary amounts to 364 USD while the carbon price is 705 USD. The project has a positive NPV/GRS ratio in all scenarios considered, even when implementation is still achieved at a cost of 18.7 USDton CO2eq which is almost half of the lowest social price of carbon.

#### Replicability
An explicit model for climate-resilient and low-emission development in production systems in the forest and agricultural sectors in Mexico has not yet been proven. As such, there is no proof of concept or replication strategy by multisectional coordination structures.

The project's paradigm shift has the potential to be replicated across Mexico through:
- The operationalization of a new model for the financing of rural development incentives.
- The strengthening of multisectional platforms and access to resources of national financial development programs such as CONAGUA. These approaches are the backbone of the project strategy and its potential for replicability in Mexico.

- The strengthening of multisectional platforms and access to resources of national financial development programs such as CONAGUA. These approaches are the backbone of the project strategy and its potential for replicability in Mexico.

There is currently no consistent long-term budget allocation to climate finance within Mexico’s development framework. Financial flows are mainly directed to top-down national development projects in the country's rural areas.

The core medium-to-long term result of the project is related to the application of innovative financing mechanisms introduced above, first piloted in the Balsas PA using GCF concessional funding. The project will realign an average of USD 160 million annually from Mexico’s existing budget provided by CONAFOR towards climate finance for adaptation and mitigation initiatives in the Balsas PA. The financial exit strategy is based on a blended approach. This will ensure that all-aligned projects, including those with climate and mitigation initiatives in Mexico are financially sustainable.

The prioritization and selection of interventions and sites using climate vulnerability criteria focused support in rural areas within the Balsas PA. The project will use an annual progress-based payments system to incentivize the implementation of the form of incentives in these areas. Increased resilience and sustainability impacts of the climate-focused initiatives. Building on the lessons learned, it is establishing participatory mechanisms for local communities to: i) identify and setting up social rules that afford them access to resources while taking into account the threats and vulnerabilities they face; ii) Developing forestry, agroforestry and


---

---

adaptive forestry system technologies which promote native tree species and its spatial arrangements and combinations with other trees and crops (i) Encouraging existing rural extension services to include a climate change dimension into their technical assistance programs (ii) Facilitating international and technology transfers, especially for the most vulnerable populations.

## Activity 1.3.1: Capacity development programs for extension workers and local facilitators of forest production brigades

The Project enhances the current capacity development program for extension workers and local facilitators of forest production brigades, with a focus on training in sustainable management of forested lands, use of agroforestry systems, climate change adaptation, including the inclusion of youth and indigenous total productive units (TPUs) in forest-based livelihoods, food production and nutrition.

## Activity 1.3.2: Support and capacity development for community-based climate change resilience facilitators

The project supports the creation and strengthening of local facilitator groups to support women, youth, and indigenous total productive units (TPUs) in communities linked to the climate change resilience

## Activity 1.3.3: Strengthening CONAFOR Promotoras/Proyectos forestales

The Project will strengthen the CONAFOR Promotoras/Proyectos forestales mechanisms to support the project implementation.

### Sub-activity 1.2.2.1: Basic training event (2)

Sub-Activity 1.2.2.3 Formulates Investment Plans for Forest Production Brigades (Community Consultation)

### Sub-Activity 1.3.2.1: Financial support for logistics and equipment to facilitate the operation of brigades and monitoring positive action measures to improve the livelihoods of women, youth, and indigenous total productive units (TPUs) linked to forest production

Sub-Activity 1.3.2.2: Financial support for the operation of brigades against forest fires & parasites (financial support for logistics and equipment)

### Sub-Activity 1.3.2.3: Acquisition of equipment and materials

Sub-Activity 1.3.2.4: Training sessions and technical assistance related to the inclusion of women, youth and indigenous total productive units in agroforestry systems, as well as Project M&E system.

## E.7. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

## During project implementation, CONAFOR will be responsible for overall Project monitoring.

The project will recruit a full-time Monitoring and Evaluation (MAE) officer who will collaborate with the UCP and ETP of CONAFOR. The Project's progress will be measured against the indicators included in the Project Logical Framework above. A projected Monitoring and Evaluation plan will put together according to the approved Logical Framework. The plan includes a household survey, which is part of the baseline survey, and an endline survey that will be conducted at the end of the project.

The MAE officer will also collect data on the indicators related to the implementation of the Project. The final review will assess the achievement of indicators, sustainability of results, and lessons learned. The parameters of the monitoring system are described in detail below.

Although impact indicators are essentially related to the improvement in the capacity for adopting new climate-resilient forest landscape management and restoration practices, they also include a set of indicators that measure the progress towards the achievement of the project's broader objectives, which are defined as follows: (1) reduction in deforestation; (2) increase in forest cover; (3) increase in carbon sequestration; (4) improvement in livelihoods related to the implementation of the Project. The MAE system will also measure improvement in terms of the development of new institutions and capacities, which are defined as follows: (1) improved institutional capacity for climate change adaptation at the national level; (2) improved institutional capacity for climate change adaptation at the subnational level; (3) improved institutional capacity for climate change adaptation at the different levels of the Government. The Logical Framework also includes indicators that reflect the progress of the Project in terms of the forest landscapes areas covered by the project, as well as the number and type of households involved.

The MAE officer will be responsible for the collection of data on all these indicators. The MAE officer will work closely with the project team to follow up on the progress of the different outcomes of the Project. Each Multistakeholder Investment Plan supported in the prioritized areas will also include basic information about the households involved.

### Collection of MAE Data

The collection of MAE data in the field and at the CONAFOR level will combine different systems and activities:

- **MAE System**: The MAE system is based on the Apoyo SIAC (for the Spanish acronym) collects and records information on the proposals formulated by potential beneficiaries, the evaluation of these proposals and the support actions by the PAFES. This system also includes a database that will be used to record the names of the households involved in the project, as well as the location of the beneficiaries, the intervened polygons, and the delimitation of the rural communities. SIAC will be a major information system to collect, organize, and disseminate data on the project's activities.

- **Regarding overlapping / double counting of beneficiaries**: To grant and incentive payment within the PAFES, CONAFOR signs a legal agreement with each beneficiary. The agreements are signed by the beneficiary and CONAFOR. The agreements include the name of the beneficiary, the number of the individual identification number "Censo Unico del Registro de Populación" (CURP). This information is used to identify the beneficiaries in the SIAC database. In some cases, a single household benefits from more than one action, as some are synergistic and/or complementary. In those cases, CONAFOR will consider that there is no overlapping or duplication by using the unique personal identification number CURP; as its holder is counted as only one beneficiary for the CONAFOR.

- **Displacement of beneficiaries**: The MAE officer will collect data on the displacement of households to new locations in order to obtain information on a probabilistic sample of potential project beneficiaries, which will be later divided between control and treatment groups. This survey aims to determine whether there is an association between displacement to measure improvements in climate resilience, livelihood, and adoption of new practices, as well as gender and ethnic disaggregated information.

- **The project indicators**: The MAE officer will collect data on the following indicators:

  - The Cits consist of 1 outreach, 20 output and 22 outcomes indicators that mapped to the strategic objectives (SOs) and areas of thematic focus of the project. These indicators are grouped into three categories: (i) Project Outputs; (ii) Project Outcomes; and (iii) Project Impacts.

  - The Local Bridges for Measuring, Reporting and Verification (LBRM) is a new CONAFOR initiative that was piloted through the Bajos project and then scaled up to the national level. Within the project area, it will be implemented by one local expert and two rural youth (men and women) who will be recruited by CONAFOR as participants in the data collection for the National Forest Inventory, the estimation of the deforestation rate, as well as


---

monitoring of a sample of pilots where the Project supports the improvement of the management of forest landscapes. These MRV (brigades will report data to the CONAFOR MRV team through a new online application that will be developed by the Project) refer to Activity 3.1.3).

The Project will also recruit independent consultants who will assess project progress in terms of main impacts, outcomes and output achievements and will provide recommendations for future actions. The consultant will conduct field visits to organize focus group discussions and evaluate the level of satisfaction of the beneficiaries, as well as correct safeguards implementation.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Low annual project budget allocations and/or the failure to allocate resources in accordance with the project timeline can seriously disrupt disbursements and operations.

### Mitigation Measures (TBC)

IFAD's experience in Mexico has shown national systems to be advanced and reliable, which will ensure that the probability of this risk occurring remains low. To further mitigate this risk and ensure that project implementation is not adversely affected, IFAD will work closely with CONAFOR (EE) to promptly allocate sufficient budgetary resources over the project life cycle.

## Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Lack of coordination between the various state agencies involved in project development (including CONAFOR, AGROCLIMA, SEMARNAT, BENEFICIA, FISMA, etc.) is a risk. This is linked to a lack of platforms to foster dialogue.

### Mitigation Measures

The project will contribute to building the institutional capacities of these stakeholders by strengthening inter-institutional coordination at the federal, state and municipal level. In addition, the project will ensure regular exchanges between the various agencies involved in project development, which will help bring them together for dialogue and planning related to project implementation. The risk can also be mitigated through close cooperation with the Secretariat of Public Administration for the Coordination of Advisors to the Presidency of the Republic, HACIENDA and the implementing partners.

## Selected Risk Factor 3

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Economic crises may arise as a result of the disruption such as the COVID-19 pandemic. This, in turn, could lead to reduced government revenue and thus, limited public investment in initiatives and incentives for rural production or forest landscape conservation activities, including PES.

### Mitigation Measures

In the case of unavoidable disasters, such as the COVID-19 pandemic, which may result in economic crises, investment in rural production and forest landscape conservation activities will be safeguarded through provisions in the project's budget. The project will also ensure that contingency funds from the national operational budgets are purposefully to support and protect economic development in rural areas, including during economic crises (such as a result of the COVID-19 pandemic), which will contribute to mitigating this risk.

## Selected Risk Factor 4

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |

## Fluctuations in the exchange range, particularly devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the US dollar, negatively impact project budget planning, disbursement and spending, which may disrupt the implementation of the project.

| Factor | Medium | High |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Fluctuations in the exchange range, particularly devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the US dollar, negatively impact project budget planning, disbursement and spending, which may disrupt the implementation of the project. |  |

The GCF funding will be disbursed quarterly throughout the project's term, in the historically and presumptively stable currency of the USD, which will mitigate the impact of any devaluation of the Mexican peso relative to the dollar. The project will also have access to a contingency fund that may be used for disbursement into non-documented accounts to allow for an onshore account. Although the project's financial model is articulated in US Dollars, it will be implemented using Mexican pesos. The project will be managed by CONAFOR and funded by CONAFOR for project costs. The Operating manual governing CONAFOR’s management of these funds documents the use of a contingency fund to mitigate the impact of currency fluctuation.

## Selected Risk Factor 5

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Low | Medium |

### Description
The project may face a change in the Federal Government, shifting priorities in the target region and resulting in varied institutional support during the implementation period.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
This risk can be mitigated by aligning the project with state programs aimed at climate change mitigation and poverty reduction – both during project design and implementation. This will ensure that the project is tightly aligned with government priorities at all times during the implementation period. Similarly, the project will align with other federal and state contests which may foster opportunities for relevant stakeholder to ensure continued support.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | High |

### Description
Forest fires (caused by humans) and extreme weather events (such as floods) may result in damage to or loss of forest cover, negatively affecting the project's progress and subsequent conservation activities.

## Selected Risk Factor 7

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description
Organized crime in the Project Region may hinder or delay project implementation and/or put project personnel at risk.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
Military intelligence of the Government of Mexico informs the CONAFOR, as well as all other government institutions, about the presence and the situation relative of organized crime in their respective region. CONAFOR's local field offices (promotores) obtain direct information about the security situation so that dangerous situations can be avoided and the security of CONAFOR’s technical staff is granted. This project will reinforce the communication
