G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social safeguards (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

#### 189. To identify environmental and social risks and propose appropriate mitigation measures, an Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) has been prepared for the project (Annex 6). Based on the assessment of potential adverse impacts, the project will be categorized as Category B. The ESMF is a living document. This categorization was selected because any potential environmental or social impacts related to activities (including those that may occur in the future) are considered low risk and can be managed through appropriate mitigation measures. All sub-projects under OIM will be screened to ensure a Category B risk level or lower, although some projects may have higher risks due to their specific characteristics. An ESMF for each of these sub-projects will be prepared.

#### 190. CONAFOR has an adequate institutional framework to assess and manage potential social and environmental impacts. CONAFOR’s operational rules (Reglas de Operación - ROs) and manuals (guías) are used to support the implementation of projects, which include specific requirements for the identification and mitigation of activities as per Mexican Law and safeguard requirements. The project will strengthen these ROs and CONAFOR’s capacity in terms of human resources, information systems, and institutional framework to ensure the implementation of diverse programs and activities with IFAD, the World Bank, the Forest Investment Program, the Bio Carbon Fund, and other international organizations. CONAFOR has a strong track record of compliance with the GCF’s safeguard requirements. CONAFOR has successfully managed tens of over US$500 million and global projects in Mexico.

#### 191. The project seeks to increase the resilience of vulnerable forest-dependent communities and the ecosystems they depend on, while also contributing to the achievement of national climate change mitigation goals. For Tzotzil, Triqui, and Pamehutic ethnic groups and other vulnerable groups, the greatest risk identified is the possibility that their participation in the project will be limited by their lack of access to resources or opportunities for beneficiaries rather than on their potential leadership. In this sense, the most significant risks revolve around:

- Marginalization and erosion of Indigenous and vulnerable groups’ aspirations, capacities, knowledge, practices, and traditional land use and stewardship.
- Indigenous governance mechanisms.
- Indigenous participation capacity.

#### 192. The project’s environmental risks and impacts on biodiversity and natural habitats are very small. Introduction of invasive species, deforestation, or the introduction of exotic species, are prevented at the inception phase of sub-project through the use of CONAFOR’s screening tools and exclusion list. To ensure proper implementation of these measures, CONAFOR will provide training to project staff in risk identification, mitigation, and monitoring.

#### 193. Reference Handbook Mechanism (HR) to handle eventuality questions, complaints, suggestions, access to information requests, comments and grievances is already place at CONAFOR (called the Mecanismo de Acción Ciudadana). The mechanism includes two components: (i) Citizen Information and Complaints Service (SIC), which regulates, and attention protocols, depending on the nature of the matter to resolve and/or attend; namely, (a) Information Request (RI); (b) Complaints (C); (c) Suggestions (S); and (d) Access to Information (AIF). If any project-related grievances cannot be resolved at CONAFOR level, they will be referred to the GCF’s Compliance Body (CBCC), which is responsible for handling complaints. The CBCC has a dedicated mechanism to handle such cases.

#### 194. A Complaints Procedure exists to ensure that appropriate mechanisms are in place to allow individuals and communities affected by the project to lodge their concerns, suggestions, or grievances with CONAFOR if they believe that the project not complying with IFAD’s social and environmental policies. Complainants are thus encouraged to first bring the matter to the attention of CONAFOR. If the complainant is not satisfied with the response received or has not adequately responded or if they feel they might be subject to retaliation if they approach the EE directly, they may lodge their complaint with the CBCC. The CBCC will then investigate and resolve the complaint.

#### 195. The GCF Board of Directors (BGC) has approved the Global Compliance Body for Climate Change (GCBCC). In order to complement GCB’s supervision capacity at the field level, safeguard monitoring will also be carried out by the GCBCC in collaboration with CONAFOR and other implementing agencies as appropriate purpose. The GBCC will be in charge of overseeing training and capacity building at the IFAD to ensure proper implementation of safeguards requirements. The GBCC will also monitor the project’s compliance with the GCF’s safeguard requirements, ensuring compliance with safeguard requirements.

#### 196. Information on all safeguarding sub-projects will be disclosed in a timely manner. For Category B projects, disclosure shall be at least 30 days before the expected date of approval of the GCF in accordance with the requirements set out by the GCF. CONAFOR will provide information to the public and other stakeholders via its website at least 30 days before the expected date of approval by CONAFOR. Documents disclosed will be presented in an

### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)

197. In line with GCF's gender policies, the project stimulates climate investments that contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing resilience to climate change while promoting gender equality. To this end, a Gender Assessment and Action Plan (GAAP) (see also Annex B) was it carried out now, that shows in the Millennium Development Goals (MDGs), women are more vulnerable than men to the effects of climate change. Women are poor. Women represent only 23% of landowners and gender stereotypes and machismo are deeply rooted in rural communities. Women have less access to credit and technical assistance and less decision-making power, which makes them more vulnerable to climate change.

Among the most relevant gender gaps related to the project are:

- **Women’s vulnerability**: Women are more vulnerable than men to the effects of climate change. Women have higher levels of literacy in, are less connected with the market economy, and have less access to credit and technical assistance.
- **Women’s participation in agriculture**: Only one third of women in agriculture are paid for their work and have less access to credit and technical assistance.

198. The project will address these gender gaps by:

- **Improving women's livelihoods**: Women are the heads of households due to the labour migration of men and are in charge of agricultural production, which is more vulnerable than other economic activities to climate change.
- **Increasing women’s access to credit and technical assistance**: This will increase their capacity for production and resilience to climate change, affect women's performance and increase their vulnerability.

199. The GAAP includes the following actions:

- **Convenes and strengthens women's participation in decision-making in RCMTs for the prioritization of investments that contribute to reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing resilience to climate change**
- **Promotes greater gender equity in institutional work by adjusting sectoral and/or interinstitutional policies, where appropriate.**
- **Includes women as beneficiaries of investment plans in basins and micro-basins and for this purpose develops a gender action plan that includes specific actions based on the needs of the target population according to gender-based calls for projects with inclusive governance and based on profiles of the target population according to gender-based calls for projects with inclusive governance.**
- **Proposes training in gender and inclusion for extensionists, local facilitators, brigadiers and promotes development of a gender action plan that includes specific actions based on the needs of the target population according to gender-based calls for projects with inclusive governance.**
- **Promotes the participation of women in agro and communal authorities and to use selection criteria that include gender equality as an objective.**
- **Promotes that women participate in projects to improve the productive systems of the farming communities, to increase their capacity for production and resilience to climate change.**
- **Promotes that women benefit from the financial transfers made by the project to ACs for the hiring of technical assistance, provision of inputs and training on gender equality.**
- **Proposes that climate-resilient sustainable forest management activities open up opportunities for women's participation in decision-making processes at all levels (local, regional, national) and in the development of timber and non-timber forest products, strengthening their capacity on gender, sustainable and climate-resilient production practices, organizational, managerial, and marketing strengthening.**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V3.0 | PAGE 95

In Mexico, adopted an official interinstitutional poverty measure that combines income-based monetary poverty with a non-monetary dimension of vulnerability to shocks and stressors. The new measure is based on the following components:

- **Household Income:** This component measures the household's ability to meet its basic needs.
- **Vulnerability Index:** This index assesses the household’s exposure to various risks, such as natural disasters or economic downturns.

No-objection letter issued by the national designated authority(ies) or focal point(s)

HACIENDA

Subsecretaría de Hacienda y Crédito Público  
Unidad de Asuntos Internacionales de Hacienda  
Dirección General  

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)  
Mexico City, 06 March 2023  

Re Funding proposal for the GCF by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) titled Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies (BALSAS) project.  

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project titled Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies (BALSAS) in Mexico as included in the funding proposal submitted by the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) to us on 24 February 2023.

The undersigned is the duly authorized representative of the Ministry of Finance and Public Credit, the National Designated Authority for the United Mexican States.  

Pursuant to GCF decision 6/005, the content of which we acknowledge or have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection it is implied that:

a) The government of the United Mexican States has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal.  
b) The project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with the national planning and budgeting process.  
c) In accordance with the GCF's environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.  

Kind regards,

THE DIRECTOR GENERAL  
Laura Elisa Aguirre Tellez  
NATIONAL DESIGNATED AUTHORITY FOR MEXICO

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP236

## Proposal name: Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies (BALSAS)

### Accredited entity: International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)
### Country: Mexico
### Project/programme size: Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
|-----------|-------------------|
| This project introduces a new integrated territorial development approach by using climate change related criteria for selecting and prioritizing communities, beneficiaries, interventions and sites. | The project has the potential to unlock up to USD 1.5 billion in domestic public finance by aligning federal rural extension modules with the BALSAS project. However, achieving the USD 1.5 billion figure depends on the federal government’s willingness to use its own approach to allocation of rural development funds, which lies outside the scope of the project. |
| The BALSAS project's sustainability will be increased by using and strengthening existing public domestic rural extension and development channels. | - |
| Its potential impact ratio is 1:40 thanks to the possibility of scaling up the realignment of domestic public finance towards sustainable objectives (USD 1.5 billion over 20 years). | - |
| The project focuses on one of Mexico’s poorest and most vulnerable regions, the population of which includes Indigenous Peoples. | - |

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal in accordance with the term sheet agreed between the Secretariat and the accredited entity, and if considered appropriate, subject to the conditions set out in Annex II of document GCF/JL31/02.

### 2.1 Project background


---
**Page 105**
---

The Basin in Mexico, a region highly vulnerable to climate change, faces exacerbated challenges as one of the country’s poorest and most marginalized areas. Climate effects such as increased temperatures, erratic rainfall, and extended dry seasons are reducing agricultural productivity, increasing food insecurity, and leading to more frequent and severe flooding, crop damage and droughts, further reducing crop yields. Local efforts to combat these issues are hindered by inadequate capacities and institutional frameworks that fail to prioritize climate adaptation.

The cross-cutting project (78 per cent adaption, 22 per cent mitigation) aims to increase the resilience of communities in the Basin to climate change impacts on ecosystems, and to reduce emissions from production systems in the Basaltas project area (see below).

(a) Operationalizing the use of climate change criteria in current institutional modalities for the generation of rural development incentives through a community-centric bottom-up approach;

(b) Investing in the climate-sensitive management of ecosystems and climate-resilient productivity enhancement, including adaptation and mitigation measures; and

(c) Supporting a climate information and risk management system, and the systematic management of knowledge and lessons learned.

The total project cost is requested at USD 95,000,000, of which GCF investment is requested of USD 28,000,000 in loans and USD 13,750,000 in grants. 

The project is expected to achieve a mitigation outcome of 3,926,876 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e) by 2030, which will benefit approximately 4 million people, including 201,878 (0.5 per cent of the population of prioritized waterheds and indigenous territories) as direct beneficiaries.

GCF rates the programme as category B [medium] for environmental and social safeguards.


### Component-by-component analysis

The project is split into three components leading to the following outcomes:

(a) Outcome 1: Government programmes and community development plans integrate climate-change criteria for the allocation of support;

(b) Outcome 2: Forest management units are more resilient and better adapted to climate change for the conservation of ecosystems, biodiversity, and human well-being; and

(c) Outcome 3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Basaltas project area receive timely and relevant climate information for climate risk management, disaster prevention, and knowledge management.

Component 1: Operationalization of a system that prioritizes sites, beneficiaries and incentives for work on climate change criteria in current institutional modalities using climate change criteria (Total cost: USD 10,891,726; GCF loan: USD 4,032,991 in loans and USD 1,524,045 in grants).

The outcome of component 1 is that government programmes and community development plans integrate climate-change criteria for the allocation of support.

The activities of component 1 are:

(a) Activity 1.1.1: Facilitate the creation of forest management units and regional committees for the integrated management of the territory.


---
**Page 106**
---

(b) Activity 1.1.2. Agree on priority intervention areas at the micro-basin level;
(c) Activity 1.1.3. Strengthen multi-sectorial coordination structures, operation rules, and a mechanism for co-investment within the basin;
(d) Activity 1.1.4. Strengthen and operationalize criteria for adaptation to climate change in institutional programme procedures;
(e) Activity 1.2. Launch an awareness campaign to disseminate calls for proposals;
(f) Activity 1.3.2. Formulate climate-smart multi-annual investment plans based on micro-basin level plans and validated through free, prior and informed consent;
(g) Activity 1.3.1. Implement capacity development programmes for extension workers, local facilitators and "brigades";
(h) Activity 1.3.4. Develop and create forest protection brigades and local climate change resilience facilitator; and
(i) Activity 1.3.5. Strengthen the Promotoras forestales (regional and local offices) of CONAFOR in the municipalities of Mexico.

Component 2: Climate change prioritization criteria applied to the allocation of rural development incentives for the climate-sensitive management of ecosystems, and climate-resilient systems and value chains in the Basin, with a focus on agroforestry and forest landscapes.

15. The outcome of component 2 is that forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change.
(a) Activity 2.1.1. Payment of environmental services;
(b) Activity 2.2.1. Climate-smart forest landscape restoration of micro-watersheds and strategic areas;
(c) Activity 2.2.2. Commercial forest and agroforestry plantations adapted to climate change;
(d) Activity 2.3.1. Sustainable forest management adapted to climate change, inclusion and accessibility;
(e) Activity 2.3.2. Support households to improve food security/nutrition; and
(f) Activity 2.3.3. Brigades to strengthen adaptation capacity in agrarian communities (Component 2).

Component 3: Development of a climate information system, and implementation of knowledge management (USD 3,469,721 GCF cost USD 3,208,721 in grant)

15. The outcome of this component is that stakeholders in the Basin receive the required information to make informed decisions on adaptation and disaster prevention in a timely manner.
(a) Activity 3.1.1. Development of a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information for basin communities;
(b) Activity 3.1.2. Strengthening the CONAFOR prediction and prevention systems for fire and forest phytophysical risks; and
(c) Activity 3.1.3. Strengthening the CONAFOR measurement, reporting and verification system.


---

## Assessment of performance against investment criteria

16\. The programme scores well against GCF investment criteria. A detailed assessment of the programme’s fit with GCF investment criteria is provided below.

### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

17\. The impact potential is expected to be medium to high, as the number of direct beneficiaries totals 201,867 (members of agrarian communities, small producers, and Indigenous peoples) in 45 municipalities across Mexico. This represents a reduction in emissions with 3,924,874 Mt CO₂ over 20 years will be achieved from the reduced degradation and therefore enhanced carbon sequestration potential.

18\. Direct beneficiaries of the project are defined as small producers of agrarian communities from 121 of the most socially and climatically vulnerable municipalities in the BALSAS project area. Indirect beneficiaries include 3,540 individuals who are not direct beneficiaries but are members of agrarian communities targeted by the project. The indirect beneficiaries are assessed with a focus on those impacted by land use over change and value chain innovations, such as agroforestry systems, as well as those involved in community forestry and brigades across all components.

19\. Indirect beneficiaries of the project include 3,540 individuals, are defined as members of agrarian communities who are not direct beneficiaries, but are community members from the 68 agrarian communities targeted. Indirect beneficiaries were primarily estimated as a proportion of the total number of direct beneficiaries (27 per cent). Beneficiaries in component 2 are scheduled to occur. Beneficiaries of early warning systems (component 3) correspond to the population living within the project area, which is expected to largely overlap with the priority watersheds; they are not double counted in the total of indirect beneficiaries, reflecting a conservative approach.

20\. With direct beneficiaries (84 per cent), and cross-cutting program also includes a mitigation component (22 per cent). The project will contribute to Mexico’s 2030 national target of reducing greenhouse gas emissions by 52 per cent. This is achieved through afforestation, reforestation and forest, farmland, and pasture management (land use, land-use change and forestry sector); and agricultural soil management activities and programmed soil burns (agriculture sector).

### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

21\. The programme has the potential to be scaled up by enhancing public finance flows aligned with GCF investment criteria. This will be achieved through improved institutional coordination, knowledge and data collection, and skill development. It is estimated that


---
**Page 108**
---

average of $160 million per year in traditional forestry finance provided by CONAFOR - totaling more than USD 1.5 billion (net present value) over the project's 20-year lifespan - will be redirected towards climate change adaptation and mitigation finance to benefit Mexico’s most vulnerable communities.

The project also has high potential for replication across the country through the operationalization of climate change criteria in the prioritization of rural development incentives, as well as by leveraging existing national financial mechanisms or other sources of national development finance towards climate finance, as mentioned above.


### 3.3 Sustainable development potential

**Scale: High**

The programme contributes to key Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs): SDG 13 (Climate action), SDG 1 (No poverty), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 3 (Good health and well-being), SDG 5 (Gender equality), SDG 6 (Clean water and sanitation), and SDG 15 (Life on land).


### 3.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scale: Medium to high**

The population of 0.8 million people living in the BAJAS project area is extremely vulnerable to climate change impacts due to considerably higher poverty rates and lower social development levels compared to the rest of Mexico. Inequities affecting women’s access to services are also a major concern, with only 25 per cent of women having formal employment; 70 per cent of the population, mainly young people, lack economic opportunities and community decision-making influence; and 18 per cent live in extreme poverty. Additionally, there are over one million Indigenous People living in poverty in the municipalities are particularly vulnerable.


### 3.5 Country ownership

**Scale: High**

This project is highlighted as the top priority in Mexico’s pipeline by its national designated authority, the Mexican Ministry of Finance and Public Credit (Secretaría de Hacienda y Crédito Público), referred to as Hacienda, and has been developed in collaboration with multiple institutions and stakeholders under the lead of CONAFOR and the guidance of the

# Efficiency and effectiveness: Scale: High

## Project cost and economic analysis

The estimated total cost of the project is USD 91 million, of which USD 73.5 million will be used in investment components in 2024.

### Project cost recovery through mobilization of USD 1.5 billion over 20 years if scaled up across Mexico

The economic analysis and sensitivity analysis shows that this project is economically viable as a whole. The project has the potential to generate quantifiable benefits from its investments in component 2, and include the increase in productivity, resilience of the productive systems to climate change, and mitigation of greenhouse gas emissions.

### Economic results at the aggregate level

The economic results at the aggregate level of the project are estimated based on the number of agrarian communities and producer groups to be covered by the project. This step also includes the calculation of carbon sequestration and avoided deforestation, as well as emission reductions estimated with the EX-Ante Carbon Balance Tool (EX-ACT) for the different models.

### Variations in social carbon prices

With various scenarios of social carbon prices and discount rate, the economic internal rate of return of the project reaches a value between 16.6 per cent and 34.6 per cent and a positive economic net present value between USD 25 million and USD 216 million.

## Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

### Environmental and social safeguards

#### Project background
The project aims to increase the climate resilience of the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems and reduce emissions from production systems in the BAUMA region. The project will be implemented through a combination of community-based initiatives, through the climate-focused transformation of rural development incentives for the conservation of forest resources and strengthening of institutions and capacities.

#### Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) category and risk assessment
The accredited entity (AE) has assigned an E&S risk category B to the project, given that the project will not be involved in actions and investments for the development or expansion of new agricultural activities in environmentally sensitive areas and will not encourage the conversion of wetlands, changes in hydrology or the introduction of exotic species. The AE confirms that it is within the E&S risk category that the AE is accredited for. The environmental and social management framework (ESMF) has been prepared, which indicates the processes and guidelines for the preparation, implementation, monitoring, and evaluation of the project.


---
**Page 110**
---

implementation and supervision of E&S risk instruments designed for the project and its subprojects.

### Compliance with GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Standards

The following paragraphs describe how the project complies with the standards.

#### ESS1: Environmental and social risks and impacts. The environmental and social risks and impacts under the project are expected to be of moderate scale and can be readily managed and mitigated through the deployment of appropriate measures. These include, but not limited to, the implementation of capacity-building, environmental restoration, and targeted agricultural/agroforestry activities; there are no significant risks associated with land acquisition or resettlement due to water contamination, or on labour and working conditions. The ESMS follows the management procedures for assessing E&S risks and impacts as described in the GCF’s Environmental and Social Safeguards derived from the execution of the BALAS project. The ESMS will be updated during the design stage of the project with the latest information when establishing the details of the exact locations and the potentially affected peoples. The ESMS establishes the principles, rules, guidelines and procedures for assessing the E&S risk and impacts under the project.

#### ESS2: Labour and working conditions. The activities of the project will mostly be implemented in rural areas where there are no significant risks to indigenous communities that are present within the locality. It is expected that the labour of migrants or temporary workers will not be required. Some of the low-risk activities in the forest areas include harvesting, pruning, and thinning of trees. These activities may expose workers to exposure to high temperatures, accidents involving forest machinery and exposure to biological hazards (e.g., snake bites, stings from insects, and diseases such as malaria and tropical diseases). The project will provide occupational health and safety training and promote the use of a safety equipment. The project will also ensure that all workers are covered by national and regional labour regulations, including the ILO and GCF requirements, to be applied with these regulations.

#### ESS3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention. The project intends to promote measures to reduce pollution and increase the sustainable-use of resources, given that the project is expected to generate a significant amount of waste (e.g., packaging materials). Activities could lead to generation of liquid and solid waste as a result of agricultural production and processing activities (e.g., fruit peels, vegetable peelings, and other by-products from packing and processing waste, packaging material) that could pollute land and water resources. Land and water contamination may also occur due to the use of fuels and lubricants in machinery or the use of pesticides and herbicides. The project will promote the adoption of practices that support the implementation of agroecological best practices such as composting, mulching, recycling packaging materials, and reducing the use of chemical fertilizers and pesticides. Farmers will also be trained on the safe use and handling of agrochemicals. The project will also promote the use of organic fertilizers and other natural resources that could reduce runoff which could enhance infiltration, reduce soil erosion, improve water quality and minimize siting of waste bodies.

#### ESS4: Community health, safety and security. The ES has conducted consultations at the design stage, and the risk of conflict among the targeted communities on the use of natural resources is considered low due to the existence of a land-use plan that defines the boundaries of the free, prior and informed consent plan and by ensuring that meaningful and continuous consultation with the affected communities will be carried out. The ES has also identified the risk of the presence of organized crime in the project region, which may affect project implementation and/or put project personnel at risk. The project plans to mitigate this by recruiting additional facilitators in the field who will be made aware of security protocols and


---
**Page 111**
---

## ESSS: Land acquisition and involuntary resettlement

The project will exclude any activity requiring land acquisition. The exclusion list also includes "Activities that imply the economic or social displacement of communities or individuals, unless it is demonstrated that there is no risk of potential restriction of traditional access to land/livelihoods due to project activities."

## ESSS: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project seeks to improve the provision of ecosystem services, such as water capture, flood control, soil retention, carbon sequestration, and climate regulation. To ensure environmental restoration and promote biodiversity, some activities have the risk of introducing invasive species. The ESSS exclusion list includes "Activities that are not consistent with the goals and intent of CONAMOR supported projects." Unwarranted introduction will lead to the suppression of native species.

## ESSS: Indigenous Peoples and Afro-Indigenous Peoples Policies

The project sets a target of 50 per cent of direct beneficiaries being Indigenous peoples; thus, the project has potential impacts on Indigenous peoples. The project is designed to ensure that the rights of Indigenous Peoples and Afro-Mexican communities are mainstreamed throughout the project's ESSS, including through the provision of technical support for the development of a community-based action plan (CBAP) through the provision of technical support, demand-driven incentive payments and strengthening of interinstitutional coordination. The AE has undertaken meaningful consultations at the design phase involving Indigenous peoples and Afro-Mexican communities. The Indigenous Peoples Advisory Group is available to provide advice to the AE and executing entities on how best to mainstream Indigenous peoples' rights in project implementation.

### ESSS: Cultural heritage

The project is not expected to directly affect cultural heritage. The ESM's exclusion list states that the project will not influence "Activities that involve directly or indirectly the conversion, loss of major or minor adverse impacts on the physical and/or spiritual/religious archaeological sites and monuments affecting the local, rural and indigenous population."

## Sexual exploitation, sexual abuse and sexual harassment (SEAH) safeguarding

The project is expected to potentially face high SEAH risks and impacts given the existing prevalence of gender-based violence (GBV) and social conflicts linked to non-comprehensive access to land and natural resources. The project will mainstream opportunities in protected natural areas, which disproportionately affect rural women, youth and Indigenous peoples. The AE will ensure that the project promotes gender equality and good governance and the participation of women, youth and Indigenous Peoples at the sub-basin level. The AE will also ensure community awareness, capacity building, and community health and safety to prevent and mitigate GBV and SEAH-related risks and impacts related to the project. Moreover, the AE will further identify and assess specific GBV and SEAH risks as part of the risk assessment process. The AE will ensure that a GBV and SEAH risk assessment is conducted at the community level. Detailed SEAH risk assessments and relevant mitigation measures will be included in the project's gender equality action plan and the environmental management plan. The AE will ensure the safety and security of women’s groups in the target communities, including through the provision of training on GBV and SEAH prevention and the participation of women in the project. The AE will also enhance community awareness, capacity building, and community health and safety to prevent and mitigate GBV and SEAH risks and proactively map the existence of and gaps in services for survivors/affected people. In the case of an GBV incident, the project will ensure that the AE provides immediate support to affected women. All that include specific SEAH-related procedures to provide essential services and redress to

# Implementation arrangements

CINAFOR as the ES will have the overall operational responsibility for implementing the project. A project coordination unit will be established, based at CINAFOR's headquarters, which will be responsible for day-to-day project management and monitoring of project implementation, including financial performance and social performance. The unit will coordinate the screening of subprojects, assessment and management of risks, and the development of a communication plan to promote awareness, capacity-building and disclosure of information. The AE will also provide training to the relevant staff on safeguards-related topics when the project is launched and will continue to provide technical assistance as needed.

## Stakeholder engagement and information disclosure

In preparation for the project, the HUD will engage with key stakeholders including national ministries, regional organizations, multilateral bodies, Indigenous Peoples, as well as local government organizations and the private sector. A stakeholder engagement plan outlines the processes that will be used to identify and engage these stakeholders, particularly including disadvantaged and vulnerable groups, during project implementation. Engagement approaches will include public consultations, workshops, distribution of accessible materials, outreach on project activities and local radio, among others.

### Risks

#### 4.3 Overall program assessment (medium risk)

10. This funding proposal is a cross-cutting project (78 per cent adaptation and 22 per cent mitigation) to strengthen the climate resilience of vulnerable communities and reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation in Mexico. The project will implement a climate-sensitive ecosystem management system, a climate-resilient productive system, and a climate-smart value chain for timber products. The total cost is US$105 million with a grant of US$20 million and a grant of US$9.5 million together with financing of US$5.5 million in loans and grants from the World Bank and government of Mexico. The co-financing ratio is 1:3 and accounts for 57 per cent of total financing.

#### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (low risk)

10. IFAD is an international AE with over 40 years of development work in global agriculture, rural development, and food security. IFAD provides concessional loans and low-interest rate loans to fund livelihoods in fragile and developing countries. The project's BEs are HACIENDA, NAIFIN, and CONAFOR. The Ministry of Finance and Public Credit will sign the subsidiary loan agreement with IFAD, and the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources will sign the environmental impact for project execution will rest with CONAFOR, which also manages the country’s National Forest Fund. Since 2013, IFAD has been a key partner in Mexico to support the implementation of internationally funded forestry projects and programmes. CONAFOR has worked with IFAD for over 15 years, including on the implementation of loans and grants for forest areas and vulnerability.

#### Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

17. In addition to the three BEs mentioned above (HACIENDA, NAIFIN and CONAFOR), decisions are overseen by a steering committee that reports to political decision makers who approve project funding. The steering committee is chaired by the IFAD representative and steering committee members. Under the steering committee are two layers of technical coordination teams and implementation committees. At the top are the national implementing partners and local co-implementing partners, and at the bottom are the grass-roots organizations, communities, and forestry enterprises. Given the multilayered and cross-sectorial arrangements, there is a risk that project execution may be delayed or not executed as planned. This is a concern. This risk is partly mitigated by the project’s initiative to strengthen intersectoral coordination and regular participation of all stakeholders in the steering committee meetings. The Office for Coordination of Affairs and HACIENDA. The funding proposal presents low credit risk given that the project will be implemented with concessional loans, and that most of the funding will be denominated in United States dollars to fund local currency generating activities, foreign exchange risk is mitigated by the IFAD’s investment grade rating. With regard to the risk of local currency devaluation after disbursement affecting the project budget, this will be managed by planning currency conversion as close as possible to expected disbursement.


---
**Page 114**
---

### Project viability and concessionality

The proposed project will develop plans to integrate climate change into government programmes, enhance forest capacity and strengthen coordination. Other components will enhance sustainable forest landscapes while enabling timely climate information availability to manage climate risks.

### Compliance risk (medium risk)

Mexico faces a number of challenges in terms of internal conflict and social unrest. This is largely due to the presence of drug cartels and criminal gang violence, including in the targeted region. The proposal by the AE (IFAD) does address this issue and sets out measures to address it.

### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of results area and single proposal is not material.

### Results monitoring and reporting

#### 4.5 Cross-cutting initiatives: This project aims to generate results under GCF mitigation results areas 1 and land degradation results area 2, 1-6. It has an implementation period of eight years (six years under GCF funding) and a lifespan of 20 years. During implementation, it is expected that the project will achieve 35,784 ha of forest restoration (95% in designated riparian watersheds) and 110,734 indirect beneficiaries (9.25 per cent of the project area population). The estimated total mitigation impact is 6,358,000 tCO₂ eq.

The Secretariat worked with the AE over five rounds of review to improve results management under the project. This included refining the project theory of change to make the intended co-benefits more explicit and to better reflect the expected distribution and allocation of the co-benefits expected under the project, as well as the underlying risks and barriers.

The logical framework has also been refined to align with the GCF Integrated Results Management Framework. The revised framework includes a new set of indicators that have identified suitable paradigm shift and GCF outcome indicators. Moreover, project-level outcome and output indicators are now aligned with the GCF integrated results management framework feedback on formatting GCF core and supplementary indicators to ensure smooth reporting and results aggregation at the Secretariat level.

The Secretariat will provide the AE with feedback on various reporting milestones listed in annex 5 (Implementation timetable) to bring it in alignment with GCF recommended standards.


### Legal assessment

#### 7.0 The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 24 September 2016 and became effective on 9 November 2018. The Accredited Entity's five-year accreditation term lapse on 8 November 2023 but was extended by Decision B.37/1R, paragraph (q) until the earlier of three years from the date of lapse of the five-year accreditation term or completion of the project, whichever is sooner, subject to approval by the Board. The Board approved the re-accreditation of the Accredited Entity pursuant to Decision B.37/1R and negotiation of the Amended and Restated Accreditation Master Agreement is ongoing.

#### 72.0 The Accredited Entity has not provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.


---
**Page 116**
---

70\. The proposed project will be implemented in the United Mexican States ("Mexico"), a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be managed by the Accredited Entity.

71\. The Government of Mexico has recently provided a letter of credit for the project and immunities and a background note to the Government of Mexico most recently on 24 January 2023.

72\. The Blinds of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities in Mexico, as Mexico is not a party to the Convention on the Settlement of Investment Disputes with Relevant Privileges and Immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the project has been provided with appropriate protection against litigation and expropriation in this country, or have been provided with privileges and immunities.

73\. With regard to the activities being financed by the GCF/Amex, the Accredited Entity has proposed a direct contractual relationship between itself and the Ministry of Finance ("HACENIDA"), with HACENIDA subsequently entering into a mandate and implementation agreement as required under Mexican law with National Financiera, S.A., Banco de México, S.A. de C.V., and CONAFOR (the "Executing Entities"). The term sheet includes an assignment clause from HACENIDA to CONAFOR ("CONAFOR"), assigning NAFIN to act as intermediary on behalf of the Government of Mexico in relation to the project and providing for a direct contractual relationship with HACENIDA and coordination. Although it has been agreed that NAFIN and CONAFOR are Executing Entities, they will not be parties to any contract or agreement with the Accredited Entity.

74\. The Accredited Entity will not have a direct contractual relationship with NAFIN and CONAFOR which are acting as intermediaries on behalf of the Government of Mexico in relation to this project respectively. For the Accredited Entity to pass down and enforce the relevant requirements of the AMAs, it is necessary that HACENIDA assigns its rights under the term sheet to NAFIN and CONAFOR has agreed in the term sheet that it will ensure that HACENIDA fulfills all its obligations, supervises and enforces such requirements against NAFIN and CONAFOR in a manner that allows the Accredited Entity to pass down and enforce those requirements as if they were a direct contractual relationship with NAFIN and CONAFOR.

75\. Since the Accredited Entity's direct country is a sovereign state (as one of the Executing Entities), it would be difficult or impossible to disply the provisions of the AMAs allowing GCF to step in to the Accredited Entity's contractual arrangements with the Executing Entity, i.e., the Government of Mexico. As such, it is recommended that the terms and conditions set out in the AMAs are not enforceable against the Government of Mexico.

76\. It is therefore not feasible for the Accredited Entity to allow GCF or any other party to take over its current contractual relationship with NAFIN and CONAFOR as intermediaries on behalf of the Government of Mexico. Accordingly, and similar to the arrangements for PF143, PF182, PF183, PA102 and SA107/1, as a condition precedent to GCF providing project funding, it is recommended that the National Designated Authority (NDA) of the Host Country of this disapplication and its implications. The Accredited Entity will be responsible for the successful implementation of the proposed project and an express provision has been included in the term sheet to provide for such responsibility.

77\. To address the matters raised in this section, it is recommended that any approval by the Board of Directors of the GCF should include:

(a) Submission by the Accredited Entity to the Fund of a certificate or legal opinion, in form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat, within 120 days after Board approval granting funding for the project, confirming that all internal approvals needed by it and has the capacity and authority to implement the proposed project;

(b) Signature of the FAA in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 30 days after Board approval granting funding for the project, confirming that HACENIDA has provided a certificate or legal opinion confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals, whichever is later; and

# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP236

## Proposal name:
Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies (BALSAS)

## Accredited entity:
International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD)

## Country:
Mexico

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

This is the first submission of the funding proposal "Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies" to the International Fund for Agricultural Development's Independent Technical Advisory Panel (ITAP) under the standard proposal approval process. The accredited entity (AE) is the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD). The executing entities (EEs) are the National Commission for Forestry, Agriculture and Livestock (CONAFOR), the National Bank of Mexico (Banxico), Banca de Desarrollo (BANFIDEN), and the Ministry of Finance (HACIENDA).

The project aims to increase community resilience of the most vulnerable community and ecosystems, while reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emission from production systems (agriculture and forestry) in the Basin Mexico in 2014. In priorities eight weeks after the call for proposals, the ITAP considered the document as a grant and USD 20 million as a loan.

It is recommended that the ITAP consider the following points:

- The written answers to the ITAP and the further clarifications during the call with the AE on all 25 May, as well as minor revisions regarding the mitigation impact potential received on 23 May 2014.


### 1.1 Impact potential

#### Scale: High

##### Adaptation potential

6. There are four ecoregions in the project area: (i) the inter-montane depressions; (ii) the southern Pacific coastal plains and hill landscape; (iii) the temperate mountain ranges of the Sierra Madre Occidental; and (iv) the tropical lowlands of the Sierra Madre del Sur.

7. The funding proposal includes an analysis of climate change and variability for the 4 ecoregions, based on data from the period 1950–2016. The analysis compares temperature and precipitation data for 1979-1999 as a reference period, with data from the period 2000–2016. In order to contextualize the analysis, the observed climate change trends are compared with those projected by the representative atlas of the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change. Future climatic trends were considered for


---
**Page 119**
---

The period 2014–2020 and using Shared Socioeconomic Pathway (SSP) K5 and SSP-4.5. The funding proposal includes an analysis of how increasing climate hazards will affect the main land uses in each of the four ecoregions, which are agriculture, forestry and grasslands.

With regard to the level of exposure and the adaptive capacity of the population, the funding proposal considers that the most vulnerable groups are women and children due to low education level, poverty, gender and intergenerational aspects as well as food insecurity. These findings were based on a participatory approach with representatives from indigenous groups (indigenous women, women, Afro-Mexican communities, agricultural communities, etc.) Existing adaptation measures in place are not sufficient.

Considering future climate scenarios, current and future vulnerability of the productive systems and expected evolution of the vulnerability of the socioecosystemic society, the funding proposal considers that the capacity to adapt to climate change will increase if further adaptation measures are not implemented soon.

As a response to the increasing climate vulnerability, the funding proposal aims at achieving three main outcomes:

(a) Outcome 1: Government programmes and community development plans integrate climate change considerations. This will be achieved by strengthening intersectoral coordination, improve local capacity and facilitate the implementation of climate-resilient investment plans. Of the total budget 11 per cent has been allocated to this outcome.

(b) Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change, where forest responses to climate change will be more resilient if they are implemented. This includes the implementation of land-use plans through multilateral payments for environmental services (PES), climate-smart forest landscape, restoration and rehabilitation activities in degraded areas and agroforestry systems. This has potential to avoid the deforestation of 4652 hectares. This outcome will occur at multiple scales: from local communities to national level, including the region while reducing GHG emissions (mitigation impact). Further, the main systems for climate change adaptation are already in place or being implemented and future climate scenarios will contribute to increase the overall landscape resilience. The agroforestry and forest landscapes management system is a key component of this outcome aspect implementation and then assessed against future climate scenarios. These agroforestry and forestry systems will also respond to needs of the stakeholders related to food production, water supply, carbon sequestration and other ecosystem services. A set of schemes for payments for ecosystem services that already exist in Mexico to facilitate implementation of these activities are being considered by the project with local communities. In addition, activities included in this outcome will strengthen the participation of women and indigenous groups in decision-making processes as well as in monitoring activities. The implementation of these systems will also have a mitigation impact (mitigation potential assessment). Of the total budget 84 per cent has been allocated to this outcome.

(c) Outcome 3: Producers, inhabitants and authorities in the project area have timely and relevant information on climate change impacts and adaptation measures. This includes where the project will contribute to increase of local stakeholders to climate information systems (CIS). The CIS will provide them with a better capacity to prevent forest fires and pests; improve safeguard management; and promote knowledge management and exchange. Of the total budget 8 per cent is allocated to this outcome.

In several cases, the funding proposal includes an adaptation component that aims at understanding the impacts of climate hazards over time and monitoring the progress achieved through the adaptation measures. This includes the creation of forest protection brigades and

## Paradigm shift potential

### Scale: Medium to High

The BALASAS project can contribute to a paradigm shift towards resilient and sustainable land use in rural Mexico. First, the project will mainstream climate change adaptation and desertification risk reduction into the national forest policy framework. Second, PES climate vulnerability and adaptation were not systematically considered when preparing or implementing the Mexican Forest Law. The project will provide an opportunity for institutional coordination that this project will put on mainstreaming adaptation to climate change in the context of the new Forest Law. Third, the project will contribute to the resilience of Mexico’s forests by providing a model for integrating PES into forest management and facilitating an upscaling effect. However, this potential paradigm shift is limited by two factors: first, the capacity of the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources for PES in other regions; second, the fact that the current ongoing PES activities will not directly benefit from these innovations.

The success rate of mainstreaming adaptation to climate change in PES schemes in other countries depends on the specific scheme and on the regulatory framework of a given country. As GEF already co-finances other projects that mainstream adaptation into climate-resilient PES schemes (TAPF highly relevant), it is important to share knowledge of experiences between these projects, aimed at identifying success factors and common challenges that can be useful for this project.

Two additional features of the project can further contribute to a paradigm shift: the activities on community monitoring for adaptation, and the participation of agrarian communities in decision-making processes. Community-based forest management is key to climate-resilient forestry and agroforestry. Community monitoring can reduce the cost of adaptation while increasing its effectiveness by providing early warning signals about climate-related events. Participation in new value chains can increase farmers’ income, reduce their overall vulnerability, and provide them with a better livelihood through climate-resilient land-use options.

Considering the above, the ITAP assesses the paradigm shift potential of this funding proposal as medium to high due to the limiting factors explained in paragraph 18.


## Sustainable development potential

### Scale: High

In addition to its contribution to Sustainable Development Goal (SDG) 13 on climate action, implementation of the BALASAS project will directly contribute to achieving SDGs 1 and 5 poverty; SDG 2 zero hunger; SDG 5 gender equality; SDG 10 reduced inequalities; and SDG 15 life on land.

The project uses a basket approach aimed at diversifying farmers’ income by combining PES with income from climate resilient agricultural systems, timber and non-timber forest products. The production of timber and non-timber forest products is promoted in activities 2.2 to secure the production of timber and non-timber forest products and output 2.3 promotes the participation of women in decision-making processes related to forest management. This diversity of options will have a positive impact on improving income for rural livelihoods.

On the environmental side, the project will preserve and increase environmental services in the BALASAS watershed through climate-smart agriculture and resilient forestry and


---
**Page 122**
---

agroforestry systems, producing a positive impact on biodiversity, soil protection and water regulation services.

24. The project has a socially inclusive approach, with special consideration of the rights and benefits for women, Indigenous Peoples and youth. Along with this approach, several activities are proposed to promote the participation of Indigenous Peoples and youth, including: strengthening "promotorías forestales" (a mechanism for promoting forestry, agroforestry and reforestation projects); developing a participatory process for preparing multi-annual investment plans; implementing climate-smart agriculture, resilient plantations or agroforestry systems; and supporting the development of women's cooperatives.

25. The project team conducted multiple consultations during the design of the funding proposal in explicit alignment with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy adopted by decision B.17/11. The results from these consultations were used to inform the project design and serve as the basis for the Stakeholder Engagement Plan (annex 3) and the Gender, Social Inclusion and Action Plan (annex 8) aligned with the GCF Updated Gender Policy, adopted by decision B.24/12.

26. Considering the above, the ITAP assesses the sustainable development potential of this proposal as high.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

With a population of over 130 million, Mexico is among the 15 largest economies in the world, the second largest in Latin America, and belongs to the Organisation for Economic Co-operation and Development (OECD). However, despite its economic growth, Mexico's gross domestic product growth for 2024 is at 2.7% per year. However, a Gini index of 43.5 (2012) indicates a significant level of inequality.

Approximately one-third of the population in the project area suffers from poverty and exhibits low social development relative to the rest of the country. This is characterized by high levels of unemployment, limited access to education, housing, drinking water and basic sanitation, as well as limited income, access to decent employment, and a lack of productive options, causing overall vulnerability. In addition, the project area is suffering from the effects of climate change, which are exacerbated by the investment priorities of the government and migration patterns, especially for young people.

Gender inequality in Mexico is reflected in low levels of education, employment, credit and economic activities as well as lower representation in decision-making bodies, especially at community level. Consequently, women are even more vulnerable to poverty than men. According to the National Institute of Statistics and Geography (INEGI), participation of women in consultations for this funding proposal was far lower than that of men. This is a critical issue that must be addressed by the project team. The following highlights the importance of conducting monitoring of gender-related aspects during the implementation period, especially:

Indigenous and traditional peoples in Mexico have the lowest monetary income and access to services, and have high rates of food and nutritional insecurity. Seventy-five per cent of the indigenous population lives below the poverty line (INEGI). Indigenous people are concentrated four of which are included in the project intervention area: Oaxaca, State of Mexico, Puebla and Guerrero. The majority of the indigenous population is located in rural areas where poverty is concentrated in the states of Guerrero and Oaxaca. The funding proposal acknowledges this social diversity and includes multiple strategies for promoting the participation of Indigenous People and the Afro-Mexican population during the planning and implementation of the project.

1\. As highlighted in the funding proposal, there are several aspects that prevent tailored funding for climate change adaptation in the project area: (i) lack of specificity of the current financial support on the most vulnerable communities and ecosystems; (ii) lack of consideration of future needs and opportunities for sustainable development; (iii) limited focus on promoting ecosystem management; (iv) limited access to credit for promoting the inclusion of small producers in value chains related to timber, non-timber and agroforestry products; (v) lack of inclusion of indigenous peoples and local communities in value chains related to timber, non-timber and agroforestry products; (vi) lack of inclusion of women in forest management and value chain activities; and (vii) current low-level contribution to the maintenance of the relevant ecosystems despite the efforts of the Government of Mexico.

2\. Considering all of the above, the ITAP response is the response to the needs of the recipient described above as high.


### 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

3\. The funding proposal includes a no objection letter signed 2 March 2022 by the Director General of the national designated authority Mexico, Laura Elena Aguirre Téllez. It also includes a detailed description of the alignment of the project with existing policies, prior programmes and projects, as well as with other relevant international commitments and contributions (2022 update), the National Climate Change Strategy (2013), the Special Programme for Climate Resilience (SPCR) (2015), the National Forest Policy 2020–2024, the General Law on Climate Change (2012), and the most recent national forest inventory.

4\. IPAF's first accreditation term by GCF lasted from 9 November 2018 to 6 November 2023; the re-accreditation date was 25 October 2023, but the execution date of the accreditation margin is 1 January 2024.

5\. The funding proposal mentions three EEs: HACIENDA, NAIFN and CONAFOR.


6\. HACIENDA is the political and operational government focal point for international climate change projects. It has responsibility for the budget and expenditures for implementing public programmes, including those related to climate.

7\. NAIFN is an accredited national direct access entity based in Mexico. It will act as the fiduciary agent of the project. The funds will be transferred directly from GCF to NAIFN; CONAFOR cannot directly receive external loan financing. As such, NAIFN will be its EE, but it will not implement any activity.

8\. The Secretary of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT) has been designated to be formally responsible for implementing the project, and has delegated responsibility to HACIENDA. It is also the entity that will provide technical support to SEMARNAT responsible for implementing the country's public policy on forestry and with extensive experience in operating projects at international level.


9\. (Note: NAIFN will play a central role during the implementation of the project. The Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations (FAO) in Mexico will act as advisory body to NAIFN, providing technical support for the project. It is also an implementation partner; and the regional committees for integrated management of the territory (RCIMT) will act as multistakeholder platforms for regional coordination, transparency and accountability. The RCIMTs are responsible for the implementation of each of the above-mentioned institutions [see main document table 4 and figure 13]).


10\. As part of the project preparation phase, several stakeholder consultations were conducted as input for this funding proposal. Furthermore, the project will be implemented using integrative and participatory mechanisms.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to High

#### (c) GCF financing for the project is made up of loans worth USD 20 million, which consist of:
- USD 10 million loan with high concessionality (GCF Loan A)
- a USD 10 million loan with low concessionality (GCF Loan B), and
- a USD 19.5 million grant.

The total co-financing is USD 5.5 million from the World Bank, including a USD 3.75 million loan from IAFD and two grants from the Government of Mexico, one for USD 7 million through CONAFOR and the second grant of USD 6 million through GONAMIA.

#### (d) HACINNDA will be responsible for channeling the GCF proceeds to the EEs:

- (a) For the subsidiary loans: HACINNDA will subsequently enter into a Mandate and Implementation Agreement to assign:

- (b) For the GCF grant: Under both subsidiary agreements, CONAFOR will undertake all operational responsibility for implementation of the project in the country (see Figure 1).

# Executive summary

The Gender Assessment takes into account GCF key questions for the development of a gender analysis/assessment and, at this stage, is based on information from databases and national documents, mostly produced by the Mexican Government, and studies carried out by international research agencies.

The Government of Mexico has adopted international instruments on the human rights of women and together with its Member States has legislated extensively and developed institutions, policies and strategies in favor of rural women.

In the Mexican countryside and especially in the most remote areas, women are more affected by poverty than men. (55.8% and 46.7% respectively). The situation is even more adverse for indigenous women living in rural areas where it falls to 10 out of 10 in poverty and 4 out of 10 in extreme poverty.

For centuries, stereotypes have been constructed in Mexican culture (as in other contexts) that characterize men as providers of the household, heads of the family and, to a certain extent, decision-makers. Women are characterized as homemakers, caregivers and mothers who are in a position to control and manage the economic and technological resources to which the exercise of power, both public and private, is strictly linked. In contrast, women's work within the household is considered less important than men's work outside it, whether economically or socially. Gender stereotypes and machineries are determining factors in women's living conditions and their position as workers and employees.

Gender inequalities exist with respect to access to education and information, as well as long working hours, lack of recognition of their role in agriculture, little representation in decision-making positions, limited access to land and services for production in the context of climate change, are gaps that women face. The gender gap in terms of access to education is 10% in favor of men; in the last 12 months according to the National Survey on the Dynamics of Relationships in Households (ENAGRI) conducted by INEGI, 85.3% of women have completed secondary school or more, but in the state of Guerrero literacy is 13.6% and in Oaxaca 13.3% followed by Michoacán and Puebla is 8.3% in both cases*. In Mexico women are connected to the internet an average per week less than men, but in the State of Oaxaca, the difference raises to four a hours*.

On average, women work 6.2 hours more than men because of unpaid household work. In the project area, the gender gap is 10% and in the state of Guerrero it is 35%. The gender gap is -9.8 and -9.9 hours respectively*. On the other hand, as for the labor force employed in agricultural work, 56% of men and only 29% of women are paid for their work*.

**Page 132**
---

data from the National Agrarian Registry (RAN), women represent only 26% of landowners in the project area.

Women hold much fewer* decision-making positions in ejidos and communities. At the national level of ejido public decision makers are women, but in the project area, women account for only 6.3 percent of ejido commissariat presidencies. This figure goes down to 7.4% in Puebla and 2.8% in the State of Guerrero.

Only one third of women in agriculture are paid for their work and they have less access to credit, inputs, extension services, and land tenure security than men. The new law (Ley de Ejidos) incorporates an additional paragraph in article 37, which refers to “Candidates for elected posts that represent the ejido community must be women.” This is a positive step but it does not require more than sixty percent of candidates of the same sex and may aspire to any of the posts indiscriminately. For the commissions and auxiliary secretaries of the ejidal commissariat, the integration of women will be voluntary.

In addition to the above, women are recognized to be more vulnerable to the effects of climate change than men. Household activities – for which women are largely responsible – are becoming increasingly difficult due to changes in weather patterns such as increased rainfall and water, increasing challenges related to washing, cleaning and cooking. The increasing occurrence of pathogenic diseases and the limited availability of food for the family also add to women’s psychological stress. Women are also more vulnerable than men to the effects of climate change on activities such as harvesting of non-timber forest products, which are also often carried out by women, are affected by changes in weather patterns. The lack of access to land and credit for women, due to the labour migration of men, but the lack of recognition of their labour and land ownership, as well as the usual inequity in access to services and goods for production and resilience to climate change, affect women’s performance and increase their vulnerability.

# Introduction

1. Mexico has legislated extensively in favor of women and has been vigilant in its compliance through institutional programs and initiatives at the federal and member state levels. It has ratified several international instruments on gender equality, among them the United Nations Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) and the Inter-American Convention to Prevent, Punish, and Eradicate Violence against Women (better known as Belem do Pará), ratified in 1986 – both in force in accordance with the provisions of Constitutional Article 130. At the national level there is a National Program for Equality between Men and Women (Programa Nacional de Igualdad entre hombres y mujeres) from the Political Constitution of the Mexican States, The Official Journal of the Federation, National Development Plan 2019-2024, and the General Law on the Elimination of Violence against Women. This law entered into force in 2008 as part of the General Law on Access to a Life Free of Violence for Women, which also includes provisions related to Human Trafficking and for the Protection and Assistance to Victims of these Crimes (see the General Law to Prevent and Eliminate Discrimination (2013) [5].

2. The National Development Plan 2019-2024 has the slogan “leave no one behind,” leave no one behind” in reference to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The Plan refers to the government’s work as a promoter of “equality as a guiding principle: effective equality of rights between women and men, between indigenous and mestizos, between young people and adults, among others.” It also states that “the country has been characterized by perpetuating the oppression of entire population sectors” [1]. Within the framework of this Plan, the National Program for Equality between Men and Women is one of the four programs built for the period 2020-2024. This is the special program of the Government that contains the main actions to be implemented in order to achieve equality between men and women. The Administration must implement with the support of 3 Constitutional Bodies and 2 Powers of the Union to guarantee the rights of women and reduce violence against them, by addressing six strategic axes: (1) gender equality; (2) access to justice for women; (3) full and effective participation of women in all areas and the construction of safe and peaceful environments. Among these actions is the Gender Diagnosis, which aims at identifying gaps that hinder or contribute to the promotion of actions that facilitate access to credits, financing, subsidies or international cooperation for women producers in situations of marginalization or vulnerability conditions; and to strengthen business coordination, of the productive chain led by women.

3. The Gender Assessment aims to conduct a contextualized and comprehensive review of the gender gap and gaps faced by women in Mexico, as well as identify opportunities to close these gaps. This evaluation will be used to prepare the Project “BALSAS: Basin Approach for the Sustainability of Livelihoods through Adaptation Strategies.”

4. The Gender Assessment aims at addressing gender inequalities and identify opportunities to reduce them, with a focus on the productive chain led by women. In addition to the Gender Diagnosis, a Gender Action Plan will provide detailed timelines which will ensure

## Part I: Gender Assessment

6. The Gender Assessment takes into consideration GCF key questions for the development of this gender assessment and is at this stage based on a literature review of national and international sources.

The information collected and analysed is organised according to the following aspects:
i) Poverty situation analysis,
ii) vital statistics,
iii) legal status of women,
iv) political participation,
v) access to land, credit and finance,
vi) beliefs, perceptions and common stereotypes related to gender,
vii) access to information and communication technologies and
viii) gender inequalities and climate change.

### Poverty situation

8. In 2018, poverty and extreme poverty affected 41.9% (52.4 million) and 7.4% (9.3 million) of the total population, respectively. In rural areas, where a quarter of Mexicans live, poverty affects almost half of the population (46%). The proportion of people living in poverty significantly increased by 8 percentage points between 1995 and 2018. Extreme poverty has decreased by almost 11 percentage points between 2008 and 2018 (27.1% and 16.4%, respectively). Despite this reduction, the proportion of people living in extreme poverty is still higher than in urban areas.

### Economic growth

9. Economic growth has not been inclusive in its benefits, and reducing disparities remains a key challenge for the country's development policy. 85% of women living in rural areas of the country are highly marginalized[14]. In the Mexican countryside, women are more affected by poverty than men (37% vs. 29%). Women from indigenous communities and Afro-Mexican population have historically had the highest poverty rates and continue to be a highly vulnerable population due to their unequal situation. According to CONEVAL in 2018, nearly half of the rural population is poor[15]. The extreme poverty rate among women living in rural areas is 34%, compared with 26% for men. Women are more likely than men to be in a situation of economic poverty and 28%, 3.4 million people suffered from a situation of extreme poverty, which is even more pronounced in indigenous women living in rural areas where 8 out of 10 live in poverty and 1 out of 10 is extreme poverty[16].
