10. Maternal mortality: Since the start of the pandemic to date, more than 790 pregnant women have died from COVID-19 in the country, the second worst figure in the Americas, only behind Brazil. This is a 25% increase compared with the same period last year and the leading cause of maternal death in 2021. Other causes were directly related to uncontrolled conditions during pregnancy due to poor availability of health care. Edema, hypertensive disorders, and hemorrhage are among the most common causes of maternal mortality in Mexico's population group in 2021. Prior to the pandemic, in 2019, this rate stood at 31 per 100,000 births, which is below the target set by the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) for 2030.

11. Infant mortality: Third Government Report of the Federal Executive estimates infant mortality for the year 2021 at 14.4, a figure similar to those of 2013 and higher than those recorded between 2014 and 2020[1].

12. Life expectancy: For 2019 the average life expectancy of the population was 75 years, being longer for women than for men. Women live an average of 78 years while men live an average of 72 years[1].

13. Child marriage: According to the United Nations Population Fund and the 2009 National Survey of Fertility Dynamics (ENADIF 2009), Mexico's child marriage rate is among the highest in Latin America, with the Caribbean. Two out of every 25 women aged 20-24 were married or entered into an informal union before the age of 18. This has not changed in almost 30 years[2]. Child marriage is more common among indigenous and Afro-descendant women, as well as among rural population in all 14 states[3]. The rate of child marriage among women who have only completed primary school ranges from 65.0% to 77.9%.

### Legal status of women

14. In 1974, the Congress of the Union approved the reform of Article 4 of the Constitution, which established legal equality between men and women. There are also two main laws that protect the integrity of women: i) General Law for Equality Between Women and Men, published in the Official Gazette on June 20, 1985; ii) General Law for the Elimination of Violence against Women, published in the Official Gazette on July 30, 1994. These laws aim to protect women from violence and to propose institutional guidelines and mechanisms to guide the nation towards the fulfillment of substantive equality in the public and private spheres, promoting the empowerment of women as a fundamental right for the development of Mexico[4]. The law is enforced through the National Commission on Women (CONAMUJER), which promotes its observance throughout the national territory[5]. ii) The General Law on Women's Access to Life Free from Violence. This law was published in the Official Gazette on July 30, 2010 and entered into force on August 1 of that year. It is enforced by the National Commission for the Prevention and Eradication of Gender-Based Violence (CONAPRED), the federal entities, the territorial districts of Mexico City and Transmunicipalities to prevent, punish and eradicate violence against women, adolescents and girls, as well as

to establish the principles and mechanisms for full access to a life free of violence, and to guarantee the free exercise of their human rights and within a theocratic regime established by law.

15. In the agrarian sector, the General Law of Agrarian Reform was enacted in 1971 and recognized the possibility for women to hold agrarian rights on an equal footing with men. Article 200 of this law establishes that "Mexicans by birth, men or women over 16 years of age or who have reached majority, are entitled to participate as electors in ejido assemblies." This article also guarantees women to participate in ejido assemblies, and Article 78 guarantees that women do not lose their agrarian rights when they marry. In the context of the reform of the General Law of Agrarian Reform in 2012, it repealed the Federal Agrarian Reform Law and changed its name to the current Agrarian Law. The law also established that "Mexicans by birth or naturalized, men or women over 18 years old to be a Mexican citizen of legal age or if any he or she has a dependent family or is the heir of an epistático. However, no explicit reference are made to gender and recognizes that women can hold agrarian rights on equal footing with men."

In 2017, a disapproved reforming Article 71 and adding an eighth paragraph to Article 37 of the General Law of Agrarian Reform, which established the creation of the Women's IndustrialAgricultural Units (UAIM) for the purpose of carrying out and coordinating activities such as credit assistance, resource use, commercialization or other activities notprohibited by law, that they develop in their agrarian nucleus[12].

Political participation and decision-making

16. According to data from January 2021, women occupy 50% of national parliamentary seats, but only 26% of the total number of state secretaries. In the case of state cabinets their percentage is even lower: in Mexico City, women hold more than 50% of the project area in the states of Moncloa and Puebla (54% and 53% respectively) while in Guerrero and Jalisco percentages are considerably low (5% and 16%)[13].

At the local level, the country reports 45% of women in the deliberative bodies, which rights to be elected by the population.

In the case of the Agrarian Communities, in 2016 the Agrarian Law was reformed by incorporating an additional paragraph in article 37, in the section corresponding to the ejido bodies, which refers to "Candidates for elective posts that make up the ejido commission and the auxiliary secretaries of the ejido commissioner, women will be elected in a majority of the same sex, and may aspire to any of the posts indistinctly. For the commissions and auxiliary secretaries of the ejido commissioner, the integration of women will be sought"[14].

This means that the minimum number of women representatives will be 40%. However, out of 14,531 ejidos and communities with representative bodies, only 22% have women representatives, or just 4.4% of all women in a community are represented by a woman representative. In the National Agrarian Registry 2019. In this project area, it is the state of Puebla that reports the highest number of women in ejidal positions (7.4%) while Guerrero is where they are least represented (2.8%).

### Despite the existence of legal spaces for greater participation of women in decision-making bodies, there are socio-cultural barriers that affect the recognition and support for women’s leadership capacities. Women often feel that their opinions are not valued by men and women themselves sometimes question their ability to do so. The unequal distribution of work within families also affects women’s capacity to participate fully in public life or their availability of time to take on an additional job in addition to those they already hold. On the other hand, in the case of ejidos, there is the lack of land ownership.

### Considering the existing legal protection for greater participation of women in decision-making bodies and the existence of laws that protect women’s rights and ejidos; as well as the existence of successful experiences of women’s participation, it is possible to promote greater participation of women in these spaces. This can be done by promoting education on gender patterns and stereotypes that cast doubt on women's leadership capacities and frighten men about the possibility of ceding power, the strengthening of women's leadership capacities through training and capacity building programs, and their representation through digital media.

### Education

#### In rural areas of Mexico, school non-attendance is higher (17.3%) than in urban areas (13.5%). Currently in Mexico, basic education covers 90% to 100%, mainly at primary education six years or more of age). Although the expansion of coverage has been an important educational achievement, there are several difficulties beyond increases in school services and infrastructure. These include the lack of teachers with adequate training belonging to rural and indigenous communities. In addition to the various shortages and difficulties mentioned above, there is also a significant lack of opportunities for employment opportunities, high levels of marginalization and poverty, lack of communication routes, among others), the complexity of the educational system established there must also be taken into consideration. This includes the difficulty in implementing new teaching methodologies, proposals, insufficient and irrelevant teaching materials, etc., Given that these communities are often very small, it is not always possible to have a teacher for each grade. It has been considered unnecessary to assign more than one teacher per school which is why there is a significant lack of teachers in rural areas where there is a lack of teaching staff. This has led to the establishment of multi-grade schools, that is, those where students study different grades in the same classroom.

#### In addition, there are obstacles for children and adolescents at school: for example domestic and family responsibilities, such as taking care of the family and child labour are barriers to gain access and permanence in school, especially indigenous girls. External factors such as remoteness


---
**Page 138**
---

from educational centres, especially at secondary level[18] should be mentioned. In the case of adults, illiteracy at the national level reaches 5.5% of the population, with women being more affected than men (6.3%). The states where the project area is located show a higher rate: 13.6% in Guerrero, followed by Oaxaca, Michoacán and Puebla (8.3% in both cases). In contrast, the lowest rates are found in Baja California Sur (2%), Jalisco (4%) and Morelos (5.5%, 5.6%, 6.1% respectively)[7]. The illiteracy among indigenous women and men aged 15-29 reached 4.8% and 3.5% respectively, decreasing in the youth population to 0.8% for both groups[7].

Division of labour between women and men

23. The National Household Income and Expenditure Survey ENIGH 2019 shows that while women dedicate 67% of their time outside working time to unpaid domestic work, men dedicate only 45% (of which 8% is market-related). On average, women work 8.2 hours more than men and, in the states where the project area is located, the greatest differences are found in Guerrero (-10%), Oaxaca (+3%) and Michoacán (+9%). The state with the smallest gap is Morelos (4.3%). As a consequence of this division of labour, women have less time to participate in productive activities or to manage their households. Women who live in indigenous families are even more affected: 50% do not have management committees or take an active part in assemblies or other time-consuming decision-making bodies due to lack of time. This situation particularly affects women heads of households and those with children under the age of six, as they are responsible for most family tasks, especially when the children are minors. 35% of all Mexican households are headed by women[19]. Women also have less access than men to a greater part of the income that households with a male head receive[20], aggravating their situation.

24. The functions of rural women include productive, communal and care work, representing a fundamental pillar of family survival. They fulfill their triple role generally in conditions of poverty and precariousness, which makes them more vulnerable to the chain to strengthen rescue the Mexican countryside. The gap between paid and unpaid work is also greater for women than men[21]. Women are not effectively incorporated in development in equal conditions, as active agents of development[22].

25. On the other hand, it is important to highlight the relationship of young women with work and study. Of the young people living in rural areas of Mexico (6 out of 10), 78.2% of young women are involved in productive activities or domestic tasks related to agriculture[23]. In contrast, only 45.9% of young men are engaged in these activities. The participation of young women in agricultural work has been decreasing. For women, the majority are engaged in household chores, which limits their access to education and training. As a result, in addition to dropping out of school, young women related to the home have experienced reduction of opportunities for job exchange and access to public spaces in their communities.

# Employment status

26 Women’s labour force participation in Mexico was only 45% in 2019, compared to 77% for men, a gap of 32 percentage points. This is below the OECD and Latin American averages[42]. Although there has been an increase in women's labour force participation in the economically active population in rural areas, it remains below the national average.

27 According to information from the 2019 National Occupation and Employment Survey, 35.6% of rural women aged 15-64 have a paid job, a lower percentage than urban women (50.2%). Among those who are economically active in rural areas, 73.5% have no hobbies, while this percentage is significantly lower in urban areas (49.6%).

28 Of employed rural women, 24.5% work in commerce, 23.4% in services, 20.1% in manufacturing industry and 13.3% in agriculture, livestock, forestry, hunting or fishing. Among women who are economically active but not working (67.9%), only 8.1% is exclusively engaged in paid work, while 96.9% are engaged in other unpaid activities[42].

29 The three main unpaid activities performed by women working in agricultural and fishing sectors include household chores, caring for people and fetching firewood and water for their households[42].

30 Women’s unemployment as a percentage of the female labor force in 2021 was 4.2% while men it was 4.5%. As far as the labor force employed in agricultural work, 56% of men and only 9% of women are unemployed.

31 Informal economy: Mexico has the highest level of informal employment among the largest economies in Latin America with 60% according to the OECD, which translates into a population of more than 24 million people who do not have access to social protection and no taxes. Women working in the informal economy are more vulnerable to living in poverty, yet their work contributes more than 5 billion pesos daily to the country’s economy, according to the National Council for the Evaluation of Social Development Programs (LACEN). According to the World Bank, one of the effects of the pandemic has been that many women have lost their jobs and are now working informally. The problem is that it is informal employment: "Informal employment, underemployment and unemployment are associated with lower levels of labor income and therefore a greater likelihood that the women and in particular those who work in agriculture will be poor."

## Common beliefs, perceptions and stereotypes related to gender

32 For centuries, stereotypes have been constructed in Mexican culture (as in other contexts)that characterize men as providers of the household, heads of the family and, to a certain extent, decision-makers. When production work is considered as man’s own responsibility, it is in a position to use more managerial skills and control over natural resources to whichthe exercise of power, both public and private, is strictly linked. In contrast, women's work in the home is for immediate consumption and is therefore invisible and not


---
**Page 140**
---

33. The concept that men are in charge and make the decisions makes women "asking for permission" to act, which is often denied, especially when it comes to authorizing women's participation in public life. This gendered division of labor within the family sphere affects their possibilities to participate in assemblies or other decision-making activities.

34. These stereotypes, which are also present in institutional officials, limit women’s access to resources and opportunities that are not traditionally assigned to them. Thus, for example, services related to care and health are oriented towards women while those related to production and the market are directed towards men.

35. To overcome these limitations, CONAFOR has implemented Integral Productive Projects (IPP) in 10 municipalities of Guerrero by assigning 20% of the resources assigned to the project to pay tasks associated to caring.


### Gender Violence

In 2022, 27 of Mexico's 32 states experienced increases in their rates of sexual violence and 23 experienced increases in their rates of family violence. According to the National Institute for Statistics and Geography (INEGI), the number of cases of sexual violence constitutes around two-thirds of the violence that women experience in public spaces. In 2021, Guerrero recorded a total of 968 cases of sexual violence, which reached a new record for the number of emergency calls reporting incidents of sexual violence, with 5,973 calls, an increase to 1.13% since 2021 and almost double the number reported in 2022: 496 cases.

Currently, approximately one in four murders of women in Mexico is classified as femicide. In Guerrero, this figure reaches 58%, which is the highest percentage in the country, due to the lack of institutional presence and the permissiveness of sexist stereotypes rooted in rural areas. This represents a more visible manifestation of gender inequality which violates their human rights and undermines their physical and mental health and their ability to be productive members of the community.


### Access to and control of resources and assets

37. Forestry and agricultural production activities play a fundamental role in the livelihood of three million people living in rural areas, especially ejidos and communities cover 85% (11.7 million ha) of the Basin area; however, ejidatarios and communal farmers generally own small plots (2 ha or less) with low productivity, mainly due to lack of access to means of production, technical assistance, credit, and markets. According to the Rural Agricultural Network (RAN), of the 4.9 million people who own agrarian nuclei throughout the country, more than 3.5 million are women. In the state of Guerrero, which has a total area of 80,271 km², the largest ejido area, Guerrero has the highest percentage of women owners (33%) and Jalisco has the lowest percentage (24.7%). Their productive activities are concentrated in backyard


---
**Page 141**
---

production from which they obtain food for self-consumption and collection and processing of non-timber forest products mainly from community areas.

38. Lack of land ownership is a constraint to accessing credit and other formal finance production. In general, women have limited access to credit and are more likely than men to use other types of informal financing such as loans from family, friends or acquaintances. According to data from the ENIGH 2019, 65% of women use this type of credit, which is generally for smaller amounts to solve their daily needs.

39. Producers in general have limited access to technical assistance for production and less access to those practices related to climate change adaptation and ecosystem restoration. Women are more likely than men to be involved in the forest sector because they have recognition and value placed on their roles as producers. In general, services are oriented towards men, even in those cases where women led production due to male migration or formalization of the activity.

40. Women's access to existing initiatives in the National Forestry Commission (CONAFOR) stands out, with women representing 27% of the beneficiaries of the Programme for Business Strengthening in Forest Landscapes (PROFEM) and 35.3% of the beneficiaries of the Support Programme for Sustainable Forest Development (PADS) in 2021.

Access to information and communication technologies

41. In the country, women spend an average of 15.1 hours per week on mass media while men spend only 9.8 hours. Women also spend more time than men with communities with less than 100,000 inhabitants where the average is 12.6 hours for women and 14.0 hours for men. In most of the states of the project area, the average time spent on mass media is less than the theoretical average of 15.1 hours per week. The state with the highest average is Oaxaca (23.8 hours). Oaxaca stands out as the state where the population spends the least amount of time with an average of 11.7 hours, which is below the national average and also below the theoretical average.

Gender inequalities and climate change

42. In general, the effects of climate change on the population are diverse, and serious issues that are already occurring may intensify due to climate change, as for example: water shortages, food insecurity, increased frequency of extreme weather events such as droughts, floods, diarreah, asthma and others; internal migration and population growth concentrated in a small number of urban centers that are already facing environmental stress.

43. The project area is highly vulnerable to the effects of climate change due to its specific biogeographic characteristics, which have been historically associated with increased social vulnerability existing inequalities, particularly gender inequalities. It is among the two main regions of the country with the highest climate vulnerability index. The impacts of climate change, including rising temperatures and heavy rains, aggravate the situation and increase the vulnerability of rural families.

12 INEGI, INMIJUERES. National Time Use Survey 2019: Presentation of results. Second edition.


---
**Page 142**
---

44. The reduction of productive resources and domestic supply, caused by climate change and/or destruction of ecosystems, exacerbate the conditions of poverty and the unsatisfactory living standards of rural communities. To cope with these changes, adopt adaptive strategies where migration, redistribution of responsibilities and tasks on the farm and in then household, are common. This is especially true for a higher number of female heads of household and an increase in the time and burden of their working hours.

45. There is sufficient evidence that the collection and use of water and fuel for washing, cleaning and cooking increases with the adverse effects of climate change. In rural areas, the migration of men to urban areas has increased the burden on women who are left behind to manage household tasks. This means that it is sought further away from the household. In localities of less than 2,500 inhabitants, only about half of the households have piped water and electricity. In localities with more than 10,000 inhabitants, this figure rises to 87%. The effects of climate change mean that people have to travel longer distances to fetch firewood and water, requiring not only a greater investment of time, but also an increase in the number of trips made by women. This has serious consequences for people's health, causing illnesses that in turn increase women's household tasks and responsibilities.

46. In terms of their productive role, women's reduced access to institutional resources to address the effects of climate change on their farms leads to a progressive deterioration or abandonment of the activity, with consequent effects on their well-being and on the well-being of their families.

47. In many rural and indigenous communities, when men migrate to the United States, women are the ones who work the land, but they are not recognized as having rights to it even if they have worked there for years. If a woman does not own land, women cannot receive credit or forequipment or infrastructure programs on their own. Women also do not receive support for payment of environmental services.

48. In the Basas Basin, the usual inequity faced by women in accessing different services and goods is exacerbated by the adverse effects of climate change. On the one hand, activities traditionally assigned to subsistence production in the backyard, household and family care become more difficult due to changes in weather patterns, such as droughts, floods or extreme weather phenomena, such as hurricanes and torrential rains. Droughts, for example, reduce water availability for livestock and crops, which affects farm animals and households goods, situations that have to be tackled mainly by women in charge of the household. Gender gaps expressed in women's limited access to resources restrict rights and lack of voice in decision-making are the conditions that make them most vulnerable to climate change.

**Main findings for gender equality in the project**

49. This assessment shows gender gaps that limit women's participation in the project. It highlights how women are more affected than men by poverty in rural areas and that existing

gender stereotypes result in long working hours and make their participation in productive and environmental activities invisible. It also results in the increase in gender violence against women at all levels of society. Women have less access to land than men; hold fewer decision-making positions in the ejidos and communities, only a few women engaged in agricultural work are paid for their labor, and they earn less money than men. It is also evident that women are more vulnerable to climate change due to the roles they play, for example: there are more women headed households due to migration, more effort on water collection, and more responsibility of children care. 50. On the other hand, it identifies opportunities from which the project can benefit. These include the existence of national laws and policies aimed at the full entitlement of women's human rights, gender equality and a life free of violence. In the agrarian sector, laws have been enacted to ensure that women are included in decision-making positions, including positions in ejido commissions and oversight councils, and to develop productive activities through cooperatives.

51. Although women hold barely a quarter of all land ownership certificates, there are mechanisms such as the Women's Industrial Agricultural Units (UAIM), CONAFOR'S Common Interest Participatory Groups that provide opportunities for women to access land collectively or individually; they also have the opportunity to participate in ejido commissions and allow women to participate and benefit even if they are not direct ejidatarias.

52. As part of the programmatic framework of the forestry sector, the National Forestry Program (PNF) has developed a strategy for the inclusion of indigenous peoples, youth and the gender perspective in the instruments and actions aimed at the forestry sector to favour their effective participation. This strategy is being implemented through the creation of women's groups that are responsible for the participation of rural women in this sector; among them: progress has been made in the incorporation of women into the management of forest plantations, the implementation of phytosanitary treatments, rural fire management brigades, general criteria for forest sanitation and the development of productive projects. The creation of comprehensive productive projects for women's groups that include activities for the generation of added value to forest products as well as include resources to provide support care tasks.

53. There are also experiences of women's participation as beneficiaries of CONAFOR'S Payment for Environmental Services (PES) program, which is implemented in ejidos. Land tenure is not necessarily a limitation for their participation as PES beneficiaries. Male chewers and other family members who have the right to use the land for ejido forestry projects and the interest of some ejido leaders (comisariados) in benefiting only a sector made up mostly of family members and acquaintances, are factors that limit women's participation in these programs. However, the creation of women's groups as beneficiaries of collective lands in agrarian communities and ejidos is an opportunity for women to actively participate in forest management activities.

54. The Ejido of San Vicente de Benitez in the state of Guerrero benefited from PES for 5 years starting in 2012, and despite having only 160 epilapidarios, 992 people (men and women) were


---
**Page 144**
---

defined as beneficiaries, which was the total population of the five localities that make up the ejido. As a result, the participation of women was 85% and the number of daily wages contributed by women was 70% or more in all the communities. The percentage of men is in total, of which 51% were from women and 49% from men. In two other ejidos that were part of the project, the participation of women was 82%, with a daily wage contribution of 63%. This mainly due to the aforementioned factors related to machismo, limited access to information and the interests of the authorities to benefit specific groups in the communities and ejidos.

54. The openness towards women's participation in the Ejido San Vicente de Benitez is not the result of a conscious decision by the community or the ejidatarios. However, it shows that even if women do not have their own land, they can be beneficiaries of a PES programme if the necessary social conditions are created.

### Part II: Gender Action Plan (GAP)

55. The BALSAS project is fully committed to social inclusion, women's empowerment and the strengthening of the productive and reproductive relationship between men and women. Through its proposals for action, it is aligned to a mainstreamed transformative gender approach so that it is embedded throughout the project cycle and in all its components. It seeks to ensure that men and women have access to all the services offered and in particular to the resources and benefits aimed at increasing resilience to climate change and mitigating vulnerability. The Gender Action Plan (GAP) is an integral part of this transformative approach, and if this GAP is not an add-on to the project but rather an integral part of its structure as well a responsibility of all actors in charge of its design, implementation, monitoring and evaluation.

56. Its preparation required a review of current legislation, policies, programmes, commitments made and aid at the international level related to gender by the Government of Mexico, as well as institutional policies and strategies, especially those of CONAFOR as project implementer. The GAP was also aligned with the objectives of the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) and the Green Climate Fund (GCF) expressed in their respective Strategic Frameworks. It is structured into three sections: i) a review of the progress in the design of the project "BALSAS: Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategies", especially its Logical Framework and ii) the Gender Action Plan, which includes an analysis of the gender gap in Mexico, an overview of the gender gaps and issues women face in Mexico, based on a literature review of national and international studies. iii) The third section is dedicated to the identification of quantitative and qualitative information on gender gaps and the situation of women, especially those who are ejidatarios, as well as the design of the Gender Action Plan project, an aspect that will have to be completed with the results of the respective consultations.

57. The Action Plan contains three sections: the first one refers to the identification of mechanisms and activities considered necessary to eliminate or reduce the gender gaps identified in Mexico; the second section is dedicated to the design of specific activities addressed by the project for the achievement of the expected impact and results in terms of gender equality, with a focus on women's empowerment. It also includes considerations regarding the quantitative and qualitative improvement of women's participation and gender equality; the third part presents a matrix according to the requirements of the Green Climate Fund (GCF) that identifies the activities, their respective outputs, indicators and targets, the timeline of their implementation and responsibilities.

#### 2.1. Mechanisms and activities identified to address gender gaps in the


---
**Page 145**
---

# BALSAS Project

## Rural women are affected by an overload of work in the household and by stereotypical beliefs that see their lives as reduced to their supposed reproductive responsibilities. They have limited access to land, productive resources, and economic resources. Additionally, their low levels of education and literacy, their lesser access to land, their limited access to paid work and information are obstacles to the recognition of their rights.

### 2.1.1 Define quotas for women's participation as part of the target population of the project and the implementing teams

#### General quotas (minimum percentages for women participation) have been an incentive to encourage the inclusion of women and to achieve equal opportunities for men and women. In Mexico, the allocation of quotas in various fields is already a practice.

Activities:

- It is defined that the participation of women as direct beneficiaries of the Balsas project will be 40%. This figure is included within the different activities and actions promoted by the project concerning the design and potential coverage in CONAFOR and the possibilities offered by the BALSAS project. The specific rights by result and activity are detailed below.

- An increase in women's participation at all decision-making bodies of the project and its beneficiaries will be promoted. In this case, the project considers the current figures of women's participation in the representative bodies in ejidos and communities, as well as the existence of a gender equality policy according to article 37 of the Agrarian Law regarding the existence of no more than sixty percent of representatives from each sex.

- The limitations that women face in terms of land tenure will also be considered in those activities that require their availability for agroforestry and silvopastoral projects, seeking the participation of those who do have land titles or who can have access collectively through organizations.


---
**Page 146**
---

The project will use targeting criteria and methods that are inclusive of the different existing population groups (direct targeters and self-targeters) to ensure the identification of the poorest households, particularly women, head of household, and those in conditions of poverty and vulnerability. The proposal is based on carrying out a participatory process with the communities through the Support Programme for Sustainable Forestry Development 2022 (PADSF) and adding targeting criteria to support the inclusion of groups in conditions of vulnerability.

CONAFOR will seek to expand the participation of women in the technical teams, mainly in the priorities promoted by the project.


### Facilitate women availability of time to participate in project activities

60. Both men and women are expected to actively participate in the project activities as both are affected by climate change in the performance of their productive initiatives for food and income generation. In addition, given that women have a greater responsibility for their domestic role, it will be sought that they can fully participate in the processes and activities of the project. This is achieved through this meaning a greater overload of tasks. With this objective in mind, the following is proposed:

- **Assure socialization of the domestic and care tasks within the family**, as well as the community organization by raising awareness of the inequality in the distribution of domestic work between men and women. In addition to having time for project activities and thus benefiting from project-related services. To this end, the project's capacity-building spaces will be used, and dissemination materials will be developed.

- **The consideration of places and times suitable to the availability of women** for the development of project activities, so that distances, safety, mobility and the times are adapted to their needs. This way women can fully participate and their multiple tasks are not an obstacle to their benefiting from their project services.

- **Identify and create partnerships with existing initiatives in institutions**, communities or other organizations that seek to reduce the impact of multiple domestic burdens (childcare, fetching firewood and water and other tasks) to target or intensify their services for the households in the project area.

- **Strengthen CONAFOR's initiative on integral productive projects for women** that finance affirmative actions related to care work.


### Ensuring access to information on participation opportunities and benefits offered by the project

61. Due to existing constraints, the project will analyze the means used by people to get information. On one hand, it will strengthen the options of digital connectivity, and on the other hand, it will combine digital information with other mechanisms that are commonly used in the region.

62. A common problem is that the contents and means of dissemination of the products


---
**Page 147**
---

and services offered do not reach women, limiting their demand. In view of this, the BALSAS project will take into consideration the media and forms of communication utilised by women in order to increase the coverage of its services and products.

63. On the other hand, the digital divide, which mainly affects women, will be considered in the framework of climate information systems and options will be sought to bring women closer to digital technology and the use of complementary means of dissemination so that they can access climate information systems.

- Study on women's use of media (by age, ethnicity and place of residence) including information on the availability and use of internet and communication devices such as mobile phones, computers, etc.). This study could be included as part of the project baseline.
- Define a communication strategy with messages that inform and promote women's interest in the services offered by the project, taking into account their access to media and complementary means of dissemination.
- Create digital education opportunities for women, primarily leveraging smartphones to enable them to access climate information systems.

2.1.4. Valuing and drawing on women’s and men’s specific knowledge and experience to contribute to project outcomes and solutions.

64. The project focuses its activities and results on groups in conditions of greater vulnerability, such as indigenous communities affected by desertification due to climate change, but also recognises these groups for their constant struggle to face the difficulties it causes and for being protagonists of adaptation strategies that are often not recognised or taken up again as part of the solutions for mitigation and adaptation to climate change that the project proposes. Both men and women are in a position to make significant contributions to the success of the project, especially when they have access to information and knowledge of technicians and professionals. In this respect, the project will carry out the following activities:

- Systematisation of experiences on climate-resilient and adapted agronomic techniques and technologies designed and implemented by women (mainly indigenous women), to be incorporated into project proposals and used in the framework of the project.
- Consider this knowledge and experience as part of adaptation, mitigation and restoration strategies and in the development of training materials for use by the teams and the beneficiary population.
- Create participatory spaces for the exchange of knowledge and experiences where women play a leading role.

2.1.5. Create a didactic offer aimed at strengthening women’s capacities for empowerment and the development of successful climate-resilient productive initiatives

65. The use of the services and benefits provided by the project through its components


---
**Page 148**
---

depends to a large extent on the capacities of women to face the challenges involved in leading and managing agricultural initiatives, managing the production and marketing of products, decision-making and the appropriate use of information, among other issues. For this reason, the following activities are considered:

- Develop and execute training based on the didactic principles of the active methodology called APA (Aprende, Practica y Aplica), which promotes the integration of theory with practice. This method allows the trainee to be more autonomously involved in the training process. It favors the development of critical thinking skills, encourages the participation of individuals or collectively, develops communicative abilities, develops skills for social interaction and gives contextual value to their learning. The following topics would be addressed:

  * Gender focus
  * Leadership and management of organizations
  * Climate-smart production management and marketing
  * Climate-smart value chains: concept, functioning, participation, benefits and challenges
  * Application of digital technology (use of smartphones)

Some of these topics are already included in RO_22's catalogue of capacity building topics while others will need to be incorporated.

### Gender training for technical teams, project contractors and communities

Gender stereotypes are generalised opinions or prejudices that are harmful when they - limit the capacity of men and women to develop their personal abilities., - impede the social recognition of their roles in society. They also lead to a demand for access to goods and services that are not in accordance with their socially established image. The project will address gender stereotypes through the following activities aimed at technical teams, contractors and communities:

  * Through workshops, the gender capacities of the project's team professionals, technicians and contractors will be strengthened, according to their roles.

It will address

i) gender stereotypes and their effect on the lives of men and women,
ii) Analysis on gender violence prevention and care protocols
iii) the relevance of a gender equality approach to climate change mitigation and adaptation,
iv) existing international commitments, regional and national policies and strategies which can be used as a basis for the implementation of restoration actions with this approach,
v) experiences and lessons learned in climate change adaptation and mitigation and ecosystem restoration with women's participation.
vi) analysis of the commitments to the theme contained in the project design.

- How to address gender equality in the framework of the assigned activities.
- Training will also be extended to communities, producers and producers through

**Page 149**

2.1.7 Prevention and promotion of comprehensive care against violence

- The increase in gender violence in the country is a concern of the federal government and of the states and municipalities in the project area, as well as of IFAD and GCF in all their actions. In response to this problem, the project will act:
  - Coordination with the offices (secretaries, institutes, etc.) dedicated to the care of women and gender violence in each municipality to identify and/or support the development of protocols and the route of actions for reporting, and care for victims of gender violence.
  - As part of the educational material produced for the training mentioned in 2.1.6, participation will be included in the production of protocols for reporting and caring for victims of gender violence. These materials will also be distributed during the various events held by the project to achieve this objective.

- CONAFOR through its Citizen Attention Mechanism (MAC), will act on internal complaints with the support of its Internal Control Body. This mechanism will act as the project's grievance mechanism.

2.1.8 Gender specialist staff for the implementation of the GAP and gender mainstreaming in the project

60. Implementing strategies and actions that ensure gender mainstreaming in the project requires the participation of professionals specialized in gender. The support and advice of CONAFOR’s specialised gender team is essential, as well as the strengthening of alliances with other institutions such as the National Institute for Women (INMUJERES), the Ministry of Environment and Natural Resources (SEMARNAT), institutions that provide services related to health, education and care, as well as private sector companies and Non-Governmental Organizations (NGOs) that contribute to gender equality in the project area.

61. Considering also the dimensions of the project in terms of geographical coverage and beneficiaries, it is essential for the project team to have a gender specialist who provides continuous advice to the technical teams, implements concrete actions required for mainstreaming and provides support regarding the full implementation of this GAP.

2.2 Women’s participation in climate resilience, productive ecosystem enhancement and rural development activities

70. Within the framework of its expected results and specific outputs, activities are identified; indicators are defined and compliance targets are established for monitoring and evaluating their achievement. The project will also establish mechanisms to measure both quantitative and qualitative improvement of women’s participation in the activities proposed by the project for the achievement of the expected results and outputs. 2.3. Activities by result

Outcome 1. Government programs and community development plans integrate climate change criteria for attribution of supports

71. Output 1.1. Mechanisms for intersectoral coordination are strengthened to increase the level of participation of women in decision-making processes, both at the national level with the different levels of government and the private initiative. Within the framework of this output, women will be invited to participate in the elaboration of project profiles and in participatory multi-year committees. Their contribution will be relevant as bearers of knowledge and experiences that will feed back into the discussions and decisions regarding the achievement of agreements on climate change.

- Convene and strengthen the participation of women in decision-making in the RCMT for the prioritization of intervention areas, as well as in the development of participatory multi-year committees. It is expected that 40% of the members of these committees will be women.
- Considering the efforts carried out by the sector's institutions in relation to gender equality and the adjustments they have been making to adapt laws, policies, strategies and regulations for greater equity in participation between men and women, the project will support the development of a participatory multi-year committee (PMYC) with climate change criteria for adaptation to climate change and positive actions for women. An analysis of the progress achieved to date and the identification of gaps in operational procedures that can be addressed by the PMYC will also be carried out.

72. Output 1.2: Participatory multisectorial investment plans within prioritized basin, sub-basin and ethnic areas integrating mitigation instruments and adaptation to climate change.
- Women will be invited to participate in decision-making in relation to multiannual investment plans. Consultation spaces will be created so that their needs and opportunities of projects are identified as well as to collect necessary information to guarantee active participation of the women.
- The implementation of communication and awareness-raising campaigns to disseminate calls for projects will contain inclusive and gender-sensitive language and will take into account media and content (informative, motivational and empathetic messages) in accordance with the needs and opportunities of the women.
- Considering the educational levels and the limited previous experience of women in the elaboration of project profiles, a simplified format will be designed to be easy to fill in and support will be provided by CONAFOR teams when necessary.

With these measures, it is expected that at least 30% of people directly benefiting from the investment plans will be women.


---
**Page 151**
---

73. Output 1.3: Strengthened capacities of local actors for climate change adaptation, resilience and inclusiveness

- The project will strengthen COMAFOR's current capacity building programme by incorporating supplementary themes to the existing ones. These include gender equality and the inclusion of women as well as life skills. In order to address these issues, experts will be involved in designing the training curriculum and the necessary support materials taking into account the specific needs of women beneficiaries. The same applies for activities identified to address gender gaps in the BALSAS project.

This training will be aimed at extension workers, local facilitators and brigadiers, among whom women, especially young women, are expected to participate. The presence of women these actors will help to ensure a greater level of commitment from them; it is a strategic measure to facilitate project approaching to the female target population.

- The project will also strengthen COMAFOR's local promoters through training, equipment and materials for new promoters. Among the people recruited to carry out this activity are women who have been trained in the BALSAS project. As in the case of facilitators and brigadiers, the attention of female beneficiaries will be secondary by the strengthening of capacities on gender related issues for all promoters.

Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change for the provision of environmental services and increase livelihood resources

74. Output 2.1 Agrarian communities conserve, restore and sustainably use forest landscape based/multinational investment plans through payment of environmental services (PES)

- Considering the experiences of women's participation as beneficiaries of PES, even without headlines, the project will promote changes in the limiting factors identified in 2017 (i.e. machismo in the community, women's lack of access to information, selection of beneficiaries and decision-making power). The project will also ensure that women are included in the projects within communities and ejidos. The project will inform communal and ejido authorities about the importance of women as key actors for the success of PES and will include them in the process of selecting beneficiaries. The project will also work with women to develop messages that appeal to both men and women, and project selection criteria that favor women's participation will be used.

75. Output 2.2 The productive systems of Agrarian Communities are strengthened and better adapted to climate change

- In the framework of this product, women will be trained in production practices and technologies adapted to climate change and variability. This training will provide specific solutions for the problems that women face when trying to carry out their potential role in the production systems in which they participate. The implementation of training activities will be defined according to women's schedules and, as far as possible, the selection criteria will consider short journeys, taking into account the difficulties of commuting that they have.

- The agrarian communities will identify and incorporate women as beneficiaries of


---
**Page 152**
---

financial transfers made by the project for the provision of technical assistance, of inputs and computation of labor costs for their participation in landscape restoration activities, foresters' training, establishment of a sustainable forest management adapted to climate change. It will be necessary to consider the actual and potential benefits for women in the promoted activities, rather than landowner status. Adequate dissemination of these opportunities will be key to motivate women and bring them closer to these initiatives.

### Output 2.3: Participation of Agroforestry Communities and Producer Organisations in value chains of forest and agroforestry products is strengthened and their livelihood resilience improved

- As part of the activity of Sustainable Forest Management adapted to climate change, inclusion and access to markets, value chains will be strengthened in a climate-sensitive manner. This will be done through the participation of women's groups, producer organizations or with broad participation of women in the different segments of the chains, especially those that are linked to sustainable use and value addition to timber and non-timber forest products.

- In order to strengthen their capacities, gender training will be provided and women will learn about sustainable and climate-resilient production practices, organizational, marketing and business skills. More details on the proposed methodology and contents are presented in section 2.5, focusing on mechanisms and activities identified to address gender gaps in the BALAS project.

- The range of productive projects include affirmative actions with a gender perspective will be expanded, taking into account the successful experiences developed by CONAFOR.

### Considering the backyard as a key space for food production and strategies for the subsistence of families, support will be provided for crop diversification and improved irrigation systems. Producers will receive training in the use of these technologies and will receive technical assistance, necessary equipment and economic compensation further work. The improvement in the quantity, quality and variety of food will allow for better conservation of natural resources and greater resilience to climate change at both community and local level.

### Outcome 3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Balas Basin have access to relevant climate information for climate risk management, disaster prevention and knowledge management.

#### Output 3.1: Climate-hazards-related early warning and monitoring systems supported

- As part of the development of a platform and applications to facilitate access to climate information and early warning system, a participatory diagnosis will be needed to identify possible constraints in the dissemination that women may face so that measures can be taken to overcome these constraints.

- Considering the potential availability of smart phones, women will be trained in the use of these devices and in the use of the integrated climate information system. In addition, in response to the diagnosis developed, alternative ways of communicating information 24


---
**Page 153**
---

through other media such as radio and written information will be developed if necessary.

- Regarding CONAFOR’s plot monitoring and MRY system, capacities will be built to collect, analyse and report on quantitative and qualitative indicators related to gender and poverty reduction, social and environmental safeguards. To this end, mechanisms will be defined for the active participation of local communities in the collection and basic processing of information.

78. Output 3.2. Policy-relevant knowledge products elaborated from the systematic analysis of women’s practices and participatory approaches.

- Systematisation of women's experiences and good practices on the use of climate resilient agronomic technologies and techniques that are relevant for knowledge sharing, adoption of new policies and institutional strategies and replication for scaling up will be undertaken.

In order to disseminate the progress achieved and promote the exchange of knowledge and learning among beneficiaries, workshops will be held with women playing a leading role in the project. These workshops will also be attended by field technicians, who will be able to reinforce and reorient their work according to the findings. Field technicians, who will be able to reinforce and reorient their work according to the findings, will be involved in the dissemination of knowledge among beneficiaries through the spaces.

2.5. Responses of the Gender Action Plan to the gender gaps identified in the Gender Assessment

79. Considering the results of the Gender Assessment, this GAP and the project in general seek to provide concrete responses to the identified gaps that can be addressed within the framework of the "BALSAS: Basin Approach for Livelihood Sustainability through Adaptation Strategy" (see Table 2.1). The main findings are summarized in the last section of the Gender Assessment called Main findings for gender equality in the Project.

| Gender gaps/barriers identified | Recommended actions |
| --- | --- |
| Little participation in decision-making spaces | *Participation quotas as an incentive for the participation of women in decision-making bodies*<br>*40% Participation in multisectional decision-making bodies throughout the project*<br>*The selection of project proposals led by women is privileged* |
| Long work hours due to domestic overload | *Construction of water harvesting projects such as collector roofs.*<br>*Execution of comprehensive projects for women with attention to their domestic role and use of time.*<br>*Partnerships with actors in the territory for care and domestic activities.* |
| Increased poverty and low income | *Priority for women heads of household in projects on productive activities.*<br>*Access to economic compensation for their participation in PES, RFM, CP, Value Chain, and food security and nutrition projects.*<br>*Improvements in income due to improvements in production.* |
| Gender prejudices and stereotypes | *Training activities in gender equality for beneficiaries of the project.*<br>*Preparation and dissemination of materials on the topic.* |
| Gender violence | *Coordination with the offices (secretaries, etc.), bodies, and institutions that women in state and local (municipal) governments.*<br>*Identify mechanisms and procedures with the mechanisms and route of actions for reporting and care for victims of gender violence.*<br>*Production of a manual of the Actuación del Internal Control Body del MAC de COMAPA, as well as the corresponding questions y reclamos.* |
| Invisibility of the participation in productive and environmental activities | *Training to project staff and community members on gender equality and the effects on discrimination against women.*<br>*Participation quotas for women in the different activities averaging 40% throughout the project.* |
| Less access and control of productive resources and assets | *Access to credit, land, and other production inputs in PES, RFM, CP, Value Chain, and food security and nutrition activities.* |

# Gender Action Plan Implementation, Monitoring and Evaluation

## Implementation

### 80. The Balsas project has a Steering Committee at the federal level, which is the highest level in decision-making for the Project. Gender issues that will be on the Committee's agenda through the following mechanisms:

- Annual work plans and budgets that are presented to the Committee for approval will include gender equality as a component of the plan, as well as the budget line item for gender activities in the project.
- With the support of the Project’s monitoring and evaluation system, information will be provided to the Committee to monitor the results in relation to gender and the improvement measures that must be implemented.

### 81. In the context of the Regional Comprehensive Management of Forest Territories (RCMT), the Project will involve women in decision-making for prioritizing areas of intervention, as well as in the development of participatory multiparty alliances. Annual investment plans will, based on the call aimed at the RCMT, include a gender component and will be approved by 50% or more of the members of these committees will be women as mentioned in the GAP (Product 1.1).

### 82. For the implementation of this action plan, the project will have human resources specialized in gender issues to ensure that the technical teams, forest extension agents and the beneficiary population itself can direct efforts towards a greater participation of women, the young population and the indigenous population.

### 83. The strengthening of capacities in gender and inclusion will help the technical teams, forestry extension agents and the beneficiary population itself to direct efforts towards a greater participation of women, the young population and the indigenous population.

### 84. The support and advice of CONAFOR’s specialized gender team and the strengthening of alliances with key actors in the territories are essential.

### 85. It is necessary to assign adequately prepared personnel to advise the teams, gender training for all staff, a clear definition of monitoring and evaluation mechanisms for gender issues and women's participation.


---
**Page 161**
---

Monitoring and Evaluation

86\. As part of the project’s monitoring and evaluation system, activity-based indicators are considered for each output and component.

87\. The possibility of incorporating the results indicator on empowerment will be analyzed, measured by FADIC’s Women’s Empowerment Index that addresses 10 dimensions: economic autonomy, self-efficacy, attitudes towards domestic violence, participation in production decisions, control over resources, decision-making power within the family, access to education and training, balance of income, balance in the use of time, belonging to organized groups and belonging to influential groups. The result indicator is Beneficiary women showing an improvement in empowerment (Percentage of women who improved). In this context, the dimensions that make up the index can be analyzed in terms of the following dimensions.

88\. The collection of the information required by the index is carried out through a questionnaire that will be applied as part of the baseline at the beginning of the project and at the end during its evaluation.

2.7\. Budget

89\. Of the project’s general budget, it is estimated that around USD 30,874,935 will be allocated to the necessary arrangements to guarantee the provision of quality services with a gender perspective in the areas of education, health and social protection of project benefits to women. This amount is distributed according to expected results as follows:

| Outcome 1: Government programs and community development plans | BUDGET USD |
| --- | --- |
| Integrate climate change criteria for attribution of support | 3.488.930 |
| Outcome 2: Forest landscapes are sustainably managed and better adapted to climate change for the provision of environmental services and increase livelihoods | 26.380.669 |
| Outcome 3: The producers, inhabitants and authorities of the Baías PA have adequate knowledge about sustainable climate risk management, disaster prevention and knowledge management | 1.005.336 |
| TOTAL | 30.874.935 |
